## EM Economic & Strategy Outlook Waiting isn't easy

Amid steady global growth but sticky inflation, markets await more clarity on the path of US rates. In the meantime, idiosyncratic developments are fuelling volatility in EM assets. We expect limited beta conviction and continued issuer diferentiation to prevail in EM credit, while EM rates should remain rangebound.

Local: The Fed remains a constraint for EM local markets, where margins of safety are still running thin and the 'easy' part of the disinflation process is behind us. Fundamentally, high rates are not causing much pain. EMs (overall)  are still pricing some degree of normalization in the policy stance, but terminal rates are likely to stay priced above neutral amid a shallow cycle expected in the US. We thus expect rates to remain rangebound. Limited room for error leaves us tactically looking for policy diferentiation, but the window to capture the move is short: rates trades require getting ahead of the move in spite of negative carry costs, while weaker currencies are trades that can be chased. LatAm's moment is mostly behind us, and we favor divergence in CEEMEA and parts of Asia. Politics has become a more relevant driver for diferentiation, both in FX and rates.

Credit:<sup>1</sup> We expect diferentiation among EM HY sovereigns to remain elevated. Our high conviction OWs include Ghana and Sri Lanka based on our expectation that the respective restructuring processes can be successfully concluded soon, and we see further upside in the post-restructuring bonds of Zambia and Suriname (both OW). In the performing EM HY sector, we rate Nigeria, Egypt and Ecuador OW, upgrade Kenya to OW (from MW) and recently also upgraded the Dominican Republic to OW. Conversely, we remain UW on South Africa and Argentina. Among IG sovereigns, valuations look attractive in the aggregate, but the sector faces substantial headwinds from rates volatility and some idiosyncratic developments. We rate Mexico UW, downgrade KSA to UW (from MW) and recommend buying KSA 5y CDS. The CEE region could be a potential target for global USD IG investors given attractive valuations, but contagion from the political volatility in the euro area may be a hurdle in the near term. We expect EEMEA/LatAm corporates to further outperform sovereigns, but recommend being increasingly selective in HY. In Asia corporates, we recommend focusing on lower-rated rated segments, especially in HY India corporates (OW).

## Economic outlook: Steady, sticky, slow… with uncertainty looming

## Steady growth…

Global growth remains steady, and since our last EMQ in March, our 2024 growth forecasts now aggregate to 3.1% global growth, up from our projection  of 2.9% in March, implying an almost unchanged pace from 2023 (3.2%). Even if data suggest some cooling in the US economy, its overall growth dynamic remains supported by continuing robust consumption on the back of a still-strong labour market and positive wealth efects. Our US growth projection for 2024 is almost unchanged at 2.5% (versus 2.6% in March). The small upward revision to 2024 global growth is explained by a better cyclical recovery in the euro area (to annual growth of 0.7%, up from 0.4% in March), following two years of stagnation, and somewhat stronger growth in emerging markets, driven mainly by upward revisions to China (to 5.0% from 4.4%) and India (to 7.2% from 6.8%).

That said, our upgrade for China annual growth is simply the mechanical consequence of a surprisingly high oficial Q1 GDP, while all indications are for a sharp sequential slowdown in coming quarters, as domestic consumption continues to be dragged down by an ailing property sector and its negative debt-deflation dynamics. Thus far, no 'Big Bang' stimulus has been forthcoming and the authorities do not seem intent on providing one. However, even with a slowing China, we see the rest EM holding up in coming quarters, as the global manufacturing cycle recovers and other EM economies, especially in Asia, pick up pace.

Overall, our baseline projections see global growth expanding at a pace of 3.0% in 2025, hence, only marginally lower than in 2023 and also the projected rate for 2024. This certainly would not be a boom, but a decent, steady expansion, which in principle provides a favourable backdrop for investment.

FIGURE 1. Global growth broadens as global manufacturing slowly recovers

## … but sticky inflation makes for slow monetary easing…

On the flip-side of steady growth, however, inflation has proven to be somewhat sticky. After the 'easy' gains from the reversal of earlier pandemic and energy supply shocks were largely made in 2023, further progress would have to come from decelerating services inflation. However,

tight labour markets and robust wage growth, especially in the US and Europe, keep services inflation elevated. We now forecast core PCE inflation, the most relevant measure for the Fed, to be 2.8% at end-2024, not far from where it was at end-2023.

Certainly, given where inflation was at its recent peak, projected inflation rates between 2% and 3% look fairly benign, and there is little indication of an upward trajectory. However, with core central banks committed to going ‘the last mile’ to return to their 2% targets, they have become much more hesitant about embarking on easing cycles. For the Fed, we now expect only one 25bp cut in 2024, compared with the three that we saw in March. Similarly, we have reduced the number of cuts by the ECB in 2024 to three from five. As a consequence, EM central banks, many already much further along the easing cycle than the advanced economies, have also turned more cautious in light of higher-for longer core rates and thus tighter USD liquidity conditions.

FIGURE 3. Sticky services inflation has slowed disinflation in core economies...
![](images/422d78d90d88de3feb2616c222fc0a5db4f2d3d396f0971763f3adeae13bde1e.jpg)
Our aggregation of US CPI and HICP for the euro area, the UK, Switzerland, and Scandinavian countries. Includes OOH for US.

FIGURE 4. ...which keeps the Fed higher for longer and makes EM central banks more cautious in advancing with their  easing cycles.
![](images/43bd2b725bac09dc36fd7cfcb4b473b50e961d7b61ecd26b2d67d7efebde8d68.jpg)

## … while political uncertainty looms large

One of the most prominent developments since the March EMQ has been the arrival of ‘voter dependence’ as a dominant theme, in addition to ‘data dependence’. Outlooks for 2024 had already highlighted this being one of the biggest global election years in history, and Argentina's election (at end 2023) provided a taste of how surprising outcomes could be. A number of surprise election outcomes in recent weeks have further emphasised  how much political change can matter for markets. In India, disappointment over an unexpectedly small win for incumbent President Modi caused a sellof in domestic asset markets (although later partially reversed). In South Africa, the initial sharp sell-of following the ANC’s loss of a majority turned into a rally after the announcement of a Government of National Unity that is efectively a dressed-up grand coalition with the market-friendly DA party. In Mexico, asset prices remain under stress after a surprise two-thirds majority win for the incumbent government party has raised fears about the repercussions of potential constitutional changes and the quality of institutions.

Such ‘voter dependence’ is not restricted to emerging markets, as the recent European election results have shown. Large gains for the far right, especially in France, not only raised questions about the future cohesion of the EU, but also ended up pushing France into deep political

uncertainty – and French asset markets into turmoil – as President Macron called for early parliamentary elections, which will be held on 30 June and 7 July.

That said, the biggest uncertainty still lies ahead: the US election in November. According to some of the ideas floated by former President Trump, the issues at stake seem to be not only a potential further deterioration of the US fiscal position with consequences for US Treasury yields, but also simultaneous pressure on the Fed to accommodate fiscal expansion (and weaken the USD). In addition, there is the spectre of a blanket 10% tarifs on all US imports and much larger tarifs, not only on some imports from China, but also imports from Europe and other countries.

It is far from clear where any of this ultimately will settle and what the precise impact on EM economies and asset prices will be. The 2018 US-China ‘trade war’ episode has shown that the related diversion of trade and FDI flows can benefit certain ‘connector’ countries, such as Mexico and Vietnam. However, an escalation into a global trade war, the risks of which seem higher now than in 2018, would unlikely be positive for EM economies more broadly. This remains an immense uncertainty for the world economy entering 2025 and will be in increasing focus as Q4 approaches.

## The challenging pursuit of summer carry

Heading into the summer holiday months, many participants naturally hope for a quiet period in markets, with limited volatility and returns generated by carry. We think the reality may be

diferent this year. While high levels of rates volatility are likely to hold back investors in EM credit, a large carry unwind already seems to have happened in EM local markets.

## EM sovereign credit yields and spreads to US credit still attractive

EM credit, particularly HY, appears an attractive source of potential summer carry. EM credit index yields remain elevated in a historical context (Figure 6). Spreads have compressed substantially, but especially in the EM HY sovereign segment (and to a lesser extent in EM IG sovereigns) they still screen as attractive relative to rating and maturity-matched US credit spreads. This is driven by the significant underperformance of EM versus US credit in the rally in Q1, which has only been reversed to a small extent so far in Q2 (Figure 7). Moreover, historical return data at the aggregate index level confirm that the summer months of July and August are usually associated with relatively low total return volatility (Figure 8).

FIGURE 6. EM sov yields are still high in a historical context. And while spreads have compressed...
![](images/c2748cf8b15ab75d6e5d7b26040233783662c9fbb4634dd1e1b83d8e0a614625.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

FIGURE 7. ... they remain wide vs US credit, especially in HY but also in IG
![](images/952e0685ad35db89eae7f32b42a4141f798ce22f35577daee6b899aeee1b2bb5.jpg)
\*Rating- and maturity-matched EM sovereign versus US corporate credit spread ratios.
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

## But high rates vol weighs on flows and carry-to-vol ratios

However, the same does not hold true when looking at spread volatility in the HY segment, which is likely also a consequence of the sometimes more limited trading liquidity over the summer that can result in sharp swings especially in smaller, higher-yielding names. This year has brought significant diferentiation among EM HY sovereigns driven by idiosyncratic developments, and we think it is likely that bottom-up and idiosyncratic considerations will remain an important driver of returns over the next few months as well (which we discuss in more detail below).

Furthermore, we think that while cross-market volatility has trended lower over the past few months, rates volatility (as measured, for example, by the MOVE index) remains far too high for comfort. First, HY-IG spread diferentials have historically been closely aligned with the MOVE index (Figure 9), but recently appear to have tightened further than persistently high rates volatility would have implied. And second, there is a clear correlation between flows into EM credit (and EM bond funds more broadly) and the MOVE index. At current levels of the index, EM bond funds have historically struggled to attract inflows (Figure 10). Of course, high rates volatility is not exclusively problematic for HY. In fact, given the high duration of the EM IG sovereign index, rates volatility results in elevated total return volatility for this segment in particular and, as a consequence, in unfavourable carry-versus-vol characteristics (Figure 11).

This, we believe, will remain an impediment for more substantial crossover flows into the asset class until rates volatility subsides.

FIGURE 8. July/August usually bring lower total return volatility, but HY spreads can be volatile

FIGURE 11. The high duration of EM sovereign indices, especially in IG, results in unfavourable carry-to-vol characteristics

Source: Bloomberg, EPFR, Barclays Research

## Local markets are already in a great carry unwind

Local markets are already providing a preview of why the summer carry may prove to be deceiving. Sell-ofs were triggered by election surprises in Mexico, South Africa and India (in addition to ongoing local noise in Brazil), and now the political turmoil in France. Beyond the implications at the country level, these shocks have led to a sharp FX carry unwind and positioning squeezes in rates, particularly in LatAm and CEE, with Asia largely spared. Election outcomes are proving to matter for carry trades, and we shouldn't forget that the one with the most global consequences is still to come this year.

High US rates and a strong USD, along with a very heavily positioned market following a period of low volatility, means contagion through the portfolio channel is fast. We tend to think the

market will need to take a breather over the summer. The question then becomes how much these moves are an overshoot, and where the FX actually needs to find a new equilibrium that incorporates a larger risk premium; also, whether countries that haven't moved yet need to catch up.

Markets are still digesting the shocks and it is too early to fade recent moves, in our view. A reduction in vol is needed for currencies to come back (Figure 12), and rates should eventually follow. Figure 13 shows how much EMFX actually weakened, versus what a simple model forecasts, which takes into account only the EUR and another that also controls for FX vol. The model using just the EUR falls very short of explaining recent moves. Adding FX vol is important in explaining moves for currencies that were under pressure (ie, high-carry FX and those related to the Europe shock in CEE), while low carry FX outperformed. The analysis points to outsized moves in the MXN, COP, PLN and CLP, while the ZAR has outperformed the model, although this is likely on the positive post-election outcome.

FIGURE 12. FX vol spiked for high-carry FX and those related to the Europe shock in CEE
![](images/cc9c58dd55402ab0966996ac30bcb1eb1f744e9d2505b89751056ecd079936ad.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

FIGURE 13. FX vol is important in explaining moves for currencies that were under pressure
![](images/54776cbdeec7fc3cbf3c46d79488691bf5fd208fe6e6c716acb57b11607b4d96.jpg)
Note: Blue marker refers to the fitted value of a model that regresses EM FX as a function of the EUR. Green marker refers to the same model but adding FX vol as a regressor.
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

## Higher-for-longer still means little room for error

## The Fed remains a constraint for EM local markets

Last quarter, we turned cautious on EM rates on the view that markets were pricing enough easing without the all-clear from US yields (see this note). The easy part of the disinflation process had already played out in many countries, and the margins of safety were running thin without an impulse from the Fed. The story has not changed much since then. Emerging markets are stuck in a protracted waiting game, where US data remain in the driver seat and exacerbates local vulnerabilities.

EMs are still (overall) pricing some normalization in the policy stance, but the US has become a fundamental constraint for how much central banks can ease. Broad USD strength, driven by higher US risk-neutral rates and a still resilient economy, makes it is hard for EM central banks to move too far (and especially too fast) ahead of the Fed. Cycles are still proving to have gotten ahead of themselves in this context, having priced a return to normal too early and are still vulnerable to corrections.

Figure 14 shows how the USD forced central banks to take a more cautious stance this cycle, with markets pricing out cuts both in countries that are already easing and those that are still on hold. Currencies also sold of as the USD strengthened, with the move exacerbated in countries where carry was eroding, albeit directional behavior has not been significantly diferent from those on hold (Figure 15). These trade-ofs are likely to continue as long as the Fed is data dependent.

FIGURE 14. EM curves priced out cuts as the USD strengthened, especially in countries already easing
![](images/2147d15c09c1edd3db90dcfd129af4af1832da8dcf9b5115294c23747de0212d.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

FIGURE 15. EM FX has been tied to the USD, regardless of whether currencies have carry
![](images/a841e4eed28a2df2b7a5370a9ae4fcaf01bed0d7c3b2411ec8920f3d582469f3.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

Looking ahead, while the US economy is gradually moderating, it is still operating above capacity, and any Fed normalization will entail policy rates staying above neutral for some time. In this context, EM 1y1y spreads versus the US are largely still at their tights (Figure 16), allowing very little room for error in the asset class. EM Central banks have opted to go slow, putting more emphasis on mitigating FX volatility and preserving carry, especially as local pressures have not been screaming for fast cycles. There also appears to be some hesitation from the market to price more cuts, with data dependence keeping uncertainty high across the board.

FIGURE 16. EMs have little margin of safety, with tight spreads to US rates
![](images/686e8c99e973cea8767b19188d71be4bfa2365dfdc146fd2785445d4dfe554d1.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

FIGURE 17. Most countries are pricing some easing, but with terminals above neutral
![](images/9f3df5879fc8b3b861e105a8bb9f7e94ab4edc416a1d9f786e898b58539cd321.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

For EMs to perform, two US rates catalysts will matter, as we have highlighted in the past: when the Fed delivers the first cut, and the depth of its cycle. While the start of Fed cuts may

ultimately reduce FX volatility and support rates, the depth of the cutting cycle in the US may prove a more lasting constraint. EM terminal policy rates are currently priced above some estimate of neutral across countries (Figure 17), with most cutting cycles quite shallow (Brazil the only country pricing outright hikes). We think this should remain the case, as it reflects the risks that a higher US neutral rate would inevitably translate into higher EM neutral rates as well.

## Though, fundamentally, high EM rates are not causing much pain yet

While asset prices are struggling with “higher for longer,” it's not clear that the same can be said of economic fundamentals. Real rates are optically high, but not so high relative to the US, and they have not been crushing growth or leading to a collapse in output-sensitive inflation measures.

The disinflation story has largely played out in EM, with inflation continuing to normalize (Figure 18). However, things are more challenging from here, with base efects unlikely to provide much of an impulse this year (see Embrace Goldilocks), and services inflation still need to ease in many countries for inflation to converge to target. In the past, the level of services inflation consistent with periods when inflation was in the target range was lower in most countries, as reflected by the red diamonds in Figure 19. The main exceptions are South Africa, India, Philippines, Malaysia and, to a lesser extent, Chile. Thus, overall there are still upside risks to the last mile of the disinflation process.

FIGURE 18. EM inflation continues to normalize, even with stickier core
![](images/4522e1ffe952961cae47e83117a81742ffe61ca3aa9d369017b399b62d28b295.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

FIGURE 19. But core services are still largely above levels compatible with the inflation target
![](images/3eff68bb6b238fc40ebe4d6924e3d5527d51e1a45373f8c324781af563a109a2.jpg)
Note: Brazil's target today is lower than the past, and thus levels consistent with past targets are higher compared with the current target. Source: Bloomberg, Haver Analytics, Barclays Research

The overall growth picture also looks decent in EM. Our economists' forecasts suggest that countries that experienced slow growth last year may have bottomed, especially those in Asia, and Chile and Peru (see economics section above). CEE is also expected to improve, but this is related more to base efects, while in Asia we actually see an impulse beyond this. Countries where growth was strong last year, such as Brazil, India and Mexico, are expected to slow, but remain above the average of the past 15 years.

## Fiscal dynamics in a higher-for-longer world could compel some central banks to ease

Another consideration in a higher-for-longer world are the debt implications. That is, defending the currency with high rates could lead to weaker growth and a higher local debt burden, while easing and letting the FX go could become a constraint for countries facing hard-currency debt pressures. Ideally, problematic debt dynamics should be addressed with a fiscal adjustment, but its true that some central banks could face pressure to cut rates in this context.

We run two simple exercises to assess local- and foreign-currency debt dynamics (see FXQ for details): on the one hand, how much local real rates need to change to keep local-currency debt stable, and on the other hand, how much the FX needs to move to keep foreign-currency debt stable. We use IMF data to have a comparable approach across countries (though we acknowledge this data set has its shortcomings). We combine these exercises with the composition of local- versus hard-currency debt and average maturity of the debt burden (Figure 20).

Figure 21 summarizes the results in four quadrants. Easing of local real rates would open more fiscal space in the local market, but at the same time the likely FX depreciation would tighten the space in the hard- currency market. Taking a look at these two forces at the same time can help map countries according to the degree of freedom debt dynamics allow for their monetary policy.

We find that foreign currency debt considerations are unlikely to be binding for EM central banks, as these ratios look manageable across the board. The Philippines screens as a candidate for caution when only looking at the foreign currency debt stock, but our analysis suggests there is margin to keep it stable even at a weaker FX, given fiscal and growth expectations. In local currency debt, Brazil, South Africa and Mexico could face pressures from the Treasury to lower rates, considering the current gap to local currency debt-stabilizing rates and initial debt levels.

FIGURE 20. Hard currency debt is low for many of the countries that would be vulnerable in FX terms
![](images/c5c1a120ce9a10e32a62309ff25ba25c9c8d90c01250568aaa453d5a2c260343.jpg)
Govt debt in USDGovt debt in IccyAverage maturity (RHS)

FIGURE 21. COP, MXN and ZAR could become vulnerable if currently high rates stay high
![](images/47309e351306cf14a208cdab1c89a91ae046ba68af413347f1e3ec3f831df9ce.jpg)
Source: IMF, Barclays Research
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

## EM rates & FX recommendations: Tactically looking for diferentiation

Holding a view for a quarter is not easy these days. Markets move violently up and down, waiting for a turning point that is not here yet. The US economy going in the right direction, decelerating very gradually, but this will take time and a shallow cycle is not enough to get EM local markets excited.

Markets will still seek policy divergence, but selectively, given limited wiggle room We thus expect rates to trade in a range, and recommend investors stay largely tactical in this context. That said, there could be an impulse to price divergence in select economies. A look at recent cutting cycles suggests FX underperformance is the trade to chase if central banks are in fact compelled to out-dove market pricing and ease more ahead of the Fed, while the rates trade requires getting ahead and paying the negative carry.

In the past, the playbook was to chase easing cycles via rates from beginning to end, as neutral takes a while to get priced and central banks tend to go below neutral. This has clearly not been the case this cycle, owing in part to the Fed constraint, but also because markets have come to know this pattern. Thus, we think the window to capture this move is short. Figure 22 shows that by the time the first cut (or an acceleration) materializes, the cycle has been largely priced. We think this could be the case this time, as there is the risk that the shallow US cycle could again become the driver. Therefore, it is better to get in the trade too early if one has conviction, despite high carry costs.

FIGURE 22. Markets priced rate cuts too early this cycle, becoming vulnerable to US rate corrections
![](images/fdccf087ef3a9c5198c28bb0622e72a43068a0d07ddbd4aaba240b98886a014e.jpg)
Note: HU is omitted due to scale reasons. Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

FIGURE 23. Currencies weaken as carry erodes, and pricing out of cuts has stabilising efects
![](images/7e029c6c403dadef59772b6adc2e2aa3ff2444fa6f66d08400b5bbac805e1d09.jpg)
Note: The chart shows USDXXX changes. Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

In FX, Chile and Brazil are prime templates to consider (Figure 23). Both countries delivered cuts, though at diferent paces, which impacted the FX. Carry erosion is likely already priced in Chile, with fundamentals again a relevant driver for the CLP. But as the cuts played out, the FX meaningfully weakened versus its peers. Meanwhile, BRL carry looks high on a relative basis. But looking across its own history, real rates are actually low versus the US and in line with the end of past cutting cycles (see this note), suggesting carry erosion supported BRL weakness (exacerbated by the current confidence crisis). Thus, we think that positioning for weaker currencies as the first cuts or accelerations get closer is a strategy that can be chased.

Certain central banks may thus face a larger domestic impulse for easing, driven by their own fundamentals, and markets may have room to price some more divergence, assuming EM stability. Last year was the year of LatAm and some CEEs, but now prime candidates to monitor throughout the quarter include Czech, Korea, Philippines, and Colombia, as well as South Africa, which also benefits from less political premium, as we mention below.

Weak German manufacturing should spillover to Czech, where growth has been slow since the pandemic and core services recently dropped dramatically. In Korea, positioning is less

crowded now, and increasing concerns of a slowdown in the domestic economy should add pressure for the curve to price more. In the Philippines, easing pressures should intensify as inflation falls into the target range in August, given already slowing growth on the back of high rates. Colombia could out-dove the market on easing inflationary pressures, but we stay on the sidelines for now, as the authorities will likely remain cautious in the near term to protect the FX with the region under pressure.

In Chile, the policy stance has already diverged and we are likely in the last innings of the easing cycle. Inflation has largely eased, but is in the last mile, and could prove dificult to get back to target. A more stable CLP should still help the disinflation process, along with lower fuel prices. However, electricity prices are the big upside risk. These are expected to add pressure in the coming months, as the CNE will adjust prices after adjusting them by only 1.8% since 2019. The impact of these increases on both headline and core is uncertain, especially as the government may ofer subsidies along the way and the second-round efects are dificult to estimate. We stay long 2y breakevens, as 1y expectations have started to creep up and should be monitored closely.

## Politics can also ofer room for diferentiation in EM local

As we mention above, EM assets have been afected by big election surprises in Mexico, South Africa and India, and now the political turmoil in France. This adds to already high noise in Brazi and ongoing geopolitics in Israel. These outcomes are proving to matter for local assets, and we see room for diferentiation on this front as well.

• The impact of the French elections on CEE: President Macron's decision to dissolve parliament and call snap elections generated an uncertainty shock that has afected EM as well. CEE currencies are the most vulnerable to an increasing risk premium driven by European politics, primarily via the Russian connection. The CEE FX sellof could extend, even if the reaction has already been large, as the risk of a Le Pen victory would raise questions about the sustainability of EU fund inflows to CEE countries and the EU’s commitment to counter Russian aggression. Consequently, we still see value in our long EURHUF recommendation.

• South Africa: South African assets were boosted by formation of what appears to be a de facto ANC-DA coalition, in an election that was positively resolved. The post-election premium compression in both USDZAR and SAGBS, which were lightly positioned and also spared of the recent carry unwind, still has room to go, though arguably at a slower pace. A stronger ZAR amid better sentiment should open scope for markets to price more probability of rate cuts (we see 50bp in cuts this year and another 50bp in 2025). We see value in the belly of the curve and recommend receiving 5y ZAR IRS.

• Israel: The situation remains very uncertain amid the ongoing war, and both USDILS and longer-dated Israeli rates command large risk premia. That said, we stay in our short EURILS recommendation, as the BoI and large foreign holdings by local funds likely cap upside risks in EURILS, while our constructive structural view on the FX points to a larger move lower. Moreover, we think ILS back-end rates are cheap, ofering a very wide premium over US rates; hence, we recommend receiving 5y5y ILS versus paying 5y5y USD OIS.

• Thailand: We are bearish TBH, as politics is once again a driver amid high profile court cases against PM Srettha, former PM Thaksin Shinawatra and the Move Forward Party. This, coupled with persisting legislative and legal troubles related to the funding and unclear timeline, continues to plague the proposed digital wallet scheme. Portfolio flows are likely to remain a drag on the THB in the short term, given still-weak foreign interest in low-yielding

Thai assets. We recently recommended being short THBKRW 3m NDFs. We fund out of KRW given an improving external sector.

• Mexico: The elections drove MXN weakness, as the darling trade of EM got unwound and markets incorporated the premium associated with AMLO's reforms. Markets are pricing just one cut from Banxico this year now, from almost three before, despite limited evidence of FX pass-through to inflation. While FX volatility will likely force Banxico to stay put in June, real rates are high and we think markets will go back to pricing Banxico as a function of the Fed as the situation recovers from the current shock.

We expect some near-term relief in rates and FX, given the likely overshoot and much negativism on the reforms already in the price. Relief in FX should mean the spread between TIIE and US rates compresses, as vol falls in the near term. We tactically recommend receiving 5y TIIE versus paying 5y SOFR, as we think there will be a quiet period before we get more details on the reforms and the US elections come into play.

• Brazil: In Brazil, we stay bearish on local assets. The DI curve is the only curve in EM pricing the end of the easing cycle, with inflation expectations de-anchoring, and a meaningful hike premium is priced (see this note for details). While we don't expect hikes from the BCB at this point, we doubt markets are in a rush to price these out. An estimation of risk-neutral rates suggests there are still upside risks if breakevens continue to rise, while the term premium has lagged the FX and fiscal risks and could also pressure rates higher.

Real rate spreads to the US are close to those at the end of past cycles, where the BCB was thought to have over-cut as carry eroded and the fiscal outlook was deteriorating. The narrative today is not so diferent, with the country in the midst of a confidence crisis, as inflation expectations de-anchor and fiscal targets are under question. Authorities are showing good will to rebuild credibility, but this will take time. We thus think the BRL still needs a local premium in the FX and expect it to continue to underperform.

## CNH to remain a favorite funder

Broad USD strength should weigh on Asia FX, especially low-yielders. We see expect USDCNH to go higher, at a PBOC-engineered gradual pace. Summer tends to see negative CNY seasonality given widening services deficits, and CNH conversions to HKD as we head into dividend payout season for Hong Kong-listed Chinese firms. This, combined with soft growth and likely moreaggressive policy responses from the US and Europe on trade, bodes badly for the CNY, especially with high risks going in the US elections. We maintain our short CFETS, and short CNHINR ideas, and enter into a long USDCNH 6m forward trade recommendation. In particular, INR stands out as an outperformer amid a firmer USD for longer, with the RBI expected to continue tightly managing the currency pair.

## EM credit recommendations: Credit picking

## A sideways summer for spreads, with scope for continued diferentiation

We think the uncertain and volatile global macro environment, as outlined above, will dent investors' appetite for directional risk taking in EM credit over the summer months, especially after the significant spread compression YTD. But we do not expect a major market correction either. EM fundamentals, especially for EM HY sovereigns, remain well supported by resilient growth, still-elevated prices of most key commodities, and in some cases, a positive policy trajectory. This has also been reflected in a positive rating trend: on our count, a total of nine EM HY sovereign issuers represented in the Bloomberg EM USD sovereign index have benefited from at least one rating upgrade YTD, while only Bolivia and Ukraine have sufered downgrades in this sector.

Moreover, we think positioning has turned neutral to supportive. Most EMBI-benchmarked funds have outperformed the index YTD (Figure 24), as they appear to have been well positioned for the HY-IG compression in Q1. However, more recently, fund performance has been broadly in line with index performance, which suggests that positioning has moved closer to the benchmark. Moreover, investor positioning in the CDX EM index has remained modestly short risk over the past few weeks, contrary to the substantial long risk positions by investors in CDX HY (Figure 25).

Our updated country-by-country positioning analysis based on reported fund holdings (Figure 26) lacks a clear beta trend and appears to reflect investors focusing on single-name selection and idiosyncratic developments: underweights in Turkiye have been cut back, and investors have added to Egypt, for example. In the distressed/defaulted segment, a nuanced picture emerges: positioning in Ecuador, Sri Lanka, and Ukraine has moved towards heavier overweights, while investors appear to have reduced exposure to Pakistan and Ghana, with positioning moving to a small underweight and very close to neutral, respectively, according to our data. Large overweights in Mexico and Brazil are reflective of EMBI funds’ of-benchmark holdings of corporates and/or local-currency bonds. As highlighted in EM credit strategy: EM corps to continue to outperform, 11 June 2024, on average, EMBI funds allocate c.13% of their portfolios to of-benchmark EM quasis and corporates, and close to 5% to EM local-currency bonds.

FIGURE 24. EMBI-benchmarked funds outperformed the index earlier in the year, but recent performance has been more in line
![](images/cb1c519105e39748f7f2f1961440847fbc4dae30cfba5de0ee1b81c64bbf6b49.jpg)
\*Based on a sample of 68 EMBI-benchmarked funds. Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

FIGURE 25. Investor positioning in CDX EM has been modestly short risk recently, contrary to CDX HY
![](images/7d9eb45c84a2b89dcd1636508ac46be2f07f01cf4e6b1f7f3467eded9ce82661.jpg)
Source: DTCC, Barclays Research

FIGURE 26. Idiosyncratic considerations, rather than beta, appear to have driven allocation decisions for EMBI funds
![](images/b44840256bb92e568f6205c09395cd021bea01ee79baa4b3385aeaa3e1f2f01c.jpg)
\*Based on a sample of 34 EMBI-benchmarked funds with a reporting lag of 1-3m. Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

Closer-to-neutral positioning among investors is likely a reflection of a generally low level of conviction in an uncertain macro environment. But it also provides some bufers in case of unforeseen negative developments.  In sum, we expect EM credit spreads to trend sideways over the summer, but with potential bouts of volatility and diferentiation according to idiosyncratic and credit-specific considerations.

More specifically, we recommend:

• Focus on credit selection in HY sovereigns: Although YTD spread moves have been characterized by overall HY-IG compression, the diferentiation in returns among HY sovereigns has been very meaningful. We expect idiosyncratic stories, rather than beta factors, to continue to drive EM HY sovereign returns over the summer. Our high conviction OWs include Ghana and Sri Lanka on our expectation that the respective restructuring processes can be successfully concluded soon and will deliver recovery rates higher than current market prices. We also see further upside in the post-restructuring bonds of Zambia and Suriname (both OW). In the performing EM HY sector, we rate Nigeria, Egypt and Ecuador OW, upgrade Kenya to OW (from MW) and recently also upgraded Dominican Republic to OW. Conversely, we recently downgraded Bahamas to MW (from OW) following outperformance and think that the post-election, medium-term uncertainties in South Africa warrant a continued UW stance. We also maintain an UW rating on Argentina.

• The dificult search for value in IG sovereigns. Aggregate valuations of EM IG sovereigns look attractive relative to HY and relative to similarly rated IG credit, as discussed above. However, the sector faces substantial headwinds from rates volatility and idiosyncratic developments in some index heavyweights. Notably, we rate Mexico UW given political and policy uncertainties after the elections and we downgrade KSA sovereign credit to UW (from MW) and recommend buying KSA 5y CDS, reflecting our concerns about a deteriorating supply/ demand balance. Lack of supply has resulted in very tight spreads for Kazakhstan (UW) and Malaysia (downgrade to MW from OW). This leaves the CEE region as a potential target for investors with relatively attractive spreads, including for global USD bond investors. We rate Romania OW and Hungary MW, and see opportunities in Poland's 10y USD sector – even though contagion from the political volatility in the euro area may be a hurdle in the near term, possibly (partially) ofset by underweight positioning among EMBI funds.

• In EEMEA/LatAm corporates we expect further corporate-to-sovereign outperformance, supported by favourable net supply dynamics and continued crossover interest from US IG funds. However, we do not think this positive momentum will benefit all credits, and recommend being increasingly selective in HY. In EEMEA, we like HY credits with idiosyncratic tailwinds. In the mid-beta space, we rate OCPMR OW and would favor the long-end, given fair valuations to similarly rated credits in Asia and EEMEA, and Morocco's positive rating trajectory, which could see OCP's s index rating being eventually upgraded to IG. In the higher-beta space, we like IHS and KOS (both OW) given potential positive catalysts, and we like Turkish banks’ subordinated bonds, with issuance likely receding and tailwinds from Turkiye’s ongoing rebalancing of its economy. In LatAm,  SAMMIN (OW), BEEFBZ (OW), TIGO (OW) and VTRCOM (OW) are among our favourite credits heading into the summer, while we are more cautious on CFELEC (UW), CDEL (UW) and CELARA (UW).

• In Asia corporates, we recommend focusing on HY, including lower rated segments (B/CCC), especially in India HY corporates (OW), which will likely continue to benefit from macro tailwinds from the country's positive growth momentum. Corporate fundamentals are largely stable to improving in India HG (MW) and Korea HG (UW), but we think valuations are fair to tight. In the China complex, we expect the constructive tone to continue in asset management companies (AMCs), driven by the sector's reform narrative. Fundamentals for TMTs and other corporates are on a strong footing, but headline noise around US-China relations will likely remain a potential overhang. Performances among property developers are likely to vary by name, as the efects of the recent unprecedented policy steps to tackle the protracted property-sector crisis remain uncertain.

EM Heat Maps

# A comparative overview of EM country metrics

Our heat maps track various key indicators across Emerging Asia, EEMEA and Latin America related to political election calendars, vulnerability risks, and exposure to energy price volatility.

Our heat maps help to diferentiate across EM countries according to their vulnerabilities and strengths.

• Political Heat Map: Provides an election calendar marked by the relevance of each event.

• Vulnerability Heat Map: Provides a look at key metrics regarding the external, fiscal and financial risks of key EM countries we cover.

• Oil Heat Map: Analyzes the degree to which various EM countries are exposed to the volatility of oil prices and the associated efect on their economies.

• Current and Prospective IMF Involvement: We describe current IMF programs across the EM universe and where there might be programs in the future.

(2) Refers to the short-term external debt on a remaining maturity basis reported by the IMF. (3) Data from the IMF's Sovereign Debt Investor Base. (4) General government data from the latest IMFWEO. (5) Domestic credit by banks to the private sector as reported by World Bank Source: Haver Analytics, IMF, Bloomberg, Barclays Research

## Emerging Asia

## Patience still the watchword

EM Asia generally started the year with stronger sequential growth, even if we have a cautious take on China's GDP data. With CPIs also still not quite where they need to be and no clear turnaround yet for Fed policy, EM Asia central banks will likely remain relatively conservative in terms of monetary policy easing.

Trade Summary | China | EM Asia Frontier Economies | Hong Kong | India | Indonesia | Korea | Malaysia | Philippines | Singapore | Taiwan | Thailand

## Overview

Compared with the rest of the world, Emerging Asian central banks will likely remain relatively conservative in terms of monetary policy easing.

• With the Chinese economy still under pressure, we believe the People's Bank of China (PBoC) is likely to further ease monetary policy via a 10bp cut in the MLF rate and a 25-50bp reduction in banks’ RRR in H2.

• Outside of China, however, our base case is for just three Emerging Asian central banks – Bangko Sentral ng Pilipinas (BSP), the Bank of Korea (BoK) and the Reserve Bank of India (RBI) – to start cutting policy rates this year. In terms of timing, we have rate cuts commencing in October for both Korea and the Philippines – though the latter could start earlier – and in December for India.

• At the other end of the spectrum, we note a relatively high risk that Bank Indonesia (BI) hikes its policy rate again to defend the IDR; a RON95 petrol price hike, while not our base case, could also trigger policy tightening from Bank Negara Malaysia (BNM).

## What growth slowdown?

Economic activity in the region still does not seem to have deteriorated enough to send even the more pro-growth central banks sprinting for easing – in fact, recent growth developments have been relatively positive.

Q1 GDP data suggest the region generally started the year with stronger sequential growth – we estimate economic activity rose by 1.4% q/q sa on average, versus growth of 0.9% in Q4 23.

• In particular, we estimate China's headline GDP rose by a stronger-than-expected 3.0% q/q sa in Q1 24, picking up from 0.8% in Q4 23 – though we suspect this may not be fully capturing underlying developments in the economy.

• At the same time, economic activity in India proved significantly more resilient than expected, with sequential growth slowing only modestly to an estimated 1.3% q/q sa in Q1 from 2.1% in Q4.

Taking this into account, our 2024 GDP growth projection for Emerging Asia as a whole now stands at 5.2% – up by 0.4 percentage points (pp) from our 4.8% estimate in the March edition of the Emerging Markets Quarterly – implying a broadly similar expansion as 2023's 5.3%, not a slowdown.

• To be sure, the upgrade largely reflects our China GDP growth forecast – which tends to dominate the regional aggregate due to the sheer size of its economy – which we have raised to 5.0% from 4.4% previously. This, in turn, came mainly on the back of the strongerthan-expected oficial Q1 GDP data – which we are taking a cautious view on, as noted above. Regardless, our current 5.0% forecast for 2024 would imply only a marginal slowdown from 5.2% in 2023.

• Still, we have also raised GDP growth forecasts since March for Korea (by +0.8pp), India (+0.4pp), Taiwan (+0.3pp) and Malaysia (+0.2pp) – ofsetting a 0.2pp reduction in our growth projection for the Philippines.

For 2025, we continue to forecast GDP growth of 4.6%, which represents a slowdown from our 5.2% projection for this year.

• We have left our 2025 GDP growth forecast for China unchanged at 4.0% – implying a sharper drop from our upgraded 5.0% estimate for 2024.

• Outside of China, we have raised our 2025 GDP growth forecasts for Singapore (+0.4pp), Malaysia (by +0.2pp) and Taiwan (+0.2pp) – but ofset these with sharp 0.7pp reductions in our estimates for both Thailand and Hong Kong and 0.1pp cut for the Philippines.

FIGURE 2. Barclays GDP growth forecasts
![](images/194c2d84560836f3e6f25247e43c199b0429f121da3e07592224c332127a60f6.jpg)
Source: Haver Analytics, Barclays Research

In contrast to the regional aggregate, which China tends to dominate, a simple average of our GDP growth forecasts across the 10 major economies in Emerging Asia would in fact imply a pickup from 3.6% in 2023 to 4.1% in 2024, followed by a moderation to 3.8% in 2025.

• Specifically, we continue to project GDP growth rising in 2024 in Taiwan, Singapore, Korea, Thailand, Malaysia and the Philippines – and falling in India, Hong Kong and China. GDP

growth should then moderate in 2025 for most economies, except for Thailand, Malaysia and the Philippines.

• By contrast, Indonesia's 2024 and 2025 GDP growth should remain broadly stable at 5.0%, in line with the pre-pandemic trend for economic growth to hew close to 5% y/y.

Our projected rise and fall in the region's GDP growth partly reflects base efects – which we estimate would imply slight underperformance on the part of our 2024 forecast of 4.1%. Taking the Q1 data into account and assuming trend q/q sa growth for subsequent quarters, we estimate the simple average of GDP growth across Emerging Asia should rise from 3.6% in 2023 to 4.3% this year before moderating to 3.6% in 2025 (Barclays: 4.1%, 3.8%).

Relative to what quarterly trend growth rates would suggest, we have more cautious 2024 GDP growth forecasts for China, Taiwan, the Philippines, Korea and Hong Kong, reflecting negative domestic factors and shifting export dynamics – and comparatively stronger outcomes in Thailand and Malaysia where there is greater scope for a catch-up in economic activity.

Source: Haver Analytics, Barclays Research

## Shifting export dynamics

Into Q2, the available data suggest Emerging Asian exports edged up in May after recovering in April from the slide into a sharp dip in March. Our Barclays EM Asia Export Cycle Tracker – which aims to provide a broad-based and timelier measure of the region's overall export cycle – suggests a slight pickup in May following a rebound in April. Our estimates indicate Singapore's non-oil domestic exports held up sequentially in May amid a jump in shipments from China, Vietnam and India, ofsetting a sharp drop in Taiwan's exports and a slight slowdown for Korea. The region's export cycle now appears to be marginally below trend, with shipment levels looking much improved compared with their Q2 23 lows (see Emerging Asia: A new tool to track the export cycle, 15 April 2024).

FIGURE 4. EM Asia Export Cycle Tracker
![](images/84984250a29442e890791f7b6423bd6bce31e7b8fcbeade72fb27dfa9b0f674c.jpg)
Source: Haver Analytics, Barclays Research

FIGURE 5. Seasonally adjusted export levels relative to trend: China, India and NIEs
![](images/8967129e3298201ce4b2e0f0cfbb313b2d33f00e803c4fe2ed08875c80103b18.jpg)
Source: Haver Analytics, Barclays Research

FIGURE 6. Seasonally adjusted export levels relative to trend: Rest of Asean
![](images/8f244d62ac15f8d37c8c3cb5ebf15d4c1ccfbb277389d4d40ac67ea67359cab1.jpg)
Source: Haver Analytics, Barclays Research

However, while we continue to expect a gradual export recovery for the region as a whole, we are turning more cautious on shipment volumes from the North Asian tech heavyweights of Korea and Taiwan where the tech upcycle has been more front-loaded. The positive momentum in the tech cycle has so far been largely concentrated in the more sophisticated end of the semiconductor supply chain catering to the artificial intelligence (AI) boom – to the benefit of Korea and Taiwan. To be sure, the strong nominal export growth will likely sustain, helped by robust chip prices. However, there are signs that following the earlier ramp-up in shipments in H2 23, exports out of Korea and Taiwan appear to have lost some momentum in real terms and downshifted to a more gradual pace of expansion. This has had a particularly visible impact on sequential GDP growth in Taiwan, which slowed to just 0.3% q/q sa in Q1 24 from an average of 2.1% in the preceding three quarters.

By contrast, a gradual broadening of the tech upcycle should provide a back-loaded lift to Southeast Asian economies, especially Malaysia. There are increasing signs that Southeast Asian tech exports – which have been lagging on account of greater exposure to legacy chips such as those for smartphones and server/PC demand – are also bottoming out, albeit gradually, though this has yet to be reflected clearly in the industrial production data. Malaysia – which has sufered deep export underperformance in recent quarters – has significant scope for a catch-up in economic activity.

Exports should also remain the bright spot in the Chinese economy, even in the face of escalating in tarifs and other trade barriers. In the medium term, we think China's diversified and broadening export markets, innovation and cutting-edge technology and products, as well as the new wave of Chinese companies "going global," will continue to lend support to Chinese exports, especially in volume terms (see China Exports: An enduring bright spot, 7 June 2024).

Source: CEIC, Barclays Research

![](images/c54038ea264297908c5bfb585c0f0bbd89cba972785e4c4fcafa2e9643b4acd3.jpg)

## Domestic drags

However, there are significant domestic drags in China, where we believe it will be dificult to sustain the surprising strength implied by the oficial Q1 GDP print – which we expect to be followed by a slump in sequential growth rates. Not only have industrial production and other indicators of economic activity slowed, the well-known travails in China's residential property market remain deep, challenging and largely unresolved. Given Hong Kong's deep

economic linkages with the mainland – along with domestic troubles such as its own residential property market downturn and more consumers opting to spend their money in nearby cities such as Shenzhen – our caution on China extends to the the special administrative region as well.

Likewise, amid a likely waning of tech export volume momentum, our GDP growth forecasts reflect some caution in Korea due to negative domestic factors – which are also weighing on the Philippines.

• Like China and Hong Kong, Korea is also experiencing a residential property market downturn, which is fuelling financial stability challenges in the real estate project-financing loan segment – the size of which we estimate to be equivalent to around 10% of GDP. Government eforts to contain these problems will likely weigh on the economy (see Korea: Financial Stability – Project financing fix risks stagnation, 30 May 2024).

• In the Philippines, the efects of earlier aggressive monetary policy tightening are becoming increasingly visible, weighing on private consumption and investment. While there should also be some benefits from the broadening of the tech upcycle, this is unlikely to be a gamechanger for the Philippines' more domestically oriented economy.

## Here comes fiscal

By contrast, the domestic factors are likely to play out more positively in Thailand, where we also see scope for economic activity to play catch-up. In particular, the signing of the much-delayed budget into law in late April has finally reopened the fiscal taps in Thailand, which should help revive economic activity – even as the government is trying to push through its digital wallet program to distribute cash to households – though we would note that political risks seem to be heating up again.

## Inflation cooling – but still elevated

With economic activity staying generally resilient, we sense a continued lack of comfort over inflation – keeping central banks cautious against easing too quickly.

While our forecast remains for CPI inflation across Emerging Asia as a whole to moderate from 2.2% in 2023, our latest 2024 forecast of 1.9% is 0.1pp higher than the 1.8% estimated the March edition of the Emerging Markets Quarterly – though our 2025

projection is now down to 2.2% from 2.6%. In contrast to the 2025 rebound in the regional aggregate however, the simple average of our forecasts shows a continued slide, from 3.2% in 2023 to 2.3% in 2024 to 2.1% in 2025.

• While we have reduced our 2024 CPI forecasts for Hong Kong (-0.4pp), the Philippines (-0.3pp) and Indonesia (-0.2pp), we have raised our projections for China (+0.2pp), Taiwan (+0.3pp) and Singapore (+0.1pp).

• For 2025, our CPI forecasts are up only slightly for Taiwan (+0.3pp), Indonesia (+0.2pp) and the Philippines (+0.1pp), but down more significantly for China (-0.8pp), Singapore (-0.7pp), Hong Kong (-0.4pp) and Thailand (-0.2pp).

• We estimate the simple average of food inflation moderated to 3.3% y/y from 3.7% – possibly reflecting a lagged efect of earlier declines in global food prices – which is still notably higher than the 2015-19 average of 2.7%.

• The bigger reprieve has been energy. Despite a short-lived spike to USD90/bbl in April, our estimates indicate energy costs have remained tilted towards disinflation, falling by a slightly deeper 0.6% y/y in January-April 2024 versus the 0.3% decline in Q4 23.

More fundamentally, we estimate the simple average of core inflation in the region cooled to 2.3% y/y in January-April from 2.6% in Q4 23 – but is also above its 2016-19 average of 2.0%. We suspect continued progress towards the untangling of supply-side bottlenecks and normalising inflation expectations have helped to moderate price pressures across the region. That said, still-low unemployment rates and relatively tight labour market conditions are likely keeping core inflation relatively sticky.

## Most central banks still not in a hurry to ease

Overall, the CPI outlook – falling though the latest prints may be – is still not quite where it needs to assure some central banks that monetary policy easing will not simply reignite price pressures.

• The latest CPI readings in a number of economies – especially India, the Philippines, Singapore and Korea – are still above or on the high-side of their respective central bank's inflation comfort zone. Taiwan's Central Bank of China (CBC) notably announced a 25bp RRR hike on 13 June, highlighting that this would help contain inflation expectations and that monetary policy is still tightening despite leaving the policy rate unchanged.

• At the same time, policymakers are generally cautious over the risk of a CPI resurgence, warning of upside risks and uncertainties. Even in Malaysia, where inflation has been relatively benign for some time, BNM has been stressing that adjustments to administered retail fuel prices could have a higher likelihood of sparking of second-round efects in the current economic environment compared with previous episodes.

FIGURE 25. Latest CPI inflation readings versus central bank comfort zones
![](images/b9998566766faa6e3d8221fa103476ba998edf228057c73a031adb3db65d9420.jpg)
Note: Figures refer to latest headline or core CPI reading. For Indonesia and Thailand, we compare core inflation readings with their central banks' headline inflation targets as we believe policymakers are currently more focused on the core measure. For Singapore and Malaysia, where there is no inflation-targeting regime, we assume a comfort zone of 2% for core inflation.

Related to the continued lack of comfort over inflation is that economic activity does not seem to have deteriorated enough to worry even the more pro-growth central banks – in fact, recent GDP figures have been relatively positive, as noted above, implying hotter output gaps. Taking a simple average of GDP-based output gaps across the major Emerging Asian economies, our estimates suggest the region's output gap improved to a positive 0.3 standard deviations in Q1 from a practically closed position in Q4. Notably, our estimates suggest output gaps returned to positive territory in Q1 in Hong Kong, China, Korea and Indonesia – though, as noted above, it is unclear whether China's oficial GDP data is fully reflecting the state of the economy. Only Malaysia and Thailand remain underwater from this perspective – but even there, our estimates indicate their negative output gaps also narrowed in Q1, and their respective central banks have been shrugging of growth fears.

FIGURE 26. EM Asia output gap (simple average)

Don't ignore financial stability

Financial stability is also an underappreciated consideration for many Emerging Asian central banks, in our view.

• Both the BoK and BoT have been citing the need to contain household debt – the latter in particular, historically quite pro-growth, has gone as far as to claim that rate cuts would do little to help economic activity.

• Similarly, the CBC highlighted that its 25bp RRR hike announced on 13 June – when it noted monetary policy was still tightening despite the unchanged policy rate – was also targeted at cooling the housing market.

• BNM – the central bank we view as being the most focused on financial stability in the region – is likely also wary of cutting when its relatively measured post-pandemic hikes have only brought the policy rate back to its end-2019 level of 3.00%, lest rates staying too low for too long start to encourage excessive risk-taking and other financial imbalances.

FIGURE 28. Household debt

![](images/d0eb778d9c3153110af4a3fe8cb0e8548de6c8a0ae340673de878cfeae070482.jpg)
Source: CEIC, Barclays Research

## The Fed matters

And then there is the US Federal Reserve – and its efects on global currency markets.

In Emerging Asia: The Fed stoics (7 May 2024), we reviewed how the eight major central banks in Emerging Asia (outside of China and Hong Kong) arrived at each of their monetary policy tightening and easing decisions since 2010 – and identified three broad groups in terms of their reaction functions:

• BI, which had surprised the market with rate hikes to defend the IDR in October 2023 and April 2024, is the central bank most focused on the currency in Emerging Asia – and thus the one that will be most constrained from cutting before the Fed and, in fact, at risk of having to tighten further should the exchange rate come under renewed pressure.

• The BoK, BSP and CBC also keep an eye on the currency and the Fed – but clearly not to the same extent as BI.

• The "Fed stoics" are the RBI, BNM, BoT and MAS: central banks whose monetary policy decisions are least likely to be influenced by the Fed or the currency.

While there remains significant uncertainty over precisely when the Fed will start cutting, we see scope for policy rates to fall in India, Korea and the Philippines ahead of the US.

• As one of the Fed stoics, the RBI will likely be mainly focused on its domestic growth/inflation dynamics rather than the Fed. We expect inflation to undershoot the central bank's forecasts, allowing the RBI to cut opportunistically in December to further support already-solid growth. That said, the risk is that policymakers hold back, questioning the necessity of easing when economic activity is already well and good.

• For both the BoK and BSP, the importance of the Fed will likely wane as their respective CPIs return to comfort levels – allowing policymakers to better stomach the inflation implications of a weaker exchange rate should their rate cuts worsen interest rate diferentials with the US. Our base case is for both to cut in October, though this could start earlier in the Philippines given BSP's more singular focus on inflation, as opposed to the BoK's relatively multi-faceted approach.

## Politics: What we are watching

Finally, as the surprisingly subpar outcome for Prime Minister Narendra Modi in India's recent general elections should serve to remind, politics are an ever-latent source of potential volatility.

• With Lawrence Wong succeeding Lee Hsien Loong as Singapore's prime minister in May, speculation is rife over the timing of the next general elections, which must be held by November 2025. While we note the risk of an early election, our base case is for polls to only be called next year, once there is more progress in bringing down residential property prices and core inflation. Even so, these cost pressures will likely remain significant issues that Mr Wong and the ruling People's Action Party (PAP) will have to address in the coming polls.

• In Thailand, there is increasing political noise, with court cases being brought against Prime Minister Srettha Thavisin and former Prime Minister Thaksin Shinawatra, both of the Pheu Thai party.

## China: Momentum softens

Jian Chang, Barclays Bank, Hong Kong | Yingke Zhou, Barclays Bank, Hong Kong | Ying Zhang, Barclays Bank, Hong Kong | Mitul Kotecha, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Lemon Zhang, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Avanti Save, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Yimin Weng, Barclays Bank, Singapore

While we think this year's 5% growth target is achievable, given the very "strong" Q1 GDP growth outturn, we think the risks to our growth forecasts are still tilted to the downside, in view of: 1) potential larger-than-expected decline in housing market activity; and 2) a faster slowdown in infrastructure investment as the authorities enhance scrutiny of financial risks/debts facing local governments. We expect a sharp slowdown in growth momentum to only 0.2% q/q saar in Q2 (partly due to the high Q1 comparison) and an average of 3.5% in H2.

• Credit: The efects of the recent unprecedented policy steps to tackle the protracted property-sector crisis, including lower down-payment requirements for home buyers and state purchases of completed and unsold housing units from property developers, remain to be seen. Given this, we remain cautious about becoming overoptimistic given the seemingly small scale and potential dificulties in implementing these policies. Developers' credit performances are likely to vary by name. That said, the policy moves are steps in the right direction. We think it appropriate to continue to focus on China's new growth engine credits like TMTs and idiosyncratic credit stories in corporates and asset management companies (AMCs), though headline noise around the US-China relationship remains a potential overhang for the former.

• FX:  We expect USDCNY continue to grind higher at a PBoC-engineered gradual pace. Summer tends to see negative CNY seasonality given widening services deficits, and CNH conversions to HKD as we head into dividend payout season for Hong Kong-listed Chinese firms. This, combined with soft growth and likely more-aggressive policy responses from the US and Europe on trade, bodes badly for the CNY. We maintain our short CFETS, and short CNHINR ideas, and enter into a long USDCNH 6m forward trade recommendation.

• Rates: We expect a steeper curve, driven mostly by a somewhat anchored front-end and higher long-dated rates over the long run. The start of special bond auctions in May should widen term premia slowly as supply picks up. Short-tenor rates are likely to stay anchored, reflecting record-low policy rates and easy liquidity. We continue to recommend pay 3s10s CGB spread.

We think the May activity data suggests that: 1) the real estate sector is still searching for a bottom, remaining the single largest drag on growth; 2) the improvement in retail sales is a welcome sign, but we think its sustainability remains in doubt given the one-of boost to online sales from eCommerce giants' changing promotional strategies<sup>2</sup> , a deteriorating labour market, financially constrained households and negative wealth efect; and 3) the faster-than-expected slowdown in IP growth suggests that stronger exports were more than ofset by weaker domestic demand.

Overall, we expect a sharp slowdown in growth momentum to only 0.2% q/q saar in Q2 (partly due to the high Q1 comparison) and an average of 3.5% in H2. While our base case is for the government to achieve the 5% growth target this year, we think the risks to our growth forecasts are still tilted to the downside, in view of: 1) potential larger-than-expected decline in housing

market activity, and 2) a faster slowdown in infrastructure investment as the authorities enhance scrutiny of financial risks/debts facing local governments.

Exports have been the bright spot in the Chinese economy this year, benefiting from techintensive green and high-end manufacturing, cost competitiveness amid domestic deflation and excess capacity, and China’s expanding trade and investment with countries in the Global South. The threat of tarif escalation is still a headwind, but we see strength in the short term in light of: 1) a likely sustained global/EM manufacturing upcycle, as evidenced by global manufacturing PMIs; 2) still-resilient US consumer demand and likely continued inventory restocking; and 3) a still-competitive currency, as measured by the real efective exchange rate (REER).

In response to the bleak outlook for economic fundamentals, the government rolled out more bold and forceful measures to support the beleaguered housing market. In particular, the PBoC announced in May that it would establish a nationwide programme to unleash CNY300bn of relending funds to be extended to 21 banks, which should translate into an estimated CNY500bn (0.4% of GDP) of credit for housing purchases. However, we think the scale of the support is too small in the context of, perhaps, CNY28trn worth of unsold properties (estimated using the extremely elevated housing inventory to sales ratio), which implies that the CNY500bn of extra funding will account for less than 2% of unsold inventory.
