## EM Asia Frontier Economies: Attractive

Avanti Save, CFA, Barclays Bank, Singapore

• Sri Lanka: The SRILAN bonds are currently trading in the 57-60 handle and we expect progress on restructuring to accelerate in the coming months. Our fair value estimate is 60-65. Given this, we retain our rating on the sovereign at Overweight.

• Pakistan: We maintain an Overweight rating on the sovereign. Pakistan bonds have delivered solid returns in 2024 and are currently trading with yields around 11-12%. We expect the government to successfully negotiate an IMF program in coming weeks which is likely to be followed by bilateral funding/investment commitments. These actions could also provide upward pressure to the sovereign credit ratings (Caa3/CCC+/CCC all Stable).

## Sri Lanka: Tick tock, tick tock

Restructuring could be completed in the next few months. We retain our Overweight rating on the Sri Lanka bond complex. Our baseline estimate is for a recovery value of 60-65, which includes potential sweeteners from past due interest (PDI) bonds, and the value-recovery instruments (VRI)/macro-linked bonds (MLB). A few recent developments that suggest the restructuring is progressing and likely to be completed in the next few months. First,

government oficials and agencies have recognised<sup>3</sup> that progress on restructuring is critical for timely disbursals from the IMF, and completion of the restructuring will accelerate foreign institution lending/activity in the country. Second, oficial/bilateral creditors and SRILAN bondholders are likely keen to complete the restructuring ahead of the political cycle. Along these lines, Bloomberg reported<sup>4</sup>  that the Oficial Creditor Committee (OCC) has submitted its concluding agreement for Sri Lanka to sign of.

FIGURE 34. Total returns for Frontier sovereigns (YTD)
![](images/96525dd2ea5e9d6706b20fe057e230a51ae2e931f4121a01b6ab4448ec1eaaf5.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

## Bondholders and government oficials are awaiting IMF feedback. Following the

negotiations that concluded in early April, we expect the IMF to provide feedback on the MLB. In our view, the required adjustments might include incorporating real GDP in addition to nominal GDP, and lowering the payouts in the upside scenarios. It is not clear if another round of negotiations with bondholders will be required before the proposal is finalised. However, we do not expect any talks to be prolonged or hit a standstill, with constructive negotiations likely to get this over the finish line in the next few months.

The PDI on the SRILAN bonds is material. The SRILAN 6.85 25s/28s/29s/30s have high past due interest. We believe the sovereign may consider sweetening the deal for bondholders through creative recovery options for PDI. Investors might be given two options for receiving PDI bonds: 1) upfront in LKR without any haircut; or 2) capitalised and exchanged for a conventional bond without a haircut (or minimal haircut).

We recommend buying the SRILAN '25s, '28s, '29s, and '30s given their high PDI and recovery levels in an expected range of 60-65.

VRI/MLB likely to be included. Based on Sri Lanka's feedback<sup>5</sup>  on the proposal for macrolinked bonds, we believe contingent-recovery instruments similar to GDP warrants or linked to GDP could be included as sweeteners. However, the drawbacks include the complexities in determining their value and their lack of eligibility for inclusion in major EM bond indices. We find that the value of these instruments has ranged from zero to USD10 in other EM sovereign restructurings. See Sri Lanka: Tick tock, tick tock, 14 June 2024.

## Pakistan: Budget, IMF, Overweight

Buy PKSTAN 27/31/51. Even though the bonds are now trading in-line with peers, we believe they still ofer attractive yields. Potential positive news about IMF, foreign direct investment/ funding, and credit ratings could provide a boost to valuations.

Ambitious targets. The Finance Minister, Muhammad Aurangzeb, presented the FY25 budget and set a budget-deficit target of 6.9% of GDP (federal)<sup>6</sup> . Notably, the expected consolidation is underpinned by a tax collection target of PKR13trn, to be achieved by raising taxes and removing tax exemptions<sup>7</sup> . For reference the revised budget for FY24 had a target of PKR8.4trn (original target was PKR7.5trn).

Debt burden constraining fiscal space. The proposed budget reflects higher expenditure in FY25 mainly due to larger interest payments and higher subsidies to the power sector. The interest payments are estimated to be PKR9.8bn (89% for domestic debt), which is equivalent to 55% of tax and non-tax revenues estimated by the government for FY25. While Pakistan's stock of external commercial debt is not alarming, it is worth noting that ongoing access to funds and robust economic growth are necessary to keep debt within sustainable levels. In this context, we estimate that the external debt servicing costs for FY25 will total around USD25bn and we believe that the government plans to rely on rollovers from bilaterals to meet these costs.

Paving way for an IMF program. The proposed tax policies have raised concerns among the parliamentarians as they debate the budget<sup>8</sup> . Some adjustments are likely, but overall the government is likely to retain the aim to enhance tax-to-GDP ratio to 13% in the next three years (from 9% currently) in order to negotiate a new Extended Fund Facility (EFF) with the IMF. Importantly, such a target will likely imply that, if implemented, debt sustainability could remain in check. The Finance Minister has indicted that the new programme could be agreed in July<sup>9</sup> .

See Pakistan: Budget, IMF, Overweight, 18 June 2024 for full details.

## Hong Kong: Weakness emerging

Ying Zhang, Barclays Bank | Yingke Zhou, Barclays Bank, Hong Kong | Jian Chang, Barclays Bank, Hong Kong | Mitul Kotecha, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Lemon Zhang, Barclays Bank, Singapore

Hong Kong’s economy accelerated q/q in Q1 24, helped by a continued recovery in exports. That said, weakness emerged as the contraction in private consumption deepened. We maintain our forecast that Hong Kong’s GDP growth will slow to 2.5% in 2024, from 3.2% y/y in 2023, given changed consumption patterns of visitors and Hong Kong residents, and the sluggish property market. In addition, we continue to see uncertainties on exports from any tarif escalation between China and major economies. Expectations of a delayed Fed rate cut also suggest borrowing costs to stay higher for longer.

• FX: We expect USDHKD to stay close to the middle of the 7.75-7.85 convertibility undertaking, before falling in Q4 24. HKD demand driven by dividend payouts (USD80bn) by HK-listed Chinese firms should emerge in the summer, generating some HKD strength in the short run. Further out, we expect the start of a Fed cutting cycle to support the HKD as rate diferentials narrow.

• Rates: We expect HK rates to bear flatten in the near term, before bull steepening after the Fed starts its easing cycle. Our analysis shows 3m Hibor tends to rise from June to July, likely driven by the dividend payout schedule discussed above. The start of the Fed easing cycle should help keep HK rates rangebound before rates move lower and curves dis-invert, albeit slower than in the US. We recommend a pay 1y HKD IRS trade, entry 4.59, target 4.90, stop loss 4.44.

Hong Kong’s economy grew by 2.7% y/y in Q1, a marked slowdown from the 4.3% expansion in Q4 23, as favourable base efects faded. While the pace of y/y expansion beat market expectations (Bloomberg: 0.8%) and on a sequential basis growth of 2.3% q/q sa in Q1 24, accelerating from 0.2% in Q4 23, we note signs of softness in the fundamental data. In particular, the contraction in private consumption deepened to 0.9% q/q sa (Q4 23: -0.7%, 2023 average: +0.9). Although the continued recovery in exports may partly ofset drags from weak domestic consumption and a muted property market, uncertainties remain from the risk of further tarif escalation between China and major economies<sup>10</sup> . Delayed Fed rate cuts may also mean Hong Kong’s funding costs could stay high for longer. We maintain our forecast that Hong Kong’s GDP growth will slow to 2.5% in 2024, from 3.2% in 2023, amid a weak outlook for consumption, the property market and external sectors in the near term. An ageing population, spillovers from the secular growth slowdown in China, and elevated corporate debt levels, which is constraining private investment, are adding to the pressures on Hong Kong’s growth outlook in the medium term.

On consumption, retail sales plunged by 16.5% y/y in April, almost double the 8.7% drop in March. We think deteriorating household balance sheets amid falling home prices and a weak stock market will continue to weigh on local demand. Changing consumption pattens in both residents and visitors also pose further challenges. On the one hand, Hong Kong residents are flocking to neighbouring cities such as Shenzhen due to the availability of high-quality services for lower prices amid a stronger HKD. In particular, a record of 9.3mn departures from the city were made in March, which marked the single-highest monthly number since 1997. The outflows extended to the four-day Easter holiday, during which departures for mainland China and Macau rose by 10% over the same period in 2019, while arrivals to the city from the mainland and Macau declined by 46% from 2019 levels<sup>11</sup> . On the other hand, visitors are not

spending at their pre-COVID levels. While visitor arrivals rose to 11.2mn in Q1 24, or 72% of the level in the same period in 2018, visitor spending, as measured by exports of travel services, recovered to only 53% of the 2018 level. Meanwhile, latest data showed that sales of discretionary goods such as jewellery, watches and valuable gifts fell \~28.7% y/y in April, following a 17% drop in March.

In the property market, boosts from the latest property easing measures – where authorities scrapped all decade-old property cooling measures on 28 February and released lending curbs – are likely to be short-lived. We did see an initial positive response – home prices picked up by 2% in April over February, and residential transaction volumes surged to 8,551 units in April, more than 2.5 times the February sales level. However, weakness has re-emerged, with both new home and secondary home sales falling in May (new homes: -46% m/m, secondary homes: -27% m/m). The Centa-City Leading Index, which measures secondary private residential property prices in Hong Kong, also showed that home prices have reversed to falls in late May. On balance, we remain cautious on the potential for a sustained rebound in the housing market in view of elevated mortgage rates and subdued labour markets, especially in the financial sector. Major banks in Hong Kong, including HSBC, Hang Seng Bank, Bank of China (HK), Standard Chartered and Citibank all kept their rates unchanged following the Fed's June decision<sup>12</sup> . Indeed, the average mortgage rate in Hong Kong was 4.16% in March, more than double the 1.72% of two years ago<sup>13</sup> . A delayed Fed rate cut also suggests that Hong Kong’s mortgage rates will stay high for longer.

On the external sector, growth in exports accelerated in April, partly on a low comparison base. Relative resilient demand from DMs and increasing exports to Asean countries are likely to support Hong Kong’s exports. That said, we think challenges persist and downside risks could come from any potential tarif escalation between China and the US/EU.

On capital markets, while we see continued capital outflow pressures in Hong Kong amid the bear market in equities and the housing downturn, the AUM of Hong Kong's asset and wealth management businesses remain robust, picking up by 20%, to HKD30.5trn, in 2022 from 2019. Meanwhile, deposits in Hong Kong’s banking system continued to increase, rising 2% in April from end-2023, with USD deposits rising 1.5% during that period. Notably, along with intensified capital outflows from the mainland, we note CNY deposits in Hong Kong surged 16% in April from December, to \~CNY1.1trn, with 60% of it in the form of time deposits.

## India: Elections done, focus back to goldilocks

Shreya Sodhani, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Amruta Ghare, BSIPL, India | Lemon Zhang, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Mitul Kotecha, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Audrey Ong, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Avanti Save, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Yimin Weng, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Imtiaz Shefuddin, Barclays Bank, Singapore

We expect the Indian economy to see more balanced growth this year, supported by a pickup in private consumption, as well as a recovery in private investment. With gradual disinflation in retail prices and a strong growth outlook, we expect the RBI to be comfortable keeping policy rates unchanged for longer, lowering rates only end of the year. Political uncertainty is likely to fade following election results and the composition of the new cabinet, which suggests policy continuity.

• Credit: We rate the HG India sector at Market Weight given our expectations of stable technicals in view of likely continued muted supply in 2H24 and current valuations, which we think leave limited scope for compression. We have an Overweight on the HY India corporates, as we expect the sector to continue benefit from the positive momentum in the broader economy. We do not see supply as a meaningful risk, even if any pickup, as we think issuers are likely to remain disciplined.

• FX: We expect USDINR to stay in a narrow 83.0-83.6 range over much of the forecast horizon, with the RBI likely to continue tightly managing the currency pair, while continuing to absorb inflows, building its FX reserves. Improving external metrics, with a manageable current account deficit and favorable basic balance should prove supportive.  As such, we expect INR outperformance amid likely further USD strength.

• Rates: We expect IGBs yields to move lower, albeit at a measured pace over the next 12 months, with bonds supported by a relatively stable currency, fiscal consolidation, passive index buying, and declining inflation. The sizeable RBI dividend and stable IGB borrowing calendar bode well too. We look for 10y IGB yields to reach 6.9% by end-Q3 24 and to head gradually lower, to 6.7%, by end-Q2 25.

We expect India's GDP growth to remain strong in FY2024-25 at 7%, with the growth momentum continuing into FY2025-26. Within domestic demand, we anticipate a revival in private consumption in FY2024-25, led by resilient urban demand, with rural consumption also likely to recover, as inflation moderates further and assuming a normal monsoon. Within investment, we think the pace of public capex is likely to slow (as already indicated through the interim budget). Private investment is showing incipient signs of picking up, though it may take longer before this trend becomes broad-based, as businesses await a revival in consumption, and signs of stability from the new coalition government's policy choices. The expected recovery in global trade volumes should also support India's exports. Overall, we expect growth to moderate in FY24-25 from FY23-24's blistering 8.2%, and for it to be more balanced. Downside risks to growth may stem from rises in commodity prices, longer-thanexpected tightness in financial conditions, and a more prolonged wait for private investment recovery.

We reduce our CPI inflation forecast to 4.3% in FY2024-25 (earlier: 4.5%), slowing from 5.4% in FY2023-24. The pace of disinflation remains uneven, with the headline rate above the RBI's medium-term target, due to persistently elevated food prices: food inflation remains close to 8%, driven by both perishables (vegetables, fruits, eggs) and non-perishables (pulses, coarse cereals). Headline CPI inflation has been decelerating modestly (to 4.75% y/y  in May, from 4.8% in April) led largely by deflation in fuel CPI and disinflation in core CPI. Despite the only moderate slowdown in headline rates, inflation is likely to undershoot the RBI's Q1 FY24-25

forecast and likely the Q2 forecast as well with prices benefiting from a normal monsoon — which should push down food prices — base efects and the residual efects of monetary tightening. For FY2025-26, we expect CPI inflation to average 5.2%.

Monetary policy  likely on pause until December 2024. The MPC remains focused on food inflation, which has prevented headline from aligning durably with its medium-term target of 4%. We think the central bank will likely want to see the progress of this year's monsoon to gain confidence on the outlook for food inflation, and its impact on inflation expectations. The MPC remains confident on growth, with the RBI raising its GDP growth forecast for FY2024-25 to 7.2%. This, in our view, implies that a majority of the MPC sees no urgency to cut rates. By Q3 FY4-25, we expect headline inflation to begin aligning with the target in a more sustainable manner (excluding the base-efect led slowdown in Q2), thus opening the window for a rate cut in December 2024. We expect a shallower rate cut cycle this time, with a total of 50bp in rate cuts (in December 2024 and Q2 2025), with the risk of further delay in easing if the expected inflation trajectory reverses, and growth remains strong. Uncertainty on the policy path may increase as the term of external MPC members expires by Q3 CY2024, while the term of Governor Das and Deputy Governor Patra are set to expire in December and January, respectively.

Focus now turns towards the upcoming final budget (likely in July). Most ministers in the newly formed cabinet were essentially reappointed to their existing portfolios. This, to us, indicates that policy continuity can be expected from the new government. Specifically for fiscal policy, we think the government's fiscal consolidation agenda remains on track. One, a higher RBI dividend has created more space to increase spending, without compromising on the interim fiscal deficit target of 5.1% of GDP. Two, while revenue expenditure may see an increase in the final budget (likely targeted towards rural sector schemes), we expect the government's policy focus to remain on infrastructure buildout and supporting manufacturing.

Along with a positive fiscal profile, the other plank of macro stability — the external sector — shows a solid position with benign current account financing requirements and net positive trends in capital inflows. Falling commodity prices drove the lowering of merchandise imports in FY2023-24, narrowing the trade deficit, even though export values were lower y/y owing to weak external demand. Robust services exports and a continued upward trend in remittances provided further cushion. We thus revise our current account deficit forecast downwards to USD27bn (0.8% of GDP) for FY23-24. Into FY2024-25, we expect the current account deficit to increase to USD 40.9bn, 1% of GDP, but remain manageable: import values may rise driven by an expected pickup in private investment, and higher commodity prices (for energy and metals). Still, the inclusion of India's government bonds into JP Morgan's global bond index for emerging markets from June 2024 bodes favourably for the financial account position, which should comfortably finance the current account deficit.

# Indonesia: In a Fed, Fed, Fed, Fed world

Brian Tan, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Mitul Kotecha, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Audrey Ong, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Avanti Save, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Yimin Weng, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Sarah Beh, Barclays Bank, Singapore

The pre-pandemic trend for GDP growth to hew close to 5% y/y remains in place while CPI inflation remains benign. The IDR, not the CPI, is likely the bigger factor for monetary policy at the moment however. While our base case is for Bank Indonesia (BI) to stay on hold through the rest of this year, we remain on alert for further rate hikes. The risk of another rate hike is relatively high – we believe BI will do what it takes to cement its credibility when it comes to defending the currency, as it did in April. Our base case remains for an eventual return to BI's end-2019 level of 5.00%, starting in Q1 25, but the risks around this are admittedly substantial.

• Credit: We retain our Market Weight rating on the sovereign, in view of President-elect Prabowo's tolerance for a looser fiscal policy. In our view, this could raise concerns on bond supply and disrupt the robust supply technical in the bond market. In this context, market participants are likely to be watchful of the upcoming budget. Furthermore, should the administration take steps to implement looser fiscal targets, rating agencies could take a cautious tone and stall the rating upgrade trajectory. In light of Prabowo’s comments about fiscal policy and government debt, we remain watchful of the government’s stance towards natural-resource downstreaming and the subsidy/compensation mechanism for SOEs. Previously, we noted that low-coupon bonds within the Indonesia sovereign and SOE complex have lagged their higher-coupon counterparts, and think there may be opportunities to switch into low-coupon bonds without giving up material yield.

• FX: We expect USDIDR to grind to a higher range over the next year. This reflects pressures from a firmer USD for longer, broad-based Asia FX underperformance led by CNY, JPY and still-elevated domestic USD demand. We think BI is unlikely to be averse to allowing IDR adjustments, as long as the cheapening is broadly in line with regional currencies.

• Rates: We expect IndoGBs to cheapen in the short term, with 10y yields likely edging higher to 7.25-7.50% by Q3. Term premia relative to US rates still appear relatively tight given stillbearish global drivers, and especially if investors move towards pricing in more fiscal risk premia. Further out, we expect the BI pivot to easing in Q1 25 and a supportive macro outlook to trigger a move lower in IndoGB yields; we see 10y yields easing to 6.75-7.00% by Q2 25.

We continue to expect GDP growth to remain at 5.0% in 2024 and 2025 – in the upper half of Bank Indonesia's (BI) 4.7-5.5% forecast range for this year – similar to 2023. GDP growth edged up to 5.1% y/y in Q1 24 from 5.0% in Q4 23, in line with our view that the GDP indicators have resumed the pre-pandemic trend for economic growth to hew close to 5% y/y. Before COVID-19 lockdowns and social distancing that resulted in visible swings in economic activity, quarterly GDP growth in the pre-pandemic era was unusually stable, ranging between 4.7% and 5.3% y/y, and averaging 5.0% y/y through 2014-19. This pattern appears to have resumed, with all six quarterly GDP prints so far starting from Q4 22 hovering around 5.0% y/y.

Likewise, our forecast remains for the current account deficit to widen only modestly from 0.1% of GDP in 2023 to 0.5% in 2024 and 0.8% in 2025. BI's 2024 forecast is for a deficit equivalent to 0.1-0.9% of GDP. In line with our projections, the current account deficit widened only slightly to 0.6% of GDP in Q1 24 from 0.3% in Q4 23. From a price perspective, our Comodo indicators – which aim to quantify the net terms-of-trade efects of global commodity price developments – have not only stabilised following their deterioration in the earlier part of 2023 but are drifting higher, implying more positive efects for Indonesia. Still, we expect a recovery in imports into 2024-25 as domestic demand continues to pick up. Nonetheless, our forecast of small current account deficits in 2023-25 would represent a relatively benign outcome, in our view, compared with the 2012-19 period, which saw quarterly deficits average 2.5% of GDP.

In terms of the CPI, we continue to expect headline CPI inflation to cool from 3.7% in 2023 to 2.8% in 2024 – still in the upper-half of BI's 1.5-3.5% target range – and to 2.4% in 2025. Volatile food price inflation has moderated to 8.1% y/y as of May, following a 10.3% peak in March – by contrast, core inflation has crept up to 1.9% y/y in May from 1.7% in February, though this should still average a relatively benign 2.1% this year. Notably, the latest unemployment rate of 4.82% nsa as of February 2024 has finally fallen (slightly) below its February 2020 level of 4.94% – lagging most other Emerging Asian economies, where jobless rates quickly slipped below pre-COVID levels as economies reopened after the pandemic. This is in line with our estimates suggesting the output gap may have turned positive in Q1 24, which may support firms' ability to pass on cost pressures to consumers, fuelling a gradual pickup in core inflation.

The IDR, not the CPI, is likely the bigger factor for monetary policy at the moment, however – while our base case is for BI to stay on hold through the rest of this year, we remain on alert for further rate hikes, especially as the outlook will likely remain challenging for the currency. As we had expected – though this was a surprise to markets – the central bank hiked its policy rate by 25bp in April to defend to currency. Our view is that if the currency were to remain relatively stable at its April levels or even appreciate, the central bank would likely be happy to conserve its firepower. That said, the risk of another rate hike is relatively high – we believe BI will do what it takes to cement its credibility when it comes to defending the currency, as it did in April. Historically, when IDR depreciation has been the main reason for tightening, BI has tended to stop raising the policy rate once pressure on the IDR starts to ease, but can quickly shift back to rate hikes if depreciation resumes or the merchandise trade balance deteriorates (see Indonesia: BI – Maestros of the policy mix, 7 May 2024).

Our base case remains for an eventual return to BI's end-2019 level of 5.00%, but the risks around this are admittedly substantial. We continue to expect BI to only start cutting its policy rate in Q1 25, by 25bp per quarter. Historically, the central bank has been able to shift to easing mode quite quickly once currency pressures have abated. Should our US economists' Fed view of only one 25bp cut in 2024 materialise – whether cautiously in September as per their base case or, with an almost equal likelihood, delayed to December – we believe BI is more likely to stay patient and proceed with care, not chasing the initial Fed cut and waiting for more certainty that this is translating into sustainable strength for the IDR (see Indonesia: BI rate – Conserving firepower, 22 May 2024).

On the fiscal front, our budget deficit forecasts remain at 1.9% of GDP for 2024 and 2.2% for 2025. The latest figures indicate the government has run another small fiscal surplus in January-April 2024, which points to yet another year of fiscal outperformance versus the budget target of 2.29% of GDP, in our view. That said, the January-April 2024 surplus of IDR67.6trn is smaller than January-April 2023's IDR106.2trn, which suggests the full-year fiscal deficit will likely widen in 2024 versus last year's 1.66% of GDP. For 2025, the Ministry of Finance has proposed to widen the deficit to 2.45-2.80% of GDP. Our 2025 fiscal deficit projection takes into account some additional spending for Prabowo Subianto – who will be inaugurated as Indonesia's next president on 20 October – to implement his proposed free school meal program.

Media reports that President-elect Prabowo Subianto may be aiming to increase government debt to 50% of GDP by the end of his five-year term from an estimated 39.5% as of April 2024 are raising fears over the fiscal outlook under the next administration.14 Our estimates suggest that in order for government debt to reach 50% of GDP by 2029, the fiscal deficit would have to surge multi-fold, to 4-6% of GDP in 2025-29 from 1.6% as of 2023. However, it is unclear to us that Mr Prabowo would have suficient support in the legislature to modify the fiscal-deficit ceiling – even if this has always been on his agenda. Notably, Mr Prabowo's nephew Thomas Djiwandono – who is reportedly in charge of the transition in relation with the Ministry of Finance – has oficially denied that there are any government debt targets.<sup>15</sup> This is in line with our view that while the next administration could remain keen on embarking on grand adventures in fiscal policy, a likely adverse reaction in the IDR – as was already seen in the depreciation of the currency in the immediate aftermath of these media reports – could ultimately still dissuade policymakers from undertaking such a path (see Indonesia: Grand adventures in fiscal policy, 14 June 2024).

# Korea: Necessary conditions for the BoK cut is looming

Bum Ki Son, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Mitul Kotecha, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Lemon Zhang, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Avanti Save, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Yimin Weng, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Jit Ming Tan, Barclays Bank, Singapore

We maintain our above-consensus growth forecast for 2024 at 2.7%, mainly driven by exports amid slowing domestic demand. For domestic demand, we see downside risks from the acceleration in the restructuring of real estate project-financing loans and subsequent deleveraging, which could lead to reduced money supply velocity. Without some demand-boosting measures, we believe there could be stagnation risks, as reduced loan supply meets weak demand, especially in the commercial real estate sector. With underlying price pressure also softening, we believe the BoK can start a gradual cutting cycle in October.

• Credit: While expect corporate fundamentals to be largely stable, we rate the Korea HG corporate sector at Underweight in consideration of tight valuations. At segment level, we favour AI-related credits in view of AI strength over EV-related credits given expectations of a modest earnings given amid slower sales growth. We think financial sector risks have been largely priced-in on expectations that the government will be able to engineer a 'soft landing' for the troubled real estate project financing sector.

• FX: We see sustained pressure on the KRW from external factors and Korea's rising export similarity to China. NPS outflows ofset significant equity inflows. The trade recovery will likely extended into H2, but exporters may not be in a rush to convert their revenues. A likely WGBI index inclusion in September could create positive spillovers to KRW assets. We recently initiated to short THBKRW 3m NDFs.

• Rates: We expect KTB yields/IRS rates to continue to ease towards levels where they started the year. The curve should further steepen, driven by short-tenor yields. Receive positioning is less crowded among foreign investors as most positions are legacy. We see strong domestic retail demand to continue. We believe inflation risk premia should fall, and expect the BoK to ease significantly earlier than market pricing, triggering a move lower in short-tenor IRS.

We maintain our above-consensus 2024 GDP growth forecast at 2.7%, up from 1.4% in 2023. Following the upside surprise in Q1 GDP growth, we raised our growth forecast by 70bp. Our view remains the same: a solid export recovery, driven by the high-end semiconductors and some spillover to the broad memory products into H2. If anything, we believe solid export volume growth for tech sector is likely to sustain into early 2025, as we see better demand for smartphones, PCs, and traditional servers. However, we believe there will be some payback in Q2 24 as the strength in domestic demand will dificult to sustain, in our view.

We attribute some of the strength in domestic demand in Q1 24 to a number of one-of factors. Final consumption was held up by better weather, the earlier launch of new smartphone models, and potentially a boost from election-related spending. Construction activities accelerated in Q1 as colder-than-average weather in Q4 23 delayed completions into Q1 24.Despite steady domestic demand, imports were weaker than expected, partly dragged by delay in airplane imports. We believe these one-of factors will likely reverse, bringing growth down to below trend, to around 0.3% q/q sa, for the rest of the year.

We believe the key issue for domestic demand is the construction sector. We think there could be an acceleration in the restructuring of real estate development project-financing loans into H2 24. We expect limited systemic risk from the restructuring, as the loan exposures

amount to around 10% of the GDP. That said, losses are likely to be concentrated in nonbanks, and we expect reduced money supply velocity as nonbanks strengthen asset quality and reduce lending to the construction and property sector sectors. We believe this could risk failures of commercial real estate loans, which have an outstanding loan volume of around 23% of GDP. We see a risk of a vicious cycle of weaker loan supply and price corrections that could result in longer-term stagnation in the sector.

We maintain our view for strong current account surplus in 2024, on a steady recovery in exports. We upgrade our current account  surplus forecast to USD68bn from USD63bn, given the wider trade surplus. We believe tech exports are likely to hold up into H2 on steady volume growth. This should bring to exports back to USD690bn for 2024, close to the high of USD694bn in 2022. Assuming energy prices remain relatively stable level from here, we believe imports are likely to remain soft, at USD612bn, in 2024. We expect the services deficit to narrow modestly as Chinese tourism continues to recover. Our view remains that Korea continues to accumulate net foreign assets at a rate of 4-5% of GDP per year, slightly more than the size of current account surplus.

On capital flows, Korea is likely to face significant changes in portfolio flows. The Nationa Pension Services (NPS) outflow accounted for around half of the net foreign asset accumulation, and the other half comprised retail and other institutional asset managers. Given Korea’s ageing population, NPS outflows were supposed to slow from this year, but given the pending NPS reform measures, we estimate NPS outflows will remain sizable, close to USD30bn per year, for another 5-10 years. Even as the outflow becomes larger, we also see the potential for near-term inflows. Korea's corporate value-up program (CVP) to improve shareholder returns is ongoing. Korea's inclusion in the  World Government Bond Index (WGBI) is also pending, and could be announced in September 2024 or March 2025. We think  inclusion in September is more likely, especially as Korea Treasury Bond and Monetary Stabilization Bond will be come Euroclearable from 27 June <sup>16</sup> . This means most of the prerequisite conditions for WGBI inclusion will have been met and inclusion will then be dependent on investor feedback up to September. We estimate the inclusion could generate inflows of about USD60bn from passive buying.

Given the softening trend of domestic demand, we maintain our 2024 CPI inflation forecast at 2.6%. Sluggish domestic demand means headline inflation pressure is mainly from supply factors, such as energy and agricultural prices. Core inflation will remaining lower than the headline rated, at 2.3%, in our forecast. We expect agricultural prices to start normalising from June-July prints as the harvest seasons kicks in, barring any extreme weather conditions. Our baseline assumes relatively stable oil prices, likely exerting some upward pressure on the headline for next couple of months, which also suggests that the fuel tax cut will remain in place. In light of relatively high inflation burden, we also expect Korea Electric Power and Korea Gas to hike prices only once in 2024 – in Q4 and Q3, respectively – by 5-10%.

All in all, the combination of softening domestic demand growth and slowing inflation dynamics should open room for the Bank of Korea to cut rates in October. We believe conditions for the rate cut is coming into a shape as: 1) inflation pressure is softening; 2) domestic growth is slowing; 3) financial conditions are tightening in some sectors; and 4) our US economists expect the Fed to start its cutting rates in September. With the BoK governor including discussing the tradeof between earlier cuts and later cuts in his opening remarks at the May MPC meeting, we believe the BoK is weighing between earlier but shallow cuts and later but deeper cuts. With concerns about growth mounting and larger current account surplus ofering some bufer for KRW, we believe the BoK is likely to tilt toward earlier but shallow cuts instead of later but deeper cuts. As such, we expect the BoK's cutting cycle to be relatively gradual, with cuts in October 2024, February 2025 and July 2025.

## Malaysia: The RON95 rondo

Brian Tan, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Mitul Kotecha, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Audrey Ong, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Avanti Save, Barclays Bank, Singapore

Early signs of a turnaround in Malaysia's tech exports, while yet to be reflected in the industrial production data, imply significant scope for a catch-up in economic activity once it materialises. While the government finally implemented its targeted diesel subsidy program on 10 June, the inflation impact should be relatively limited. The lifting of the diesel price will likely put Bank Negara Malaysia (BNM) on alert for signs for broader second-round inflation pressures. However, while we acknowledge the risk that a 25bp policy rate hike does materialise, our base case remains for BNM to stay on hold through 2025. An adjustment to RON95 petrol prices would likely have much more significant efects on broader price pressures – but our base case remains that political challenges remain substantial on this front.

• Credit: We downgrade our rating on the Malaysia sovereign to Market Weight from Overweight, as valuations are now in-line with its peers. We are comfortable with the fiscal position and expect a stable to improving external position. We note that YTD, the SOE complex has outperformed the sovereign.

• FX: We expect USDMYR to edge higher over the next year. As one of the lower-yielding FX in the region, the MYR's relatively high beta to the CNY on a depreciating path and global drivers should keep domestic USD demand firm into H2. Although moral suasion eforts to encourage GLICs/GLCs' repatriation of overseas proceeds have had a credible signalling efect on the MYR, we would note that the "consistency" of such inflows is likely contingent on the global environment too.

• Rates: We expect MGS yields/IRS rates to consolidate in Q3, followed by measured easing over a one-year horizon. We forecast 10y MGS yields at c.3.9% in Q3, before moving lower to 3.7% by Q2 25. Short-term upside risks on yields stem mainly from global drivers and a weaker MYR, especially if markets shift to price in higher funding rates and tighter liquidity conditions for longer.

With GDP growth rebounding to 4.2% y/y in Q1 24 from 2.9% in Q4 23, we forecast GDP growth forecast rising from 3.6% in 2023 to 4.3% in 2024 – which remains in the lower half of Bank Negara Malaysia's (BNM) 4.0-5.0% forecast range – and raise our 2025 forecast to 4.7% from 4.5%. While our forecast of a global economic slowdown this year implies downward pressures on external demand, we continue to expect this to be ofset by a gradual tech upcycle. On this front – beyond solid start to the year as indicated by the Q1 GDP figures – we note early signs of a turnaround in Malaysia's tech exports, following a long and deep decline since the start of 2023. While this has yet to be reflected in the industrial production data, it implies significant scope for a catch-up in economic activity once it materialises, which we expect to provide a visible boost to GDP growth.

In terms of the external balances, we also raise our 2024 current account surplus forecast to 2.8% of GDP from 2.2% earlier; our 2025 estimate of 2.5% remains unchanged. While we had expected net outflows in the primary income balance – which had hit the Q4 23 current account hard – to normalise in Q1 24, the slowdown was more visible than we had expected, contributing to a sharp widening of the current account surplus to 3.5% of GDP in Q1 from 0.2% in Q4. In particular, the detailed breakdown shows a jump in investment income receipts – possibly reflecting the repatriation of investment income by government-linked entities to support the MYR. While we doubt such active repatriation can be sustained over the medium term, it can certainly carry on this year and we have boosted our current account projections on account of this. BNM likely views such active repatriation as a more market-friendly strategy for supporting the MYR than administrative measures – such as requirements to repatriate export proceeds – which we continue to believe are unlikely under the current Governor Abdul Rasheed Ghafour.

Despite the floating of diesel prices in Peninsular Malaysia, we continue to expect CPI inflation to cool from 2.5% in 2023 to a relatively benign 2.1% in 2024 and 2025 – near the bottom of BNM's 2.0-3.5% forecast range. In terms of core inflation, our projections imply a drop from 3.0% in 2023 to 2.0% in 2024 and 2.2% in 2025 on subsiding pressures from postpandemic supply constraints. While the government finally implemented its targeted diesel subsidy program on 10 June – which resulted in a jump to MYR3.35/litre from MYR2.15/litre in Peninsular Malaysia on that day – our regression analysis suggests that the inflation impact of a diesel price hike should be relatively limited, with a maximum direct impact of just 0.1 percentage points (see Malaysia: Diesel delivered, 10 June 2024).

The next step would ostensibly be an adjustment in RON95 petrol prices – however, our base case remains that the government faces more significant political challenges on this front. The government's decision to leave the retail price of diesel unchanged in the federal territory of Labuan and the states of Sabah and Sarawak in East Malaysia is also particularly notable, in our view. Prime Minister Anwar Ibrahim had recently noted that "in Sabah and Sarawak, almost all families use diesel so we have decided to postpone its implementation as it will be a burden for the people." Considering how widespread is the use of RON95 petrol, such considerations could set a high bar for RON95 price adjustments.<sup>17</sup> As we have been highlighting, the price of RON97 petrol – which is oficially unsubsidised and supposed to be under a managed float – has been maintained at MYR3.47/litre since 21 September 2023 despite fluctuations in global crude oil prices.

In terms of monetary policy, while we acknowledge the risk that a 25bp policy rate hike does materialise, our base case remains for BNM to stay on hold through 2025. The lifting of the diesel price will likely put BNM on alert for signs for broader second-round inflation pressures. However, the maintenance of subsidised diesel prices for selected industries should help to mitigate the second-round inflation pressures arising from the lifting of diesel prices. An adjustment to RON95 petrol prices would likely have much more significant efects on broader price pressures – but, as noted above, our base case remains that political challenges remain substantial on this front.

The near-term savings from the targeted subsidy program's implementation are likely limited – still, we continue to view the government's target of reducing the full-year fiscal deficit to 4.3% of GDP in 2024 from 5.0% in 2023 as achievable. Prime Minister Anwar Ibrahim, who is also the finance minister, recently stated that the move will save MYR4bn in terms of government subsidy spending,<sup>18</sup> which we calculate to be equivalent to about 0.2% of GDP. The press release from the Ministry of Finance noted that diesel subsidies cost the government MYR14.3bn in 2023 (0.7% of GDP), which implies about MYR10bn will still be spent on cash transfers and maintaining lower prices for selected industries. Given the timing of the targeted subsidy program's implementation, the government may only be able to achieve savings of around MYR2bn this year. Notably, the government has already run a fiscal deficit equivalent to 5.7% of GDP in Q1 24, widening versus Q1 23's 3.8% and on par with Q1 22's 5.6%. That said, we believe the government will be able to tap on the potential for the Petronas dividend to be increased in the event of fiscal slippage (see Malaysia: 2024 Budget – Rhetoric and reality, 13 October 2023).

## Philippines: Hawks no more

Shreya Sodhani, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Mitul Kotecha, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Audrey Ong, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Avanti Save, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Sarah Beh, Barclays Bank, Singapore

Improving inflation trends have allowed the central bank to take a dovish turn and focus more on growth, highlighting that a Q3 rate cut is possible. However, the possibility of headline inflation remaining elevated until July and pressure from a still hawkish Fed, despite improving external metrics, suggest to us that the bank is more likely to start easing from October.

• Credit: We maintain our rating on the sovereign at Underweight in spite of our expectation of better external metrics such as an improving current account balance and fiscal account position in 2024. Current valuations look tight, with the PHILIP bonds currently trading at levels close to INDON bonds, when in our view they should trade firmly wider to the Indonesian sovereign.

• FX: We think USDPHP can test above the psychological level of 59 in the short term, moving to a higher 59-60 range over the next year. The prospect of the BSP pivoting to easing ahead of the Fed should weigh on PHP sentiment, as carry could provide less of a bufer for the PHP in H2. We think the BSP will continue with its modest interventions in the short term, as conditions for a more-aggressive approach are not yet there.

• Rates: We expect RPGB yields to consolidate in the short term, with 10y RPGBs at 6.75% in Q3 24, before easing to 6.00% by Q2 25. We expect RPGB yields to fall in H2, reflecting policy easing expectations and a more-supportive domestic backdrop. Curves should flatten as a likely cautious BSP – pivoting after the Fed in our base case – lowers inflation risk premia.

Bangko Sentral ng Pilipinas (BSP) is emerging as the EM Asia central bank most likely to pivot first, in October in our view, with a risk of a cut coming as soon as August, as the bank's previously hawkish communiques have been turning incrementally dovish. For the past two meetings in Q2, the BSP has stated that it is "somewhat less hawkish than before", even to the extent of flagging that it would be open to easing in August 2024. This is despite the bank noting that risks to inflation are biased towards the upside; indeed, the bank did raise its baseline and risk adjusted inflation forecast in April (reducing it in May), noting at the same time that a cut in Q3 could be a possibility.

communication. While inflation was expected to breach the BSP's 2-4% target range in Q2, April-May inflation actually averaged 3.85%, with the likelihood of June inflation going above 4% also low. Indeed, lower than expected April inflation, with mainly higher rice prices leading headline higher, has made the bank "more comfortable" that inflation in 2024 will be within the target range, as underlying price pressures seem to be softening. We now expect inflation to average 3.4% in 2024, down from 3.7%. The BSP's baseline forecast has also been lowered to 3.5%, and its inflation adjusted forecast reduced to 3.8%, from a peak of 4.7% in October 2023 when it delivered an of-cycle hike. Additionally, the recent cut in tarifs for rice, from 35% to 15%, is likely to create material downward pressure on headline inflation<sup>19</sup> .

With inflation easing, we think the bank is shifting focus to growth, which slowed sequentially in Q1 as the impact of the BSP's aggressive hiking cycle became more evident in the clear slowdown in private consumption and private investment.  Lending rates have also increased almost in line with the BSP's hikes, leading to some stabilisation in loan growth recently. We lowered our 2024 GDP growth forecast to 5.6% from 5.8% on the back of the Q1 print, expecting even more pain from tight policy — the central bank has acknowledged that growth may moderate in H2 as monetary policy bites.

That said, the BSP has noted that the growth path "remains intact", with Q1 growth of 5.7% being a "very respectable number". In addition, inflation, based on the bank's forecast trajectory as of May, was expected to breach 4% from May-July (May inflation printed 3.9% eventually). With the inflation outlook still to the upside, we continue to think that an August rate cut would be too soon, especially as the bank still thinks inflation could breach 4% up to July and is currently not sounding the alarm on growth.

The bank's approach of pressing ahead with a dovish commentary, despite the Federal Reserve remaining hawkish, may have been supported by improving external 6 6

balances. With elevated services exports and still relatively low goods imports, and a much smaller-than-expected deficit in Q1, we have lowered our current account deficit forecast to USD5.5bn for 2024 (1.2% of GDP), from USD9.7bn earlier. A BoP surplus for a second year running should help the currency, though the dovish turn by the bank has recently led to increased pressure, aided by Governor Remolona highlighting that the bank's interventions in the FX market are "very modest". Finance Secretary Recto noted on 13 June that the BSP would consider the spread over Fed rates and would lower rates after the Fed this year<sup>20</sup> . On the other hand, Monetary Board member Diokno noted on 11 June that BSP could lower rates before the Fed<sup>21</sup> . Looking at the BSP's response to the Fed over the past two years, and the possibility of inflation remaining elevated until July, we think October is a more likely time for the bank to lower rates.

However, we acknowledge that the risk of a cut at the August meeting (15 August) remains high and June-July inflation, along with Q2 GDP, will determine which way the bank goes. Our forecast trajectory shows Q2 growth (8 August) being higher than Q1; while we expect June inflation (5 July) to be below 4%, we expect July inflation (6 August) to breach the target. Our forecasts thus suggest that an October start to the easing cycle remains more likely. We continue to expect the bank to lower rates by 125bp when it does start easing, with two 25bp rate cuts in 2024.

## Singapore: Entering election watch

Brian Tan, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Mitul Kotecha, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Audrey Ong, Barclays Bank, Singapore

Favourable base and carryover efects, as well as a gradual semiconductor upcycle, should lift GDP growth this year. With core inflation likely to remain elevated, we maintain our view that the likelihood of FX policy easing this year is lower than most market participants seem to think and continue to expect the Monetary Authority of Singapore (MAS) to stand pat through 2025. With Lawrence Wong succeeding Lee Hsien Loong as Singapore's prime minister in May, speculation is rife over the timing of the next general election, which must be held by November 2025. While we note the risk of an early election, our base case is for polls to only be called next year.

• FX: We expect the SGD NEER to trade c.150bp above the mid-point of the policy band until the MAS July review, before easing to c.100-125bp above mid by the end of 2024. USDSGD is likely to edge higher over a one-year horizon, reflecting a firm USD for longer and regional headwinds from USDCNY settling in a higher 7.30-7.35 range.

• Rates: We expect SGS yields/SORA OIS rates to consolidate in Q3, before moving measurably lower later. Continued US exceptionalism implies that the risk of a further delay to Fed easing remains a headwind in the short term. SORA OIS should continue to enjoy relative outperformance versus SOFR in Q3 until the Fed pivots to easing, with overnight SORA fixings remaining in a 3.4-3.8% range. We maintain our pay 2y SORA OIS versus receive 2y SGS trade.

With GDP growth recovering to 2.7% y/y in Q1 24, from 2.2% in Q4 23, we continue to forecast full-year GDP growth forecast of 2.5% – still in the upper half of the oficial growth forecast range of 1.0-3.0% – but raise our 2025 estimate to 2.5% from 2.1%. This would represent a rebound from 1.1% in 2023, partly due to favourable base and carryover efects, which we estimate will lift growth in 2024 by about 0.4 percentage points (pp). While our forecast of a global economic slowdown this year implies downward pressures on external demand, we continue to expect these to be ofset by a gradual tech upcycle, which should lift not only shipments of semiconductors, but also specialised machinery for the manufacture of semiconductors and other tech products. There are signs that tech exports are bottoming out, though this has yet to be solidly reflected in the industrial production data. A continued revival in tech demand should imply better growth outcomes in 2025 as well, supporting our upgrade.

While the labour market remains tight overall, the unemployment rate is creeping higher and we maintain our forecast for core inflation to moderate from 4.1% in 2023 to 3.0% in 2024 – in the middle of the Monetary Authority of Singapore's (MAS) 2.5-3.5% forecast range – and to 2.0% in 2025. Following the second hike in the Goods and Services Tax (GST) rate to 9% from 8% at the start of this year, core inflation has settled at 3.1% y/y as of April and will likely stay around these levels up to September before falling in Q4 to an average of 2.6% on base efects. The efect of this administered price adjustment will drop out in January next year, which is when we expect core inflation to shift down and average 2.0% in 2025 – above its 2010-19 average of 1.5% – partly as a result of firms being less hesitant to pass on higher costs, including from other administered price increases, to consumers. As for headline inflation, we have adjusted our forecasts to reflect recent dynamics in car certificate of entitlement (COE) quota premiums: our 2024 forecast is up slightly to 2.3% from 2.2% previously – still below the MAS forecast forecast range of 2.5-3.5% – but our 2025 estimate is down sharply to 1.0% from 1.7% on our expectation that more visible declines will occur only next year.

With GDP growth picking up and core inflation remaining elevated, FX policy settings still do not look particularly restrictive and we continue to expect the MAS to stand pat

through 2025. We maintain our view that the likelihood of FX policy easing this year is lower than most market participants seem to think – indeed, the probability of a slight tightening in the form of a 50bp slope increase (from 1.5% currently) seems relatively higher to us, though this remains low overall. In our view, the argument that FX policy settings will be eased just because the US Federal Reserve will cut its policy rate is overly simplistic – there have been episodes when the MAS deviated from the Fed. Given the need to manage inflation expectations, we continue to see little reason for the MAS to relax its hawkish tone. We doubt the MAS will contemplate easing before core inflation rates come down much closer to 2%, by which time the central bank would likely assess the output gap to have turned more solidly positive, obviating the need for easing (see Singapore: MAS Macroeconomic Review – More clearly hawkish, 26 April 2024).

With Lawrence Wong succeeding Lee Hsien Loong as Singapore's prime minister in May, speculation is rife over the timing of the next general elections, which must be held by November 2025<sup>22</sup> . While we note the risk of an early election, our base case is for polls to only be called next year, once there is more progress in bringing down residential property prices and core inflation, especially as the base efect related to the 1 January 2024 GST hike will reverse in January 2025, reducing the figures at least optically, as noted above. Even so, these cost pressures will likely remain significant issues that Mr Wong and the ruling People's Action Party (PAP) will have to address in the coming polls.

As our base case is for the general election to be held next year, we expect another overall budget deficit in FY2025, equivalent to 0.5% of GDP, as the government continues to address cost-of-living issues. The latest fiscal data suggest the government ran a marginally smaller overall budget deficit equivalent to 0.9% of GDP in FY2023, compared with its 16 February 2024 revised estimate of a deficit equivalent to 1.0% of GDP (see Singapore: 2024 budget – Hawks everywhere, 16 February 2024).

# Taiwan: Steady exports meets solid domestic demand

Bum Ki Son, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Mitul Kotecha, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Lemon Zhang, Barclays Bank, Singapore

e turn more constructive on growth dynamics and upgrade our GDP growth forecasts to 3.6% 2024 and 2.1% for 2025. We expect exports to remain solid, while domestic demand maintains around a trend level of growth. We expect inflation to gradually slow, likely turning real rates positive in H2. While we believe this opens room for the CBC to ease, the bank’s focus is likely to remain on credit growth in the property sector. Any upside surprise on there could delay or reduce the depth of the cutting cycle, in our view. Geopolitics are likely to remain tense, but we do not see increased risks of serious escalation.

• FX: We expect the TWD to stay weak, amid carry trades, bond outflows and dividend repatriation. Nonetheless, pressure may be less extreme given that 1) lifers' hedge ratios are at seven-year lows (65%), 2) the momentum of bond ETF outflows has weakened and 3) our FX fair value (FFV) model suggests the TWD is trading 4.7% cheaper than its FV. We continue to expect the TWI to consolidate in the lower half of its range and see potential for using TWD as a tactical funder.

• Rates: We expect TGB yields to move lower and TWD IRS to outperform TGBs by falling towards TGB yields. We think the IRS-TGB spreads still screen rich, but the next leg of narrowing should be volatile, given that inflation prints likely stay sticky and a lack of lifers' demand for TGBs. Moderating inflation should be supportive for FI, balancing out the pressure from growth recovery.

We turn more constructive on Taiwan's GDP growth outlook and upgrade our growth forecasts to 3.6% for 2024 (from 3.2% in the previous EM Quarterly) and 2.1% for 2025 (from 1.9%). Our 2024 forecast is slightly below Bloomberg consensus (3.7%) and Taiwan's Central Bank of the Republic of China's (CBC) forecast (3.77%). Q1 GDP surprised on the upside with strong tailwinds from exports amid softer imports, more than ofsetting the slowing in domestic demand. We believe the solid exports are likely to continue, while the contribution from the net exports likely to narrow as sequential export growth slow and import growth picks up into Q2. We believe the growth outlook for 2025 also likely turns modestly better than expected, with 2.1% growth, up from 1.9% in the previous EM Quarterly. We see initial signs of spillovers into more mature tech applications such as smartphones, PCs, and some traditional servers, likely supporting export growth into early 2025.

Looking into H2 24, we expect steady high-end chip exports on improved shipments and some turnaround in capex from the corporates. TSMC's recent guidance reafirmed strong demand for AI related applications, with improvements in its utilisation rate. We continue to expect demand for high-performance-related chips to remain strong throughout 2024, and some pickup in smartphone demand may help to keep Taiwan's exports on relatively strong footing. That said, overcapacity issues in mature chips, for example for autos, are likely to keep the broad industry recovery relatively gradual until demand starts to pick up from H2. Meanwhile, some recovery in the utilisation rate and a pickup in smartphone sales likely means capex will increase into H2, in our view. While capex guidance from Taiwanese corporates has been muted,  we believe capex likely picks up into H2 against a low year-earlier base.

While tailwinds from exports and investments remain solid, we expect relatively weak trickle down into consumption. We believe private consumption growth is likely to be soft, at around 0.5-0.7% q/q sa, for the rest of the year. Instead of a trickle down from exports, we believe consumption is likely to be broadly in line with trend growth, with some tailwinds from a recovery in inbound tourism. Relatively well-anchored wage growth pressure despite the tight labour market is also likely to keep private consumption broadly in line with trend.

With relatively tepid consumption growth, we maintain our CPI inflation forecast at 2.0% for 2024. CPI inflation remained elevated in Q1, hitting 3.08% y/y in February, which triggered a surprise rate hike from the CBC. In particular, the CBC cautioned against the well-telegraphed electricity price hike from April. In line with our view for only a modest impact from the electricity price hike, CPI inflation from April has softened to 1.94% y/y, before picking up to 2.24% in May. We expect June CPI inflation to print around 2.5%, partly due to base efects, before slowing to 1.8% in Q3 and 1.6% in Q4. We expect the normalisation in food prices and some services price pressures to be softer. With some softening in the services pressure, we expect core inflation to average 2.1% in 2024. Upside risks to our inflation forecasts include potential spillovers from a minimum wage hike and expectations of further increases in electricity prices leading to price hikes in other industrial prices.

In terms of monetary policy, we continue to expect the CBC to start cutting from Q1 25. After the March surprise hike in a bid to preemptively curb inflation expectations ahead of the electricity price hike, the CBC stayed on hold in June. With major economies' monetary policy pivoting towards an easing – as shown by the ECB's June rate cut and the Fed's dot plot suggesting a cut in 2024, the Bank of Japan is an exception – we believe the external backdrop likely ofers some easing opportunities for Taiwan, especially as the strong current account surplus provides room for the TWD in TWI terms to appreciate. Domestically, we believe the CBC's rationale for a rate cut is building gradually, given our expectations for disinflation and relatively modest domestic demand growth.

That said, we believe the easing cycle may be relatively shallow, with the CBC cutting only three times in 2025. With growth still running above trend, we believe the CBC may not be in rush to deliver a cut. Furthermore, we believe the CBC's easing will be somewhat contingent on cooling credit growth, especially to property sector. The CBC tightened macroprudential policy, as credit flow to the property sector started to accelerate from late H2 23. In addition, the CBC raised banks’ reserve requirement ratio by 25bp, absorbing  TWD120bn of inter-bank liquidity to help control credit flows to property market and curb inflation expectations. We believe this shows the CBC's concern on rising household debt and related financial stability concerns. Our baseline assumes M2 growth slows to the midpoint of reference range of 2.5-6.5% by end of the year with some slowing in credit growth, but an upside surprise could delay the first rate cut or make the cycle even shallower.

On geopolitics, we continue to expect the cross-Strait tensions to remain relatively high, but do not expect a serious escalation. We believe Taiwan's stance on China is unlikely to change much as the surveys suggest a divergence between how the Taiwanese view Taiwan and how China views Taiwan, with more Taiwanese favouring a the status quo and some degree of independence from the mainland <sup>23</sup> . President Lai Ching-te repeatedly ofered to talk with China on equal footing, but this has been rebufed by China <sup>24</sup> . China has not escalated its “grey zone” tactics, and instead increased economic pressures on Taiwan by reinstating tarifs on more than 100 items <sup>25</sup> . China also ramped up military exercises but avoided any collisions. We believe this means limited chances of serious escalation in geopolitic risks in the near term, but tensions lasting for some time.

## Thailand: Politics dampening the turn around

Shreya Sodhani, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Mitul Kotecha, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Audrey Ong, Barclays Bank, Singapore

We do not expect the BoT to embark on an easing cycle this year or the next, as reduced worries around growth and low inflation should allow the bank to maintain a focus on financial stability. A positive fiscal impulse is likely to cyclically lift growth, despite the digital wallet plan remaining uncertain. Positives for growth also include a faster tourism recovery, with receipts now exceeding expectations. However, political troubles may weigh on the full turnaround story.

• FX: We expect the THB to cheapen and underperform forwards in Q3. We have remained bearish on the THB reflecting pressures from still-negative carry, beta to a firm USD for longer and gradual CNY depreciation. Portfolio flows are likely to remain a drag on the THB, with a sustained recovery dependent on global drivers, improving political dynamics and the local macro outlook, reflecting still-weak foreign interest in low-yielding Thai assets. We recently initiated a recommendation of short THBKRW 3m NDFs.

• Rates: We expect ThaiGB yields/THOR OIS to be biased higher in the short term, before easing measurably over a one-year horizon. The higher-for-longer global duration backdrop should exert some upward pressure on ThaiGB yields. We see room for front-end Thai rates/ THOR OIS to move higher, as we think the window for BoT's pivot to easing has likely closed in Q2 with sequential growth momentum expected to pick up through year-end on increased fiscal disbursements.

The Bank of Thailand's Monetary Policy Committee has stayed on hold now for four straight meetings and is unlikely to embark on rate cuts through this year and the next. A majority of the MPC thinks the current rate is consistent with its three-pronged mandate to maintain price stability, support sustainable growth, and preserve financial stability. At the most recent meeting on 12 June, only one member voted for a cut, suggesting that the other six members think the 2.50% rate is still neutral.

This is despite growth remaining unimpressive — we expect the economy to expand by 2.8% in 2024, improving to 3.3% by 2025. Growth engines for the economy like manufacturing and goods exports have lost steam even as tourism is in a recovery stage. However, structural factors like loss of competitiveness are behind the worsening outlook for exports, with global soft demand is also weighing on exports and manufacturing. This is why the bank is hesitant to lower rates due to factors that monetary policy may not be able to address while easing would risk financial stability, an issue that the bank has been sounding the alarm on.

Apart from its renewed focus on financial stability, we think a positive fiscal impulse, which is likely to cyclically lift growth, will keep the MPC on hold through rest of the year. The delayed FY2024 budget's passing in late April, accelerated disbursements, and a likely timely FY2025 budget will lift public spending through rest of the year, at least on a y/y basis. Add to that the recovery in tourism, which should pick up steam seasonally in Q4, and the possibility of the digital wallet plan being approved should weaken the argument that rates need to be cut due to soft growth. At the June meeting, the MPC also noted that the current rate is consistent with the economy converging to its potential, suggesting that its 2025 forecast of 3% GDP growth is close to potential.

Finally, the MPC had not been worried about negative inflation prints. And with May's inflation print coming within target, due to the end of subsidies, this supports the bank's view that the temporary period of deflation did not reflect underlying demand. We expect inflation to be within the target range sustainably from September, albeit led by base efects. We expect inflation to average 0.8% y/y in 2024, increasing to 1.0% in 2025. Core price pressures, however, will likely still be even more benign.

With a recovery in tourism receipts, we expect the current account to register a surplus of USD10.8bn (2.0% of GDP) in 2024, a large rise from 2023's USD7.0bn. This is almost the same as our earlier forecast, as a faster recovery in tourism ofsets the subdued outlook for merchandise exports. We now expect tourist arrivals to increase to 36mn in 2024 (34mn earlier; 90.2% of 2019 arrivals). With an increase in per capita spending by tourists, compared with 2023, we see tourism receipts increasing to almost 69% of 2019 levels. Structural weakness in the typically strong export sectors of Thailand will, however, continue to weigh on the current account. In 2025, we expect the current account surplus to increase to USD26.1bn (\~4.7% of GDP).

On the other hand, deficits are increasing on the fiscal side, with the government revising its medium term framework to show higher deficits and higher debt. A supplementary budget of THB122bn for FY2024, adding to the THB3.8trn original budget, if passed, is likely to lead to a deficit of THB805bn (\~4.4% of GDP). The supplementary budget and the additional THB150bn deficit announced over and above the to-be-approved FY2025 budget are expected to fund part of the THB500bn digital wallet scheme<sup>26</sup> . The government wants the Bank for Agriculture and Agricultural Cooperatives to fund the remaining amount. With persisting legislative and legal troubles and an unclear timeline, our forecasts still do not include any impact from the proposed digital wallet  scheme, hence this presents upside risks if the scheme is implemented as planned by the government.

Even outside the legislative troubles around funding for the digital wallet scheme, Thailand has no shortage of other political troubles brewing, with high profile court cases against PM Srettha, former PM Thaksin Shinawatra and the Move Forward Party all set for further deliberation in the courts as soon as 18 June<sup>27</sup> . We think politics will be the main narrative for Thailand now, with the BoT having all but closed the door to easing.

# Emerging Europe, Middle East and Africa Winds of change

EEMEA has defied headwinds, including geopolitical risks. Yet, idiosyncratic risks remain elevated. We remain constructive on growth, but we see policy rates staying higher for longer.

Trade Summary | Egypt | Czechia | Ghana | Hungary | Israel | Kazakhstan | Kenya |Kuwait | Nigeria | Poland | Romania | Russia | Saudi Arabia | South Africa | Tunisia | Turkey | Ukraine | Zambia

## Overview

EEMEA has defied headwinds of heightened geopolitical risks and delayed easing cycles so far this year. Yet, monetary policy conditions have remained tight for longer than expected on the back of both domestic and external factors. Several tailwinds have boosted resilience across the region: still tight labour markets; well behaved goods' inflation; a less severe economic slowdown in China than feared; and heightened geopolitical risks remaining under control.

Idiosyncratic risks have ushered winds of change across EEMEA with (geo)political factors in focus. Election-led policy shifts (Poland, Turkey, Nigeria), generous external support (Egypt, Kenya), and political pivots (South Africa, Kuwait) point to the importance of medium-term structural shifts.

## A mixed manufacturing rebound

This year's rebound in global manufacturing has not fully benefited EEMEA yet. Manufacturing PMIs have remained below the expansion mark across the region, except in Russia, where the sanctions have proved to have a limited efect. Supply-side bottlenecks (South Africa) and policy tightening across the region has weighed on the recovery, although new sales orders, including new export orders, have improved in recent months. Signs of excess capacity have dominated CEE, notably in Czechia, although the trend has started to reverse. Meanwhile, in Russia, where defence has boosted manufacturing performance, orders have been growing at a faster pace than output with the diference turning negative for the first time since end-2022, pointing to a potential increase in inventory in the coming period.

## Domestic demand still in the driving seat

Mounting pressure from a challenging domestic and external backdrop over the past two years has accelerated the return to policy orthodoxy and structural reforms. After years of macroeconomic imbalances, several countries – including Turkey, Egypt, and Nigeria – have embraced a policy shift with an impressive macroeconomic rebalancing.

Overall, household domestic demand remains strong in Turkey despite considerable policy tightening. Elsewhere in CEE, domestic demand has rebounded strongly thanks to still tight labour markets, disinflation, and election-led fiscal spending (eg, in Poland and Romania). Within CEE,

Czechia's growth has strongly rebounded over the past two quarters thanks to domestic demand, albeit capex has remained a drag amid slower EU transfers. In Poland, relatively strong

growth was supported by private and public consumption (2.1% and 1.9% q/q respectively), in turn supported by resilient wage growth of some 10% y/y. A normalisation in capex thanks to improved EU relations will likely be ofset by weaker manufacturing activity and hence we keep our growth forecasts for 2024 and 2025 unchanged at 2.9% and 3.9% respectively. Hungary, too, posted a strong Q1 print but domestic demand did not contribute much despite strong wage growth. With lower EU funding, capex remains a drag so we have shaved 0.4pp from our 2024 growth forecast to take it to 2.5% y/y but leave our forecast for a rebound in 2025 unchanged at 3.5%. Romania posted the strongest performance across CEE with Q1 GDP growth accelerating by 0.5pp to 1.6% y/y, a performance that should be supported by expansionary fiscal policy ahead of Q4 elections. Similar to CEE, Russia's labour market is also tight but fiscal spending has been in the driving seat, buoyed by defence spending and social transfers. Therefore, we make large upward revisions to our growth forecasts – by 1.3pp to 3.3% in 2024 and by 0.9pp to 2.1% in 2025.

Three macroeconomic reasons that support a 'higher for longer' policy rate thesis The average policy rate of the six main central banks in our EEMEA universe (Hungary, Poland, Russia, South Africa, Czechia, Romania) has dropped 0.9pp to 8.2% year to date<sup>28</sup> . However, we expect the average policy rate of these countries to drop only 0.3pp further to 7.9% in the second half of this year.

There are three main macroeconomic drivers supporting the higher-for-longer argument across EEMEA. First, there has been a noticeable slowdown in the pace of disinflation in recent months, with support from the high-base efect having mostly gone. Both headline and core CPI have stabilised in recent months at levels well above their pre-COVID norms. Markets need more time to conclude whether this is a new normal, but if inflation settles at a level above historical averages, so would the neutral policy rate (r-star), meaning policy rates would stabilise above pre-COVID levels.

Second, we believe economic growth is set to improve after Q2 24. Even so, growth in Q1 24, when the disinflation trend started to slow, looks set to be the strongest quarter of the year in EEMEA according to our forecasts. We expect GDP growth in the region to accelerate from 2.5% in 2023 to 2.7% in 2024 and to 3% next year. Likewise, despite a major slowdown in economic growth in 2023, labour markets remained tight across the region. With ongoing disinflation and relatively stable nominal wage growth, we expect strong real wage growth in 2024 – in some cases approaching double digits (CE4 countries).

Third, fiscal deficits are above historical averages, leading to relatively more cautious central banks. For EM as a whole, the budget deficit was at 5.5% of GDP in 2023, well above the levels seen before COVID-19. The IMF expects this to increase to 5.6% of GDP in 2024, due in part to the heavy election calendar this year (EEMEA 2024 elections: A year like no other, 31 January 2024). One other driver of high budget deficits is a partial continuation of COVID-related subsidies. On the flip side, if/when an economy decides to end energy or food subsidies, it would create major uncertainty about the inflation outlook, possibly leading to more hawkish central banks. Poland is a good example – despite the May CPI print of 2.5% y/y being in line with its inflation target of 2.5% (+/- 1%), the NBP has remained on hold at 5.75% since October 2023 on the basis that uncertainties related to the end of food and energy subsidies pose upside risks to its inflation forecasts.

## The reversal of fortunes for oil producers

Saudi Arabia has been instrumental in the survival of the OPEC+ agreement in the years following the pandemic. Yet, the Kingdom has carried most of the adjustment, having cut oil output to 9mb/d since August 2023, the longest period of low output since the Global Financial Crisis. This has created ample spare capacity of more than 5mb/d among oil producers that broadly ofsets the escalation of geopolitical risks. The Kingdom's strong relations with Russia that helped to extend the OPEC+ deal has supported an implicit floor on oil prices, yet it has come at the a cost of significant market share losses favouring notably US shale producers. Still, the latest OPEC+ survey showed that strong discipline from the Kingdom was ofset by undercompliance of both Iraq and Russia, together producing 471kb/d above their quota in May. While UAE has emerged as a major beneficiary of OPEC's recent decision to gradually reverse output cuts, pressure on the Kingdom as the swing producer should remain high, in our view. Saudi Arabia has set out ambitious medium-term capex commitments which increases its vulnerability to periods of sustained lower oil prices.
