## Structural shifts in Middle East power transitions

A combination of domestic and external factors has set the stage for the ascent of GCC geopolitical influence. Yet, a generational power shift has been at the centre of the new geopolitical landscape and GCC international relations. In fact, the Arab Spring has left a longlasting impression across MENA, with countries of the Maghreb unsettled by popular protests, and Egypt's political transition taking a toll on its economy, while Syria and Iraq have been consumed by internal strife amid a civil war and political instability, respectively. Security expenditure thus remains a regional priority amid ongoing conflicts in Libya, Yemen, Syria and two regional wars in Gaza and Ukraine. Power transitions across the region have equally been marked by a consistent shift to a younger generation that has been at the centre of the changing geopolitical backdrop.

GCC political regimes have been marked by a shift in power successions from the modern founders of the state such as Sheikh Zayed in UAE, King Abdulaziz in Saudi Arabia, and Sheikh Mubarak al Sabah (al Kabeer, ie, the Great) in Kuwait. The often non-written rule of power succession to direct descendants traditionally provided relative stability and certainty about power sharing; however, over the past decade, a new generational shift has started to occur. These factors have favoured the emergence of more active foreign policy across the region, boosting economic and political cooperation with China, the BRICS and Russia after the broadening of OPEC to non-aligned oil producers. GCC countries are thus well positioned at an early stage of the return of great power competition.

## When elections keep on giving

We previously highlighted that 2024 was a year like no other across EEMEA, amid a heavy global election calendar (see EEMEA elections: A year like no other, 31 January 2024). Election-related risks and uncertainties are most tangible in South Africa, which faces a new era based on coalition-type arrangements to which both the ruling ANC government and the opposition would need to adapt. Investors have focused on President Ramaphosa's proposal to form a Government of National Unity (GNU) that is reminiscent of the Mandela era. While the DA has oficially entered the coalition following the first session of the new parliament (Reuters, 15 June), both EFF and MK Party have been left outside the GNU. While such a scenario may alleviate investors' concerns about divisive policy electives, a GNU would not be without its own challenges, and uncertainty around its composition may linger for some time, amid likely dificult negotiations. We also see a medium-term risk in the potential emergence of intra- and inter-party disagreements once discussions on the adoption and implementation of unpopular reforms begin. Even so, we believe that the inclusion of the DA in the governing arrangement gives the country a fighting chance of being able to ultimately implement tested macro policy that should lift economic growth and job creation on to a higher path.

The combination of critical elections in the West and a heavy calendar across EEMEA has created a number of externalities and heightened uncertainty about the direction of policies. The strong showing of the right and far right parties in EU parliamentary elections should extend support to some of the periphery such as Tunisia and Hungary. For example, Italian-led far right PM Meloni has long standing strong relations with Hungary PM Orban and has been a vocal supporter of financial transfers to Tunisia, as part of the Mattei plan to curb illegal immigration, a deal supported by EU Commissioner Ursula von der Leyen. More broadly, however, ripple efects from EU elections should remain muted even if the rise of far-right representation may weaken support to the ongoing war in Ukraine. In contrast, the outcome of US elections is likely to have more long-lasting implications for the ongoing wars in Ukraine and Gaza, relations with Iran and Middle East geopolitical stability, to name a few.

## Czechia: Return of growth

Ercan Erguzel, Barclays, UK | Marek Raczko, Barclays, UK

Marking to market better-than-expected performance in Q1 24, we revise up our growth forecast by 0.3pp to 1.4% y/y for 2024 while leaving 2025 unchanged at 2.2% y/y. In our forecast horizon through year-end 2025, we expect CPI to remain consistently within the target band, which puts Czechia in a unique position among the CE4. We expect the CNB to slow the pace of cuts to 25bp from the next MPC meeting on 27 June, to be followed by two further 25bp cuts at the two following meetings in August and September to reach 4.5%.

• FX: We expect the CZK to be under pressure, due to its sensitivity to the weaker EUR and deterioration in the Czech carry advantage. The EUR remains under pressure amid increased political uncertainty after the EP elections and due to continued economic divergence with the US. Although the CZK has the smallest beta to EURUSD in the CE-3 region, we still expect it to underperform the PLN on the back of weaker growth momentum and lower carry. We do not expect the CNB to push against slow-grinding CZK depreciation.

• Rates: We expect CNB to deliver three more 25bp cuts this year, broadly in line with market pricing. The CNB’s communication will likely remain cautious amid global risk aversion, but markets should continue to see risk skewed toward a prolonged cycle, as the Czech terminal rate lies around 3.5%. Given uncertainty about the pace of policy easing, we refrain from recommending any receivers of front-end rates. We see more value in back-end rates, Czech GBs in particular, but their recent performance has been highly correlated with US rates. Consequently, US rates stability will remain a pre-requisite for longer-dated Czech rate receivers.

The return of growth. Growth in Q1 24 was strong at 0.3% q/q by Czechia standards, marking the second highest q/q growth since Q1 22, following even stronger growth of 0.45% q/q in Q4 23. An annualised growth rate of 1.3% is primarily supported by household and public consumption at 4.1% and 3.9%, respectively. On the flip side, there was a sharp slowdown in investment keeping the headline print low. This was probably a result of a transition-related slowdown in EU funds—as the new seven-year budget period started in 2024—and will likely to correct itself in the upcoming quarters. On the consumption side, we expect household consumption to remain strong in the remainder of year with help from strong real wage growth of 4.8% y/y in Q1 24, a sharp reversal from -0.5% y/y in the previous quarter. It was the highest real wage growth since Q2 21 and was mainly driven by the private sector as ongoing fiscal consolidation limits hikes in public sector jobs. Marking to market better-than-expected performance in Q1 24, we revise up our growth forecast by 0.3pp to 1.4% y/y for 2024 while while leaving 2025 unchanged at 2.2% y/y.

Fiscal policy remains tight. Czechia is one of the few countries in the region, or even in the world, undertaking successful fiscal consolidation. The central state budget narrowed to CZK210bn in the first five months of this year, down from CZK271bn in the same period in 2023. In addition to spending control, strong tax collection helped to deliver this performance. In terms of consolidated balance, the oficial deficit target of 2.2% for 2024 looks ambitious, given last year's figure of 3.7%, but we expect the YE24 figure to be very close this, at 2.5%.

Inflation to remain within the target band in the forecast horizon. A negative output gap and fiscal consolidation made it possible for a strong and sustainable disinflation path. CPI dropped to levels in line with the inflation target of 2% (+/- 1%) in January this year and has remained within the target band since. In our forecast horizon until year-end 2025, we expect

CPI to remain consistently within the target band, which puts Czechia in a unique position among the CE4. It is not only headline but core CPI also remains within the target band, suggesting that unlike other EMs, the improvement in inflation outlook is not only attributable to cyclical corrections in food or energy.

There are still some upside risks to inflation. That said, the CPI trend is not free from risk. Similar to other countries in the region, strong labour market conditions need to be watched closely. Expected acceleration in domestic demand in the remainder of year is also a risk for inflation, as it also means tighter labour market conditions.

Room for an additional 75bp of cuts in total this year. Against the backdrop of ongoing upside inflationary risks and the cautious stance of the central bank, we expect the CNB to slow the pace of cuts to 25bp at the next MPC meeting on 27 June, with two more 25bp cuts at the two following meetings in August and September to reach 4.5%. The risks in the short term are skewed to a lower policy rate with a non-negligible probability of a 50bp cut in June. With our CPI forecast at 2.4% y/y by end-2024, our terminal policy rate forecast of 4.5% corresponds to an ex-post real policy rate of 2.1pp. If we assume GDP growth at around its potential in Q4 24, a real policy rate at around 2.1% looks high compared with neutral policy rate forecasts for Czechia at around 1%. Although we agree that neutral policy rates are higher for most countries, we do not think it is higher than 1.5% for Czechia, meaning that we expect the CNB to remain cautious in our forecast horizon.

## Egypt: No complacency

Brahim Razgallah, Barclays, UK | Andreas Kolbe, Barclays, UK

Egypt's generous financial support should lead to policy complacency about medium-term challenges. We believe the currency has returned to a de facto peg with a small band of fluctuation.

• Credit: Egypt is among the best performers in the global EM sovereign credit market YTD, as the market has – correctly, in our view – priced out default risks in the near and medium term. This has led to significant bull-steepening of the curve. Investor focus is now likely to shift to the longer-term outlook for the country. While risks and challenges persist in this regard, we believe that the carry on ofer is attractive enough to justify a continued Overweight rating. On the USD curve, in order to maximize carry, we see best value in the highest-yielding bonds in the 25y part of the curve and recommend buying EGYPT 2049s and 2050s. Given that EUR spreads have lagged USD spreads in the rally in the 5y belly of the curve, we also recommend a switch from EGYPT USD 2029s into EUR 2030s.

## A turning point with no complacency

Egypt's outlook has improved considerably since the announcement of several support packages, including a \$5bn top-up to its EFF programme and a \$35bn Ras el-Hekma deal. Yet, Egypt's long-standing challenges remain the same, pressing for decisive policy action to address the country's core weaknesses. While recent macro stabilisation has given investors enough comfort in the cyclical turnaround, several medium-term challenges remain, including a relatively closed economy (lowest openness among regional peers), mounting demographic challenges, still-elevated social disparities (40% live below the poverty line), a high interest burden (fivefold that of similarly rated peers), and institutional inertia (eg, monetary policy). The largesse of recent funding should thus not lead to policy complacency.

## IMF anchor still not fully addressing institutional inertia

The augmented 2022 EFF has extended the four areas of the initial programme with an increased focus on capping public investments and a shift toward sustained FX flexibility, which had proven major drivers of last year's imbalances. Egypt remains a net payer to the IMF through 2030, except in FY25/26 and FY26/27 (Figure 9) pointing to the importance of carefully managing recent bufers through a longer time frame. It also places greater emphasis on the external adjustment amid ongoing pressure on Suez Canal revenues and potential pent-up FX demand from macro stabilization and Ras el-Hekma led import growth. Importantly, the IMF programme makes exchange rate flexibility a top priority, implying considerable USDEGP depreciation to 56 from FY24/25, although inflation looks set to decelerate to 15.3% y/y by June 2025.

## Egypt's headline inflation has already peaked

In Egypt, sequential headline inflation returned to a trend-like average of 1.1% m/m in April, highlighting the fast pace at which FX pass-through has faded compared with previous periods of large FX adjustments. Still, this masks divergent trends, with food inflation contracting 0.9% m/m, the first decline since June 2022. In contrast, a number of other subcomponents of the CPI basket jumped in April, including tobacco (7.9%), clothing (7.5%), and transport (7.2%). Since food accounts for one-third of the CPI basket, this was enough to extend headline inflation's downtrend to 31% y/y in April from this year's peak of 35.7% in February. Encouragingly, food inflation has further room to decelerate thanks to the lagged efects from slower international food inflation and the end of Ramadan. Still, FX pass-through and gradual removal of subsidies remain in the driving seat of disinflation, and we thus expect the central bank to maintain its current FX policy, with limited FX volatility. We previously expected the currency to appreciate towards 45 against the dollar by the end of the fiscal year, but we now see the EGP remaining within a +/-1 pound range over an implicit central level of 47.5 against the dollar through yearend. Relative FX stability should help bring headline inflation down to 14.3% y/y by end-Q1 25, when we expect the central bank to start its easing cycle, with a cumulative 600bp in cuts in H1 25.

# Ghana: Restructuring progresses, but watch the elections

Michael Kafe, Barclays, UK | Andreas Kolbe, Barclays, UK | Stella Cridge, Barclays, UK

Ghana has now signed an MOU with oficial creditors and is fully engaged with private creditors to achieve comparable debt forgiveness. Finalization of the MOU with the OCC opens the door for the completion of the second review of the ECF programme and the disbursement of some USD360mn by the IMF.

• Credit: We believe a breakthrough in negotiations with bondholders is achievable within the next few weeks and think recovery values close to 60 are possible. As discussed in more detail in EM sovereign restructurings: Default lines, 23 May 2024, we would value the Joint Working Scenario presented in April around this number for PDI-carrying bonds, based on an 11% exit yield assumption. And while some fine-tuning of the proposal may still be required to fully meet IMF requirements and to satisfy local bondholders (which could arguably require some concessions from international bondholders relative to the Joint Working Scenario), we note that Zambia's restructuring concluded with a single-digit exit yield. This gives us confidence that meaningful residual upside remains from current market prices of Ghana bonds in the low 50s and we hence maintain an Overweight rating on Ghana sovereign credit. Among corporates, we rate Kosmos Energy – for which Ghana accounts for around 60% of liquids group output – at Overweight. The company has already made progress in extending its debt maturity profile via the issuance of a new convertible bond and extension of bank maturities, and is approaching its cash flow inflection point as capex eases in the new Tortue gas project in Senegal/Mauritania (set to come online in H2 24). We see net leverage falling to c.1.7x by YE 24 (from 1.9x at Q1 24), which should support further refinancing prospects.

Significant restructuring progress. On 12 June, the Ministry of Finance indicated that the government of Ghana had signed of on a Memorandum of Understanding with its Oficial Creditor Committee regarding the agreement in principle on debt treatment that was reached earlier in January. The individual signatory countries will now go ahead and formulate their own procedures with regards to the signing and subsequent implementation of the agreed terms through bilateral agreements. For now, the deal with oficial creditors holding some USD5bn of the country’s external debt should lead to significant cost savings, which should allow financial resources to be redirected to other crucial areas, including health and education. Finalization of the MOU with oficial creditors should help give discussions with commercial creditors some momentum, and we expect a comparable deal with the latter group to be reached in the coming weeks.

To be clear, the authorities tabled a Joint Working Scenario (JWS) to bondholders owed some USD13bn in April, where they were ofered three new bonds with difering maturities, or a longer-dated, lower-coupon ofering without a notional haircut but larger NPV loss (likely designed to appeal to local bondholders). A separate PDI bond with a 33% notional haircut is also part of the deal. However, according to the Minister of Finance, that proposed JWS would breach the Debt Sustainability Threshold by roughly 1pp of GDP on an NPV basis, and it was thus rejected by the IMF. A tweaked and hopefully compliant version with the latest IMF DSA is believed to have been presented to bondholders during the week of 10 June 2024<sup>29</sup> .

For now, sign-of on the MOU with oficial creditors opens the door for Ghana to complete the second review of its 36-month USD3bn ECF program with the IMF in the coming weeks, granting the country access to the disbursement of a third tranche of some USD360mn. The

Ghanaian cedi has been on a depreciating trend since the beginning of the year, driven in part by the seasonally high demand for foreign exchange during the first quarter of the year as importers scramble to settle their deferred Christmas-related liabilities. And while prices of key export commodities such as gold, cocoa and oil have remained relatively firm, lower export volumes have placed a lid on cocoa and oil export proceeds in particular, at a time when oil and other imports have remained rather sticky.

Looking ahead, we believe that some short-term FX stability is likely in the coming weeks and months, thanks to the inward flows from the IMF and other multilateral lenders. However, with elections coming up in December, we believe the country’s macro metrics are likely to be unhinged by the chronic fiscal excesses that have typically characterised election years. It is also worth noting that, as disbursements under the current ECF/EFF program are heavily frontloaded, more than half of the financial resources under the program would have already been disbursed ahead of the elections, leaving the fund with little leverage. It is often dificult to monitor the fiscus in the last two quarters leading up to elections, as the Ministry of Finance often does not give much away. Even so, given that Ghana is a small, open economy that relies heavily on the rest of the world for imports of most goods while exporting only a handful of commodities, the tell-tale signs of burgeoning fiscal excesses are often visible in the currency market, where its often a matter of time before even the strongest attempts to support the ced become unsustainable.

Some stickiness in inflation, however. After peaking at 54% y/y in December 2022, headline CPI decelerated throughout 2023, allowing the Bank of Ghana to ease policy by 100bp at its first sitting of this year. Since then, the disinflation process appears to have lost some momentum and CPI has remained between 23% y/y and 26% y/y for most of 2024, presumably stalled by the weakening currency. The May reading, for example, came in at 23.15% y/y, which is higher than the 22% y/y we forecast and the consensus expectation of 21.7% y/y, thanks in part to strong FX pass-through. Looking ahead, we expect headline inflation to decelerate further and close the year at around 20% y/y, and to reach 14.5% at end-2025. This should allow the Bank of Ghana to ease policy by another 100bp at its last meeting of this year, followed by a further 400bp of easing through 2025, taking the policy rate to 24% by end-2025.

## Hungary: Getting closer to the end of easing cycle

Ercan Erguzel, Barclays, UK | Andreas Kolbe, Barclays, UK | Marek Raczko, Barclays, UK

The road ahead for Hungary in terms of EU funds remains bumpy. However, its efect on Hungarian assets is likely to be more limited due to an external surplus in the forecast horizon. We lower our GDP growth estimate by 0.4pp to 2.5% y/y for 2024 and keep next year's forecast unchanged at 3.5% y/y. We expect two more 25bp cuts to 6.50% in H2 24.

• Credit: Hungary sovereign credit underperformed the broader market and regional peers earlier in the year, but has partially reversed this underperformance over the course of Q2. This makes valuations relatively less attractive, in our view, especially given ongoing fisca pressures, likely further borrowing in international markets later this year, and the longstanding tensions with the EU holding back funding. However, underweight positioning by EMBI-benchmarked investors provides a technical anchor. We hence see the outlook for Hungary spreads as neutral and maintain a Market Weight rating. Given EUR-USD spread diferentials and likely lower vulnerability of USD paper to contagion from political volatility in Europe, we recommend focusing on the USD curve and buying REPHUN \$2036s.

• FX and rates: We expect a weaker EUR to weigh on the HUF due to its high beta to EURUSD. We also expect Hungary’s weak economic momentum, as well carry deterioration, to exert additional pressure on the HUF. Any new tensions between the EC and the Hungarian government would further add to these pressures. We recommend being long EURHUF. We expect the NBH to deliver a final 50bp cut at the next meeting, or split it between the next two meetings. The market is pricing in less than 50bp in cuts this year, reflecting FX pressures. In our view, only significant HUF appreciation would open the door for additional cuts this year. On the flip side, Hungarian core services inflation remains high, which suggest that the NBH may be forced to prolong the pause in the cutting cycle. Consequently, we expect the market to put pressure on belly rates, while the back end of the curve might remain heightened amid increased global risk aversion and Hungary’s poor fiscal metrics.

Need for speed for private consumption. GDP growth in Q1 24 was relatively strong at 0.8% q/q, up from a flat performance in the previous quarter. Unlike its peers in the region, there was no contribution from private consumption to headline growth, despite strong wage growth. Despite positive performance and sequential annualised growth at 3.1%, the 4Q trailing growth performance is still negative, at -0.4% y/y. Looking at the remainder of the year, we expect capex growth to remain weak despite lower interest rates as funding from the EU are lower compared with previous years. On the flip side, there is more room to go for private consumption, with lower interest rates and strong labour market conditions. Against the backdrop of weak investment prospects, we lower our GDP growth projection by 0.4pp to 2.5% y/y for 2024 and keep next year's forecast unchanged at 3.5% y/y.

C/A surplus to remain stable around current levels in H2 24. Despite weaker growth prospects in the EU afecting export performance negatively, Hungary was able to improve its C/A surplus to €3.3bn in March 2024, up from €1bn in 2023. The strong performance was related mainly to support from finished FDIs, being more pronounced in electric car battery investments. We expect the positive trend to stabilise, with a recovery in domestic demand in the remainder of year, and the C/A balance to be at 1.5% of GDP at YE24.

Budget deficit is very likely to exceed the target in 2024. The budget registered a deficit of 6.1% of GDP in 2023, well above the revised target of 5.2% (up from 3.9% previously). The fiscal target for 2024 has also been revised up, to 4.5% of GDP from 2.9% previously, as the Ministry of Finance now envisages a gradual path for fiscal consolidation, aiming to bring the budget deficit down to 1.9% of GDP by 2028. Despite a 7.4% y/y contraction in the budget deficit in January-

May 2024, the general government deficit of HUF2.6trn in this period represents almost 65% of the annual deficit target. Based on the YTD performance, we expect the deficit to be at 5.1% of GDP in 2024. The decision to acquire 80% of the Budapest Airport for €3.1bn is unlikely to have any efect on the EU-defined budget, but it should add some pressure to cash-based figures, and would thus require excess borrowing if the government's plans to raise the necessary funds via asset sales and other means remain below the targets.

EU funding to remain on a bumpy path. After a partial ending of the disagreements with the EU, the EC announced the release of €10bn to Hungary, followed by an additional €2bn of education-related funds. There is still a frozen amount of c.€10bn earmarked for Hungary from normal budgetary funds for the next seven years. On top of this, another €9.7bn (€5.8bn in grants and €3.9bn in loans) in proposed Recovery and Resilience Facility (RRF) funding remains frozen. In addition to the Article 7 procedure, there are some other disagreements with the EU such as the ongoing infringement procedure against sovereignty law. This makes the road ahead for Hungary in terms of EU funds bumpy.

Cut in June, hold in H2 24. Despite a recent base efect-related upward drift to 4.0% y/y, the pace of disinflation was stronger than previously expected. Food CPI, at -1.5% y/y, has had the most significant disinflationary efect, contributing -0.3pp to the headline CPI. On the flip side, services CPI remains very sticky, with recreation (up 0.5pp to 9.5%) and restaurants & hotels (up 0.6pp to 9.5% y/y) on an upward trajectory. That said, as noted in the NBH statement in June, the improvement in core CPI has ended and it is expected to go up by c.1pp to c.5% in H2 24, limiting the NBH's room for more aggressive cuts. In June, the NBH delivered a 25bp cut to 7.0%, as widely expected. The interpretation of the macro outlook in the statement was unchanged from the previous: the NBH expects the ongoing recovery in economic growth to become more visible, helped by household consumption in H2 24 and stronger exports in 2025. Inflation has stayed within the target band YTD, but is likely to rise slightly due to backwardlooking pricing of services and base efects, with core CPI set to rise towards 5.0% in H2 24. In his regular post-decision press briefing, Deputy Governor Virag noted that the expected rise in core CPI limits the room for rate cuts in H2 24. We expect two more 25bp cuts in the July and August meetings to 6.50%, after which the NBH should remain on hold in the last four MPC meetings of the year.

## Israel: Complex politics

Zalina Alborova, Barclays, UK | Marek Raczko, Barclays, UK| Stella Cridge, Barclays, UK | Valentina Stoykova, Barclays, UK

• FX: We maintain our constructive structural view on the ILS because we see it as fundamentally undervalued. In the near term, however, ILS price action remains determined by geopolitical headlines. In our view, the market already embodies hefty risk premia in Israeli assets and risk-reward is skewed in favour of the ILS. Consequently, we retain our short EURILS trade recommendation.

• Rates: We expect the BoI to err on the side of caution and not ofer any more rate cuts this year. Our view stands broadly in line with the market pricing of 10bp worth of cuts by yearend. The prolonged geopolitical uncertainty and issues on the structural side, mainly due to labour shortages, may prolong the BoI's pause into the next year, putting pressure on 1y ILS swaps. On the flip side, we view ILS back-end rates as cheap, ofering a very wide premium over US rates. In our view, the risk premium embedded in 5y5y ILS rates ofers good riskreward, hence we recommend receiving 5y5y ILS versus paying 5y5y USD OIS.

• Credit: We rate the Israel corporate sector Market Weight to reflect ongoing geopolitical risks in the region, but wide sector spreads within the EEMEA context and resilient stand-alone fundamentals across corporates and banks. Among banks, strong fundamentals and high carry should support the performance of subs, which we broadly prefer over seniors. This preference is the key driver behind our rating on HAPOAL (OW), with only one Tier2 and the highest CET1 bufer among Israel bank peers in Q1 24, and IDBILI (UW), with only one senior bond that trades tight to the sovereign. Among the gas credits, we see Leviathan (OW) as the best way to take exposure, given a supportive technical outlook for bonds (owner NewMed plans to repay the next maturity – \$600mn in June 2025 – through a combination of cash flow and bank borrowing rather than a new bond).

Security crisis and interconnection of domestic and foreign politics. Since our previous quarterly update in March, ceasefire talks between Israel and Hamas have not progressed, despite increased pressure<sup>30</sup> from the US and regional countries for a pause in fighting. Amid increased frequency of the airstrikes between Israel and Hezbollah, the risks of wider escalation in norther Israel and Lebanon have increased, but we maintain our base-case scenario that the conflict will be contained between Israel and Hamas. With increased influence from the far right (associated with the exit of opposition leader Gantz from the war cabinet) and recent comments from Israel's national security advisor "expecting another seven months of fighting"<sup>31</sup> in Gaza, the probability of a permanent ceasefire in 2024 looks remote, unless a domestic political crisis pushes Israel to call early elections. What might trigger this and when remains uncertain, but the core coalition's ability to survive to the end of the Knesset summer term (28 July) could potentially help it to secure power through year-end or further, given parliamentary holidays until end-October and US elections in November. That said, the conditions of the Haredi conscription bill and/or a hostage deal may become a fault lines for the right-wing block, as the scale of the current protest movement is limited, as are the chances of a new election cycle via a vote of no confidence, in our view.

The economic challenges presented by the war are manageable, but the risks are rising. The strong GDP rebound in Q1 24 (by 14.4% q/q saar, 1.3pp above our March forecast) and the Stat Ofice's upgrade to some of the previous quarters mechanically lifted our 2024 forecast by

0.3pp to 1.9% y/y. The labour market remains tight (amid restrictions on Palestinian workers and shortages in the foreign labour force), with the broad unemployment rate (including temporary absent from work for economic reasons) declining to 4.7% in April 2024 (from 5.9% at the beginning of the year and 10.2% in October 2023). Alongside this, monthly high-frequency data indicate economic moderation since February, while the construction, manufacturing, agriculture and tourism sectors remain weak. Investments in the hi-tech sector—which in 2023 accounted for about 20% of GDP, 53% of total exports and some 12% of employees—dropped by 55% y/y, while 36% of start-up companies surveyed in March-April expected a "high probability that their next fundraising round will be at significantly lower value than their current one"<sup>32</sup> . These factors, in addition to increased security issues, pose downside risks to our growth projections of 1.9% y/y this year and 3.5% y/y next.

Protracted war increases budget deficit further above the 6.6% target. By end-May, the 12- month cumulative budget deficit had widened to 7.2% of GDP, versus 4.2% in 2023, driven by increased spending. In 4m24 total expenditures grew 35% y/y, while tax revenues increased by only 2% y/y. Discussions on the 2025 budget, which the MinFin aims to accelerate in the next few weeks, will be in focus for the rating agencies, particularly further adjustments to spending and the tax system. We forecast the deficit at 7.0% of GDP in 2024 and 4.3% in 2025. That said, a potential shift in the VAT hike from 2025 to mid-2024 and stronger economic activity in H2 24 could bring this year's deficit close to the planned 6.6% of GDP.

Inflation pressure gains momentum. During the spring months, price growth has been accelerating, stabilising at 2.8% y/y in May, while inflation expectations remained around the upper limit of the target range. On the back of rising pressure in the housing, transport and food components, we expect price growth to remain close to 3% y/y in H2 24 and slightly above the target in July (base efects), accelerating towards 3.2-3.3% y/y by year-end. Higher spending on travel during the summer holiday period and a potential shift in the VAT hike from 2025 to mid-2024 skew the risks to the upside. We expect inflation to average 2.8% y/y in 2024 and 2.7% y/y in 2025, a respective 0.5pp and 0.6pp higher than our March projections.

The space for monetary easing in H2 24 is significantly limited. In May, BoI stayed on hold for a third straight meeting, with neutral guidance due to high geopolitical uncertainty and an increase in inflation risks. Since then, there has been no progress in ceasefire talks, while the fiscal deficit has widened and the inflation path has increased (see above). For these reasons, we now expect the BoI to keep the policy rate at 4.50% until end-Q1 25, as even in a scenario of geopolitical improvement, inflation pressure is likely to prevent the bank from delivering a cut.

## Kenya: Committed to consolidation

Michael Kafe, Barclays, UK | Andreas Kolbe, Barclays, UK

Kenya remains committed to fiscal consolidation and now targets a fiscal deficit of 3.3% of GDP in FY 24-25. It also targets a primary surplus of 2.3%. If achieved, this should help contain the rising debt burden.

• Credit: Following the strong performance after the new eurobond issuance and tender for the 2024s in February, Kenya has recently underperformed some SSA peers, including Angola and Nigeria. We think this has provided a good new entry point. Although medium-term risks related to Kenya’s high debt and debt servicing burden remain intact, we are comfortable with its fundamental trajectory, and our positioning analysis indicates that EMBI investors remain underweight the credit. Moreover, as the remaining c.USD557mn of the 2024s mature on 24 June, the front end of the curve in particular could benefit from re-investment flows. We therefore raise our rating on Kenya sovereign credit to Overweight (from Market weight) and recommend buying KENINT 2028s.

On the road to consolidation. The Treasury presented its FY 24-5 Budget on 13 June, in which the government reiterated its commitment to the pursuit of fiscal consolidation by proposing a budget that seeks to further narrow the fiscal gap. For the upcoming fiscal year starting 1 July, it hopes to reduce the fiscal deficit to an estimated 3.3% of GDP in FY 24-25 from 5.7% of GDP in FY 23-24, on an accrual basis. This is narrower than the 3.9% of GDP that it presented in its 2024 Budget Policy Statement in February 2024, albeit slightly higher than than the rather optimistic 2.9% that the Treasury had hoped for in May.

Revenues to print at 18.5% of GDP in FY 24-25: The government now hopes to achieve what appears to be a far more realistic tax/GDP ratio of 18.5%, down from what was a rather ambitious 19% target for the current fiscal year, through the implementation of a combination of tax policy and administrative reforms aimed at sealing revenue loopholes and enhancing the eficiency of the tax system. This is presumably partly in response to the ongoing drought conditions that are likely to impact economic activity and, as a corollary, tax revenues. These measures include minimising tax expenditure, especially on VAT refunds; the introduction of a minimum top-up tax for large multinationals that pay relatively low efective rates; the introduction of an annual motor vehicle tax; higher excise duties on liquid nicotine, betting, gaming, telephones, internet data services and money transfers; as well as a harmonisation of excise duty rates for cigarettes with and without filters to help minimise the incentive for misdeclaration. In addition, there are regional customs measures, including an extension of stay to import rice/wheat at an import duty rate of 35%/10% instead of the CET rate of 75%/35%, as well as an extension of duty remissions on imports for the assembly of motor vehicles.

With regards to expenditures, the government hopes to cut its budgeted outlays to 22.1% of GDP in FY 24-25. This is down from its February 2024 estimate of 23.2%, and an estimated 24.2% of GDP in the current fiscal year. Expenditure cuts will be attained from a rationalisation of transfers to semi-autonomous government agencies by at least 30% from the previous year’s approved budget levels; a reduction in allocations to Ministries, Departments and Agencies; cuts in goods and services such as state travel and training outlays; reductions in motor vehicle purchases; a suspension of refurbishments, including furniture; as well as the suspension of new recruitments for a year. At the same time, the government hopes to carry out a payroll audit, eliminate ghost workers and consolidate the public procurement functions.

With the above measures, the authorities hope to reduce the fiscal deficit to 3.3% of GDP once adjusted for grant receipts of some 0.3% of GDP. If successful, this should help shift the primary balance position from a deficit of 0.4% of GDP in the current year to a surplus of 2.3% of

GDP, thus giving the government’s debt reduction plans some momentum. The deficit will be financed from a combination of domestic borrowing of KES263bn (USD2.0bn), or 1.5% of GDP, and external borrowing of KES338bn (USD2.6bn), or 1.8% of GDP. To keep the cost of debt in check, the authorities hope to reduce external commercial debt, undertake liability management operations that help pay of expensive debt, and diversify their sources of funding to include the issuance of Panda, Samurai and Sukuk bonds, as well as maximising the use of concessional financing. Following the unexpected but successful issuance of a USD1.5bn eurobond in February, and USD1.6bn equivalent in local infrastructure bonds that also attracted significant foreign interest, the authorities retired most of the maturing June 2024 eurobond, leading the IMF to reduce the size of its financial commitment under the current program from SDR3.04bn (USD4bn) to SDR2.71bn (USD3.58bn). Separately, more eficient Treasury cash flow management by fully operationalising its Treasury Single Account and automating the government's payment platform should help to improve fiscal management and reduce endemic waste.

Decelerating inflation to pave the way for rate cuts. After peaking at 9.6% y/y in October 2022, headline CPI remained relatively sticky through most of 2023, buoyed by FX weakness and the conspicuous absence of a decisive monetary policy response. Following a rather late but still welcome aggressive tightening towards end-2023, the currency appreciated meaningfully. This helped to drag down inflation, which is now at the mid point of the 5% +/-200bp target range. At its June 2024 MPC meeting, the CBK left its policy rate on hold at 13% and took the opportunity to further enhance the efectiveness of its monetary policy transmission mechanism by narrowing the corridor around the Central Bank Rate from +/-250bp to +/-150bp and reduced the rate on its discount window from CBR+400bp to CBR+300bp. We expect CPI to track broadly sideways over the remainder of this year before coming of somewhat to close 2024 around 4.8% and reaching 4.5% at the end of 2025. With this trajectory, we believe that the risks are skewed towards the onset of easier money in the final quarter of the year, when we forecast a 100bp cut, to be followed by a further 300bp worth of cuts in 2025.

## Kuwait: A structural shift

Brahim Razgallah, Barclays, UK | Andreas Kolbe, Barclays, UK

Kuwait's power transition has structurally altered the country's outlook. We now expect the debt law to be approved before year-end.

## A pivotal political transition

Emir Mishaal’s decision to dissolve Parliament on 10 May has placed Kuwait's economic and political outlook on a new path, echoing its long history of power-sharing. Kuwait has had 46 governments since the adoption of the 1962 Constitution, up to the new government of PM Ahmad al Abdullah al Sabah, who was sworn in on 15 May. We expect far-reaching changes to the Constitution and the political system, although it remains unclear whether a new upper house may be created, similar to Bahrain. The recent appointment of a new Heir Apparent Sheikh Sabah Khalid al Sabah illustrates the country's deeply rooted history of power-sharing, as he is the first person to be named in the line of succession outside the two main al Sabah family branches, al Jaber and al Salem.

Over the past two decades, tensions over the power transition have afected the al Jaber family branch, particularly between former PM Sheikh Nasser al Mohammad and his deputy, Ahmad al Fahad, in 2006-15. While Kuwait has experienced a recurrent crisis between parliament and the government that led to suspensions of the Constitution (eg, in 1976 and 1986), the post-2006 period has been closely shaped by growing competition for power. The consolidation of executive and legislative powers should unlock pressing legislation that has stalled such as the debt law, accelerate development projects, and redirect GCC relations. Medium-term political changes will be centre to Kuwait in the upcoming period. But this political transition is likely to be gradual, with the election of a new Parliament unlikely for the next four years.

## Economic reforms and the future of the debt law

Sheikh Mishaal's decision to dissolve Parliament and suspend parts of the Constitution for up to four years will likely have far-reaching implications for Kuwait's near-term economic outlook. The consolidation of power should empower the new cabinet to accelerate reforms, including the long-delayed debt law, which expired in 2017. An extension has been delayed by political bickering, even a compromise draft with a KWD20bn (\$65bn) ceiling having been rejected in Parliament. As such, the government has been unable to issue domestic and external debt since end-2017, forcing it to draw on savings, with cumulative deficits of \$73bn over 2017-21.

This heightened reliance on savings has deprived the country's sovereign wealth fund from more eficient allocation of resources (ie, borrow at cheaper costs to fund the budget and invest savings at higher returns externally). This has also taken a toll on intergenerational equity, with public debt/GDP falling to one of the lowest levels in the world at 3.2%, well below the least leveraged GCC countries (eg, KSA at 26% GDP). Paying back public debt may have been perceived as a sign of a strong balance sheet amid extremely low debt levels. Yet banks' holding of domestic debt has decreased to a historical low of 0.2% of their assets since mid-2023, complicating liquidity management. This has forced banks to hold excessive cash positions to comply with the central bank's regulatory liquidity ratio. Although that seems to have stabilised at a historical low above 22% over the past two years, it is only slightly above the 18% CBK minimum level.

We expect the debt law to be passed by decree before year-end, helping the Ministry of Finance to ramp up domestic debt issuance. In contrast with the previous debt law, which was adopted by decree, the consolidation of legislative and executive powers means it will be immediately fully enacted this time and will not require the next Parliament's approval. This should structurally weaken Kuwaiti banks' demand for GCC sovereign debt over the medium term, primarily afecting Saudi eurobond holdings. GCC banks often have a much shorter investment horizon than international institutions. Primary issuance was also dominated by a strong local regional bid across the GCC, except in Saudi Arabia, where the local bid was only 9% of primary allocations in the early years of Saudi eurobond issuance (see What GCC bond inclusion means for investors, 18 October 2018). Kuwait's power transition should thus have far-reaching spillovers well beyond the domestic political landscape.

## Kazakhstan: Slowly but surely

Zalina Alborova, Barclays, UK | Andreas Kolbe, Barclays, UK | Stella Cridge, Barclays, UK

Kazakhstan remains on a stable economic path, supported by fiscal expansion and favourable external conditions. Plans of fiscal consolidation and higher oil production in H2 25<sup>33</sup> should help to improve external and budget balances. We expect the NBK to continue its gradual easing cycle with 25-50bp cuts, as the balance of risks remains pro-inflationary.

• Credit: In the absence of new issuance and with a strong local support anchor, we think Kazakhstan sovereign cash spreads are likely to remain very tight and hence unattractive from a global EM sovereign investor perspective. Based on tight valuations of bonds, we maintain an UW rating. For investors wishing to maintain exposure, we recommend selling 5y CDS, which screens as relatively attractive, in our view, given the pick-up in KAZAK CDS spreads even over those of select HY sovereigns in the EEMEA region, such as Oman and Morocco. We also see the bonds of quasi-sovereign KazMunayGas (Market Weight) as a good alternative, ofering an attractive pickup over Kazakhstan of c.160bp in the long end. KMG continues to post healthy earnings in the current oil price environment, and we expect net leverage to remain around 1x.

Stable economic growth, manageable challenges. After rebounding 5.1% y/y in 2023, the Kazakh economy has continued to grow, expanding 3.7% y/y in Q1 24, while the short-term economic indicator (\~60% of GDP) slowed to 3.9% y/y in April after printing at 4.7% y/y YTD in March, given a moderation in services and lower production and investment activity in the oil & gas sector. Yet, strong domestic demand, supported by social transfers to the population, gradual growth in lending and wages, and investments in infrastructure projects remained the key contributors to GDP growth. Abnormal floods during the spring and expectations of unfavourable weather in some regions in June may further reduce the production and exports of grain (after a 22.4% y/y contraction in the harvest in 2023)<sup>34</sup> , and the government has increased support measures to afected households and regions. Export dynamics remained stable, supported by favourable external conditions and high oil prices. While the government base case envisages 6% y/y growth in 2024-25, the data for January-May implies a lower path<sup>35</sup> . We keep our growth projections unchanged at 4.5% y/y this year and 4.9% y/y next, with risks skewed to the downside, given global geopolitical tensions, the higher inflation environment (mainly in Russia), uncertainty around the recovery of regions afected by the flood, and lower agricultural production in Kazakhstan.

External and fiscal balances in line with expectations. After returning to a deficit in 2023 (3.3% of GDP, or USD8.6bn), the current account deficit narrowed to USD0.9bn in Q1 24 from USD2.1bn in Q4 23 on the back of reduced foreign trade turnover. We expect imports to remain relatively strong (as well as domestic demand and growth) and export flows to increase, given higher oil production (planned expansion of oil production on TSO fields from H2 25) and global commodity prices, which should help to narrow the external deficit from 3.1% of GDP in 2024 to around 2.0% in 2025. Our views on Kazakhstan's budget have remained broadly unchanged since our previous EMQ. Despite an announced return to counter-cyclical fiscal rules, fiscal discipline could remain a challenge, as the mechanism of targeted transfers allows the country to increase spending (for special infrastructure and socially important projects) above budget plans if approved by the president. We forecast a deficit of around 2.5% of GDP in 2024-25, with risks skewed towards a larger deficit if downside economic risks (see above) materialise.

NBK to continue gradual and cautious easing cycle. At its previous meeting in May 2024, the NBK cut the policy rate by 25bp to 14.50% and provided slightly more dovish guidance. While the rhetoric remained cautious and the NBK expects monetary conditions to remain moderately tight "for a long time", it signalled that a further slowdown of the stable part of CPI "shall create room for further cautious base rate cuts", versus communication in the previous statement that "the space for further easing is limited". The NBK started its easing cycle in August 2023 with a 25bp cut to 16.50%, and since then has delivered five 25-50bp cuts, and then stayed on hold in April 2024. This gradual easing is warranted by high pro-inflationary risks and unanchored inflation expectations. Despite steady deceleration in inflation (to 8.5% y/y in May 2024 from 21.3% y/y in February 2023), inflation expectations have remained elevated. They picked up again in April (by 1.9pp to 16.1%), driven by higher food prices, ongoing reform of utility prices and uncertainty around further developments of northern regions afected by floods. Additional pro-inflationary factors include fiscal expansion and rising inflation pressure in Russia. Given higher oil prices, the NBK revised its average oil price projection for 2024 from \$80/bbl to \$85bbl and the C/A deficit to 2.5% of GDP (from 3.9% of GDP). GDP growth and inflation forecasts for 2024 remained unchanged at 3.5-4.5% y/y and 7.5-9.5% y/y, respectively. We expect the NBK to continue easing at a 25-50bp clip and would not rule out the possibility of pauses in the cycle if inflation increases in H2 24.

## Nigeria: Inflation hiccup

Michael Kafe, Barclays, UK | Andreas Kolbe, Barclays, UK | Stella Cridge, Barclays, UK

Inflation has exceeded expectations, leading to a cost of living crisis that has resulted in a temporary return of government interventionism.

• Credit: Nigeria's spread performance has trailed the broader African and EM HY sovereign market YTD. We believe this leaves upside for further performance, although the reform path has been bumpy and less consistent than investors may have hoped. New bond supply seems likely to materialize at some stage this year, possibly before summer, if and when a window of opportunity opens. We would expect such issuance to be focused on the 10y part of the curve and would consider it supportive for shorter-maturity tenors in particular. We maintain our Overweight rating on Nigeria sovereign credit and recommend buying NGERIA 2028s and 2029s in particular. Among corporates, we are constructive on the outlook for IHS (Overweight), the largest market of which is Nigeria, and see multiple potentially positive triggers ahead from the resolution of governance concerns at the upcoming AGM (on Friday, 28 June), asset sales (\$0.5-1.0bn targeted) and the resolution of negotiations around contracts with core customer MTN.

Government intervenes to deal with cost of living crisis. Following concerns about belowbudget incoming revenues over the first two months of the year, as well as a worse inflation and interest rate environment than expected, the government decided to introduce a six-month Accelerated Stabilization and Advancement Plan (ASAP)<sup>36</sup> to help provide some support. This included the suspension of VAT on select items and the subsidisation of transportation costs for low-income households to help address the cost of living crisis, as well as the introduction of tax exemptions for repatriated export proceeds and a relaxation of restrictions on the use of export proceeds to help support foreign exchange inflows. At the same time, labour unions have put the authorities under pressure to increase the minimum wage from NGN30,000/month to NGN250000/month, though the government has indicated willingness to pay only 62,000/ month. Against this backdrop, we revise our fiscal metrics and now expect the Federal government deficit to come in at 3.8% of GDP in 2024 (previously 3%) and 3.7% in 2025 (2.8% previously).

Nigeria's GDP grew 3% y/y in Q1 24, above our forecast of 2.3% y/y and down from 3.5% y/y in Q4 23. Agriculture , which accounts for about a quarter of economic activity, slumped from 2.1% y/y in Q4 23 to 0.2% y/y in Q1 24, while industrial activity, buoyed by clearly unsustainable 12% y/y growth in the oil sector, decelerated from 3.9% y/y to 2.2% y/y. Activity in the services sector remained relatively stable, growing around 4% y/y. Taking the higher than expected Q1 24 outcome into consideration and marking to market our forecast yields a GDP growth of 2.6% for the year, slightly higher than our existing forecast of 2.5%. However, given that the CBN tightened policy by 150bp more than we had anticipated, and that strike activity has intensified, we shave 20-30bp of our H2 24 GDP forecast. This takes our 2024 GDP projection back to 2.5%, with the risks skewed to the downside, as the country’s trade unions have threatened to continue to down tools if their wage demands are not met. We keep our 2025 growth forecast at 3% for now, and expect GDP to expand 3.3% in 2026.

We still expect inflation to peak at 35.0% y/y in July; however, we look for a slightly stickier path thereafter. This is because the currency has been somewhat weaker than we had expected, as, the CBN did not back up monetary tightening with the macro prudential measures that we had anticipated (see Nigeria MPC Review: Tactically decisive – watch NOP, 28 February 2024). This, together with sharp increases in electricity tarifs, lifts our short-term inflation profile.

Looking further out, however, we still expect CPI to fall meaningfully from August, as powerful positive base efects kick in from the sharp rise in domestic fuel prices and significant FX weakness as the Tinubu administration embarked on fuel subsidy and FX reforms last year. We expect CPI to close the year at 26.5% y/y (previously 25% y/y), and to average 31.2% for the year (previously 30.7%), while we expect inflation to average 18.2% in 2025 (previously 17%). With the peak in inflation so close, on our estimates, we believe the CBN is done hiking. We expect policy easing to commence by November, and forecast a year-end policy rate of 24.50%. Further easing will likely ensue in next year, and we forecast an end-2025 policy rate of around 18%.

## Poland: Brand new day

Ercan Erguzel, Barclays, UK | Marek Raczko, Barclays, UK | Andreas Kolbe, Barclays, UK

Robust real wage growth almost at 10% y/y was the primary driver of private consumption growth in Q1 24. We maintain our growth forecasts unchanged at 2.9% y/y for 2024 and 3.9% y/y for 2025. On 16 April, Poland received an additional €6.3bn from the RRF and we estimate it will get c€21bn of additional EU funding in the remainder of year. We see no fiscal policy tightening in the next 12 months due to higher military spending and presidential elections in May 2025. Highlighting inflationary risks in H2 24, we believe the NBP is likely to remain on hold at 5.75% until Q2 25.

• Credit: We think that Poland USD bonds screen as relatively attractive in a global single-A USD bond context, especially in the 10y sector. Improved EU relations and funding should cap supply needs. We recommend buying POLAND \$34s, which could see increased demand from global investors seeking to diversify away from other EM regions in this rating bucket (eg, the GCC), even though contagion from the political volatility in the euro area could be a deterrent to this in the near term and fiscal and geopolitical risks remain for the medium term.

• FX and rates: The global environment, due to economic divergence between the US and EU, and increased political uncertainty amid snap French elections, should continue to put pressure on the PLN, which we thus expect to underperform versus the USD. But we continue to see the PLN as a regional outperformer on the back of a hawkish NBP, EU transfers, and conversion of hard-currency sovereign issuances. We also expect some of the political premium induced by the French elections to fade over time. We expect the NBP to remain on hold in 2024, but hawkish communication may keep front-end rates under pressure, especially when inflation accelerates after the summer. In our view, the bar for any policy tightening is very high, hence any move to the topside in Polish rates would be limited. We remain more constructive on the belly and back-end rates, as core inflation continues to decrease. However, core inflation may remain more persistent should local consumption stay strong. Long-end rates remain highly correlated with the global market, the US in particular, but at these levels should ofer value once the US market stabilizes. Finally, we see risks to Polish ASW as skewed to the downside, as non-residents' ownership of PolGBs is low.

Domestic consumption-driven recovery. Economic growth accelerated to 0.5% q/q SA in Q1 24, up from 0% in the previous quarter. Relatively stronger growth was supported by private and public consumption growth at 2.1% and 1.9% q/q. It was the strongest private consumption since Q1 22. Robust real wage growth at almost 10% y/y was the primary driver of private consumption. In the remainder of year, we expect growth to accelerate with the help of the ongoing recovery in private consumption and normalisation of investment, which contributed -1.6pp to GDP growth in Q1 24, following stronger funding from the EU. That said, we expect manufacturing activity to remain relatively weaker in the reminder of year. Against this backdrop, we maintain our growth forecasts unchanged at 2.9% y/y for 2024 and 3.9% y/y for 2025.  Following the €5.1bn disbursement from the RRF funds in December 2023, Poland received an additional €6.3bn on 16 April from the RRF, of which €3.6bn was loans and €2.7bn grants. Thus, Poland has absorbed a total amount of €11.4bn so far from the RRF, compared to a total allocation of €60bn. Additionally, there is €75bn of normal budgetary funds allocated to the country for the next seven years, meaning €10.7bn on average each year. It is important to highlight that Poland pencilled in PLN40bn (€9.3bn) from the EU RRF funds for 2024 and YTD flows have already exceeded this. We would expect an additional inflow of c.€21bn from the EU funds (RRF and budget funds) in the remainder of the year. The confirmation of ending of the Article 7 procedure should accelerate the flow of funds in H2 24<sup>37</sup> .

No sign of fiscal consolidation on the horizon. The budget deficit was up sharply to PLN40bn in the first four months of the year, from PLN10bn in the same period a year ago. Deterioration in the budget was expected following a major increase in the deficit target to PLN184bn in 2024, up from PLN86bn in 2023. Election-related policies announced last year and elevated defence spending that is likely to exceed 4% of GDP at the end of this year should mean the deterioration in fiscal performance will continue. In terms of financing, stronger inflows from the EU in addition to robust demand for domestic and external debt issuance have made it easier to finance the budget deficit.

A cyclical acceleration in CPI in H2 24. After peaking at 18.4% y/y in February 2023, CPI dropped steadily to 2.8% in February 2024 and has remained in line with the target band (2.5% +/- 1%) since then. However, this is unlikely to be sustained. First, the contribution from food to headline CPI dropped to 0.8pp in April, well below historical averages and is unlikely to continue. Likewise, the contribution from energy to CPI is at -0.2pp as of April but the end of energy subsidies will add around 2pp to headline CPI in H2 24, in our view. Third, services CPI remains sticky at over 6% y/y and strong labour market conditions should keep services CPI well above the headline in the remainder of year. On a more positive note, the stronger PLN helped to bring down tradable goods CPI to lower levels. All in all, we expect CPI to accelerate sharply to c5% y/y by July and remain between 5% and 5.5% y/y in H2 24. The NBP has been the most dovish central bank in the region over the past two years in terms of ex-post real policy rates. This year, however, is diferent. Citing potential risks from fiscal policies on inflation in addition to strong wage growth, the NBP made a radical shift in its policy stance at the January meeting and signalled that it would remain on hold over the remainder of year, according to its baseline scenario. This communication has remained intact so far this year. On the flip side, following downside surprises in CPI prints, the NBP members have stopped signalling potential rate hikes and/or quantitative tightening measures. All in all, we see the NBP remaining on hold at 5.75% until Q2 25.

## Romania: Rebalancing delayed to 2025

Ercan Erguzel, Barclays, UK | Andreas Kolbe, Barclays, UK | Marek Raczko, Barclays, UK

Ahead of two critical elections in Q4 24, we see further expansion in the budget deficit and limited improvement in the CA balance and inflation, meaning limited room for rate cuts. Despite large twin deficits, we believe rating institutions are likely to maintain IG ratings on the country for at least another year should political stability persist.

• Credit: We maintain an Overweight rating on Romania sovereign credit. While fundamental improvements have been slow, we think the likely stable political outlook and anchored IG ratings, at least in the near to medium term, make valuations attractive in a global EM IG sovereign context and relative to regional peers, including lower-rated Serbia.

• FX: In the near term we continue to expect the NBR to manage the EURRON around current levels. The NBR treats the exchange rate as a main anti-inflationary anchor, so recent fasterthan-expected disinflation has opened the door for some RON devaluation in the medium term once inflation returns to around the NBR target. The RON screens as the most overvalued currency in the CE-4 region and the economy is sufering from the twin-deficit problem. Hence, we expect the RON to weaken in the long term to help close the CA gap.

Growth remains stronger than regional peers. GDP growth accelerated to 1.6% y/y in Q1 24, up from 1.1% y/y in the previous quarter. Household consumption and public consumption were the main drivers of growth, with strong performances of 5.1% y/y and 3.1% y/y, respectively. Regarding household consumption, a strong labour market and wage growth are the main drivers. The unemployment rate remains close to historical lows at 5.1% in April, down from 5.4% a year ago. Likewise, nominal and real wage growth remain high at c.14% and c.7%, respectively. Regarding public consumption, expansionary fiscal policy continue in the election year of 2024.

Growing budget deficit. In the first four months of the year, the consolidated general government deficit reached 3.5% of 12M trailing GDP, up from -1.7% on average in the previous five years. Although there was some improvement on the revenues front, there was a major deterioration in expenditures. Capital expenditures and higher interest payments on public debt were the main factors leading to higher expenditures. As there are two critical elections in Q4 24, it is not realistic to expect an improvement in fiscal performance in the remainder of year. Against this backdrop, we expect a deterioration in the budget deficit to 6.0% of GDP in 2024, up from 5.6% in 2023. The risks are clearly skewed to the upside, in our view.

Limited improvement in C/A deficit. While other economies made good progress in their current account positions, either reducing deficits or moving into surpluses in 2023, the improvement for Romania was very limited, from a €26bn (9.1% of GDP) deficit in 2022 to only €22.7bn (c7% of GDP) in 2023. This situation remains unchanged in Q1 24 with the 12M trailing C/A deficit showing limited improvement to 6.7% of GDP. We are of the view that relatively strong growth and FX policy are behind the deterioration in the C/A position. However, strong funding inflows from the EU are delaying the adjustment process to a future date.

Limited improvement in inflation too. Similar to trends in budget balance and C/A balance, the improvement in inflation dynamics were limited in Romania compared to its peers in the region. Despite stability in RON and the sharp drop in food CPI of 1.2% y/y from 18.7% y/y a year ago, headline CPI remains high at 5.1% y/y and services CPI is still near double digits at 9.3% y/y as of May. The relatively limited improvement in inflation, despite a big currency contribution due to RON stability, is primarily related to stronger domestic demand and expansionary fiscal policy, in our view.

Source: Haver Analytics, Barclays Research

...so limited/no room for rate cuts. Following limited improvement in inflation and the widening fiscal deficit, the NBR has been on hold while peers delivered some cuts. That said, the policy stance was looser than implied by 7% due to excess RON liquidity in the system. We expect two 25bp cuts, coming in July and August, ahead of two critical elections in Q4 24. However, the risks are clearly to the upside (ie, lower than 50bp cuts in total in the remainder of year).

The recent elections signal continuation of the grand coalition. In the EU parliamentary elections, the grand coalition members, PNL and PSD, gained c49% of the votes<sup>38</sup> . More importantly, support for the coalition members was stronger at 63% in total in the local elections, held on the same day. If they are able to maintain similar levels of public support until Q4 24, we are likely to see continuation of the grand coalition in the new term. Despite large twin deficits, we believe rating agencies are likely to maintain IG ratings on the country for at least another year should political stability remain in place.

## Russia: Above potential

Zalina Alborova, Barclays, UK | Marek Raczko, Barclays, UK

Despite signs of moderating, the Russian economy continues to overheat, supported by fiscal stimulus, while the significant lack of labour force is still the main constraint on the supply side. We expect sticky inflation to keep rates higher for longer, but see the larger fiscal deficit as manageable. The external balance remains in surplus, but geopolitical uncertainty and further dificulties in trade payments skew the risks to the downside.

• FX: The RUB continues to be driven mainly by Russian trade and global USD valuations. Strong local demand in Russia, which leads to elevated imports, continues to put pressure on the RUB. In addition, prolonged broad USD strength should lift USDRUB higher. On the flip side, we expect the CBR to remain hawkish in an attempt to curb domestic consumption, and RUB weakness to limit Russian inflation.

Resilient economic activity, upward revision of growth forecasts. In Q1 24, the Russian economy continued overheating, accelerating from 4.9% y/y in Q4 23 to 5.4% q/q in Q1 24, while the CBR estimates that growth would be 4.5-4.8% y/y if adjusted for the leap year. Economic growth continued to be supported by fiscal spending, including state orders in sectors related to defence industry, social transfers to households, and military payments to mobilised citizens and their families. Alongside this, labour market conditions remained very tight: amid significant labour shortages, the unemployment rate in April stayed at a historical low of 2.6% (seasonally adjusted) and real wage growth reached 12.9% y/y. This kept credit growth elevated despite high interest rates. High-frequency indicators for April and May show some economic moderation (mainly in services), but overall growth and domestic demand remained relatively strong, outpacing the capacity of supply to expand. Industrial production growth, driven by a weaker mining & quarrying sector, eased to \~4.0% y/y in March-April, while the manufacturing PMI picked up to 54.4 in May (above 50 since May 2022) and job creation accelerated at its fastest rate in 26.5 years<sup>39</sup> . But while overall sentiment remained positive and above historical averages, business confidence dropped to a nine-month low and input cost growth reached its fastest pace since December 2023. Given better-than-expected data for Q1 24, as well as April and May, we revised our GDP forecasts by 1.3pp for 2024 to 3.3% y/y, and by 0.9pp for 2025 to 2.1% y/y.

Larger but manageable fiscal deficit. In 5m 2024, spending of the federal budget grew 18.9% y/y. Yet, the deficit narrowed to RUB983bn, or 0.5% of GDP, almost RUB2trn less than in January-May 2023, driven by 34.1% y/y growth of non-oil revenues (due mainly to VAT and strong economic activity, as well as non-tax one-of payments to the budget) and 73.5% y/y growth in oil & gas revenues (on the back of higher oil prices) . With better-than-expected revenues and an additional RUB520bn in spending, the MinFin adjusted its 2024 budget deficit estimate by 0.3pp to 1.1% of GDP, or by RUB0.6bn to RUb2.1trn. We forecast a deficit of 1.5% of GDP this year and 1.0% in 2025, while recently announced tax changes<sup>40</sup> should bring the federal budget \~RUb2.6trn in additional revenues. Domestic borrowing and transfers from the NWF to continue to be the main sources to finance the deficit, while high interest rates, which have significantly increased the cost of borrowing, and geopolitical uncertainty pose downside risks for oil & gas exports.

Sticky inflation: fiscal stimulus, wage growth and volatile RUB outweigh the efect of high interest rates. The CBR has kept the key rate on hold at 16% since December 2023, while

inflation has accelerated from 7.4% y/y back then to 8.3% y/y in May. Despite high interest rates, credit growth remained elevated for both households and corporates, and domestic demand stayed strong. Fiscal expansion is keeping consumer spending high, while both deposits and loans have been growing. Meanwhile, elevated real wage growth, driven by significant shortages in the workforce, have contributed high spending rates. and the positive output gap has further exacerbated inflationary pressures. Finally, RUB volatility is keeping import prices high and inflation expectations above historical averages (in May, household inflation expectations picked up by 0.7pp to 11.7% y/y, while for corporates it remained at 19.6%). We expect inflation to peak in July (indexation of public transport and utility tarifs in June and July should keep the headline slightly above 8% y/y) and gradually ease to 5.7% y/y by year-end.

CBR to keep monetary conditions tight for longer. Despite ongoing price pressures, the CBR stayed on hold in June, but it delivered more hawkish rhetoric, the statement noting that: 1) the bank "holds open the prospect of increasing the key rate at its upcoming meeting"; and 2) monetary conditions will remain tight for longer than expected in the April forecast. At the press conference after the MPC meeting, CBR Governor Elvira Nabiullina noted that a significant rate hike is possible in July if inflation pressures do not ease. She added that there were more votes for a rate hike than at the April meeting, while the board discussed either staying on hold or hiking 100-200bp. The CBR estimates that the recent acceleration in inflation was driven largely by one-of factors, namely the indexation of tarifs on communication services and higher prices on domestic cars. It also highlighted that the balance of risks had shifted further "towards proinflationary", which it associated mainly with an overheating economy, high inflation expectations and changes in the terms of trade. Higher income taxes recently announced by the MinFin that come into force in 2025 will be used to finance higher fiscal spending, so the CBR views the implications of this for inflation as neutral. The bank expects inflation to return the 4% target by end-2025, but it did not include updated inflation projections for 2024. We think a 100bp hike remains in the pipeline (our base case for the next meeting) unless inflation pressures ease faster than expected. That said, we would not rule out a 200bp hike if permanent components of inflation continue to accelerate.

The publication of the summary discussion of the MPC is scheduled for 20 June, while the next MPC meeting is scheduled for 26 July.
