## Saudi Arabia: IEA issues stark warning

Brahim Razgallah, Barclays, UK | Andreas Kolbe, Barclays, UK | Stella Cridge, Barclays, UK | Luc Roberts, Barclays, UK

The Kingdom's oil policy is facing medium-term challenges amid expected peak global energy demand and mega projects' large funding needs. Domestic banks have increasingly emerged as key providers of FX liquidity, mirroring a rapid increase in their foreign liabilities.

• Credit: We believe that the move lower in oil prices, recurrent bond issuance from Saudi entities in the context of higher fiscal and capex funding needs and the lingering concerns about a potential escalation of hostilities at Israel's northern border will continue to weigh on KSA spreads. Moreover, reduced demand from Kuwaiti entities for Saudi bonds if the debt law gets passed (see Kuwait chapter above) could further adversely afect the supply-demand balance in the medium-term. We hence downgrade KSA sovereign credit to Underweight from Market Weight and recommend a switch from KSA 3.25% 2030s into ADGB 2030s. We also recommend buying KSA 5y CDS. Cash spreads have underperformed CDS spreads recently, partly driven by the sharp move lower in UST yields, to which cash prices have struggled to adjust. KSA 5y CDS has returned to trading with a significant negative basis to similar-maturity cash spreads and has outperformed Qatar and ADGB CDS, as well as the broader CDX EM IG index over the past few weeks. We rate GCC Corporates Underweight, given tight spreads relative to the wider EEMEA complex. We have a Market Weight rating on Aramco, given broadly flat trading levels to the KSA sovereign, where we see fair value, while fundamentals remain robust. We prefer the bonds of QatarEnergy (OW), amid a supportive technical backdrop, and perceive valuations as attractive relative to other quasi-sovereigns in the region, and to the Qatar sovereign.

Oil policy faces litmus test. The Kingdom's long-standing role of swing producer has been pivotal in stabilising global oil markets over the past several decades, even when it was at the expense of higher growth volatility at home. Yet, in recent years, the Kingdom's oil policy has shifted from the medium term to near-term support to oil prices. As the OPEC+ deal has been extended much longer than expected after the pandemic, the Kingdom has steadily lost market share, which has fallen to 10.9% in February from 13.9% in September 2022. This sharply contrasts with previous periods (eg, 2014 and 1986), when the authorities actively competed for market share, accepting a temporary hit to the economy. In contrast to past experience, the global energy transition is challenging the Kingdom's oil policy trade-of. For instance, the International Energy Agency (IEA) recently warned that global oil markets may post a large oil surplus, the likes of which we have only seen during the COVID pandemic. According to the IEA, global oil demand should peak in 2029, and then start to contract to 105.6mb/d in 2030, when oil production capacity will increase to 113.8mb/d.

FIGURE 27. Non-oil growth normalizing
![](images/05538610f36b6cccff03d3f8878daf94fa0a32e6971933733e2e9be7c5f20d1e.jpg)
Source: National authorities, Barclays Research

FIGURE 28. Expats drive higher wage growth
![](images/b3e478adb08e5b0cf458b4e62578c025f130d3d9b5a52667938cb21052f71d13.jpg)
Source: National authorities, Barclays Research

Economy has been resilient thanks to large capex commitments. Large swings in oil production and government revenues have weighed on the non-hydrocarbon sector, which has been gradually decelerating since the pandemic (Figure 27). Private sector demand remained resilient but on a downtrend, decelerating to 3.4% y/y in Q1 24. Encouragingly, real narrow money supply growth shifted into modest positive territory in February, for the first time since mid-2022, while point of sales transactions have remained on a downtrend for the past three years, decelerating to 2.7% y/y in April. This contrasts with broadly stable loan growth to the private sector at 10.8% in January-April and a rebound in wage growth to 5.7% y/y in Q3 23, driven in particular by skilled foreign labour (Figure 28). In general, employment increased by 3.3mn over the past two years, but employment growth has been decelerating across public and private sectors, and across Saudi and expat labour. Importantly, labour force participation of the indigenous population has levelled of, decelerating by a modest 1.2pp to 51.3% in Q4 23, led primarily by an almost 2pp drop in male employment last year, while female participation remained stable at 36%, well above 2030 Vision's target of 30%.

Banks' growing exposure to funding gap. The Kingdom's external surplus narrowed sharply to \$34.1bn in 2023, well below the \$151bn reported in 2022, primarily on the back of lower crude exports led by the OPEC+ agreement. While merchandise exports fell by \$89bn last year, imports increased by \$19.6bn, reflecting the elasticity to ongoing mega projects. Despite the steady rebound in oil prices since the global pandemic, SAMA reserves have remained range-bound close to \$450bn, pointing to the structural break in the relationship between hydrocarbon revenues and oil prices. This has closely mirrored the growth in the Public Investment Fund, as the major recipient of incremental savings from hydrocarbon exports instead of SAMA as per past experience. We previously highlighted how domestic banks have become the marginal providers of FX liquidity in the domestic economy, with mega projects' financing needs fostering an unprecedented increase in their foreign liabilities.

## South Africa: De facto grand coalition?

Michael Kafe, Barclays, UK | Andreas Kolbe, Barclays, UK | Marek Raczko, Barclays, UK | Stella Cridge, Barclays, UK

The ANC's majority has slipped significantly, forcing it to work with the opposition. The ANC and DA account for more than 50% of parliament, strategically positioning the pair to fend of controversial and potentially unproductive left-leaning policies.

• Credit: We believe that peak uncertainty has now passed for South Africa sovereign and corporate spreads. On the sovereign side, however, spreads are broadly back to the preelection tights. While there is likely no near-term catalyst for spreads to move wider, in the medium term, political uncertainties and a challenged fundamental trajectory leave the balance of risks tilted to the downside, in our view. We hence maintain an Underweight rating on South Africa sovereign credit. For corporates, we expect the new government to continue to pursue growth-enhancing reforms in the power and transport sectors. We also expect implicit state support assumptions to remain intact for the quasi-sovereigns, albeit with a greater focus on private sector participation under the GNU. Sasol spreads widened during the election period (standalone and versus SOAF), leaving it our preferred exposure (OW). Eskom's spread to sovereign has been more stable, at c.180bp, with Transnet decompressing slightly; policy continuity should anchor quasi spreads, and we have Market Weight ratings on both Eskom and Transnet.

• FX: The ZAR has rallied on the back of the creation of an ANC-DA-IFP coalition, which is widely perceived as very market-friendly. The risk premium commanded by the USDZAR compressed to 2.5%, from 10% earlier in the year, so we see little room for it to rally further. In our view, the ZAR's positive outlook will be outweighed by USD strength, to which the ZAR has a significant negative beta. But the ZAR should still outperform CEE currencies, in our view, as it should regain its carry appeal.

• Rates: We expect the SARB to deliver 50bp worth of cut this year, followed by another 50bp next year, amid falling inflation and better risk sentiment toward ZAR and South African assets. The market is pricing in only 28bp worth of cuts this year, as investors are likely refraining from pricing a front-loaded easing cycle when the Fed stance is still uncertain. Consequently, we see more value in receiving belly rates and we recommend receiving 5y ZAR IRS. Longer-dated SAGBs rallied rapidly after formation of the new ruling coalition, and we expect this optimistic stance to persist.

The ruling African National Congress (ANC) lost its majority for the first time in 30 years, slipping to 40.2% of the national vote from 57.5%. This has compelled it to coalesce with a select number of opposition parties, including the Democratic Alliance (21.81% of the vote), the Inkatha Freedom Party (3.85%) and the Patriotic Alliance (2.1%), in what it refers to as a Government of National Unity (GNU). Suficient consensus exists within the GNU when parties representing 60% of the National Assembly (NA) reach agreement on anything, and such a consensus is required to make amendments to its original statement of intent. We note that the ANC and DA account for some 62% of the NA, strategically positioning them as a de facto grand coalition that could dictate future policy. With the populist EFF and the MK not having signed up for the GNU, we believe that markets are likely to view the arrangement positively for now. Yet, while a GNU certainly invokes a sense of national unity and is likely to, in the short term, calm nerves after what was a rather divisive election, over the medium term, we see risks of internal disagreements and social dissatisfaction once debate on and implementation of the potentially painful reforms that that government has shied away from begin to crystallize (for more details, please see ' South Africa: ' ANC + DA would out-vote EFF in GNU', 7 June 2024). Even so, we believe that the election outcome presents South Africa with a unique opportunity for a macro reset. Were the GNU to pursue proven macro reforms that help grow the economy and deliver socio-economic dividends, it would be reasonable to expect the ANC to claw back part of its voter base.

With regards to growth, we had expected Q124 GDP to print at 0.2% q/q (0.8% y/y), while the remaining quarters of the year average some 0.4% q/q. Yet, it contracted by 0.1% q/q, led by a broad-based contraction in mining, manufacturing, electricity production, construction government services, and transport & communications, ofset only in part by a strong rebound in agricultural activity. Expenditure-side metrics also show that every single category (consumption, government spending, investment and the external sector) contracted.  We expect activity in mining and manufacturing to be supported by the improved power situation, as should construction, transport and communications. From a demand-side perspective, we do not believe that the sharp 9% decline in imports is likely to be sustained in Q2 24, especially if one considers that commodity export prices have remained relatively firm while oil import prices have declined. We therefore forecast a rebound to 0.7% q/q (0.4% y/y) in Q2 24, while the remainder of the year comes in unchanged at 0.4% y/y. This results in a full-year growth rate of 0.8% for 2024 (down from 1% previously), and is due mostly to the carry-over efects of a weak Q1 24 outcome. Our 2025 forecast remains unchanged at 1.5%, followed by 1.8% in 2026.

We now expect May CPI to print at 5.3% y/y, but to decline slightly to 5.2% y/y in June and 5.0% y/y in August thanks to the recent drop in international oil prices and, as a corollary, the regulated domestic price of petrol. Thereafter, positive base efects are likely to push inflation lower, and we expect it to fall through the midpoint of the target band by October 2024, much earlier than our previous expectation of a return to target in mid-2025. With this profile, we believe that the SARB can commence easing at its September meeting, or possibly earlier. We look for 50bp of easing this year, followed by a further 50bp in 2025

Q1 24 current account printed at a 1.2% of GDP deficit, that is much lower than our forecast of 2%, thanks largely to a rather confusing and clearly unsustainable decline in visible imports, which was at odds with monthly SARS data and may have been due to timing diferences between flows and payments, as well as adjustments for seasonality. Export data also appear to be rather high in the quarterly aggregation relative to the underlying monthly data, suggesting that a correction is likely in the quarter(s) ahead. Thus, although commodity prices appear to be consolidating at higher levels than we had expected at the beginning of the year, we maintain our full-year current account forecast at -2% of GDP.

## Tunisia: Election watching

## Brahim Razgallah, Barclays, UK

The outlook for Tunisia remains challenging amid recurrent delays to accelerate structural reforms that have narrowed the available pool of financial support. The IMF visit to conduct Article IV discussions, crucial for a new programme, may be delayed until after the election.

Since the suspension of Parliament and the Constitution in July 2021, Tunisia has experienced a pivotal moment in its lengthy transition, which has led to a broad-based consolidation of powers. This year's presidential elections have thus been a focal point in a protracted period of political and social tensions. The elections need to be held before year-end, according to the Constitution. The Electoral Commission has indicated that the vote may be held between September and October, but the timing will require a presidential decree that could ultimately bring the calendar forward or delay it further. Moreover, the electoral law is based on the 2014 Constitution, which was amended in 2022. It is unclear whether a new electoral law will be introduced, given the tight calendar, despite several contradictions between electoral laws and the amended constitution (eg, the minimum age for presidential candidates set at 36 years by the former, and 40 years by the latter).

FIGURE 32. Real GDP vs peers
![](images/31548d903f3cbbb3c1fa91569bbaa31a9d1b94620ddae292736bbfe8e03c72de.jpg)
Source: IMF, Barclays Research

FIGURE 33. Reserves under pressure
![](images/72c850717f6cf0f7113267832e7aa2ab05165327a4f63ce3db8ca192e8dbf487.jpg)
Source: IMF, BCT, Barclays Research

The elections will shape the country's near-term outlook, in our view. The protracted period of power consolidation has delayed structural reforms, particularly socially unpopular measures. IMF discussions have thus stalled and any progress on resuming programme discussions will first require a new Article IV to be finalized due to the lengthy period following the Staf Level Agreement in October 2022. As the backdrop for presidential campaigns is highly charged, an IMF team visit ahead of the vote is therefore a high hurdle to clear. After a broad-based consolidation of powers, President Said is likely to seek a second mandate, in our view, although he has not oficially made any announcement yet. It remains unclear who might be a credible contender for the job amid weaker opposition parties, broad-based arrests and uncertainty about potential amendments to the electoral law<sup>41</sup> .

## External funding between uncertainty and moral hazard

The importance of Tunisia's presidential elections in the fall is compounded by recent European elections, which have led to significant gains for far right parties. Delays to the IMF deal put the onus of the funding gap on the EU, following the historical 2023 deal supported by Italian PM Giorgia Meloni and European Commissioner Ursula von Leyen. We believe this will anchor already strong EU financial support in the remainder of the year, despite opposition from European Commissioner Nicolas Schmidt following the Socialists' weaker showing in EU parliamentary elections.

The July 2023 memorandum of understanding has pledged about €1bn, but disbursements have been limited compared with the country's large funding needs. For example, the EU transferred €150mn in direct budgetary support in March, versus €5.1bn in external funding in the 2024 budget. EU assistance to Tunisia has amounted to €3.4bn since 2011 (€2.0bn in grants and €1.4bn in soft loans), which is much smaller than the yearly transfers to some CEE countries. This year's funding has therefore come largely from the domestic market, following amendments to the central bank law that allowed one-of direct monetary financing of TND7bn (\$2.2bn).

Direct monetary financing was previously provided in 2020 and 2021, which puts the central bank's independence at risk over the medium term. Still, the new funding line is likely to be used partly this year, as the authorities continue to rely equally on domestic banks to roll syndicated loans. Direct monetary financing helped the government to repay its €850mn eurobond maturity in February, which brought reserves to 104 days of import coverage. While these levels still ofer some bufers, the upcoming repayment of the JPY50bn guaranteed eurobond (\$319mn) in October is followed shortly by a \$1bn maturity in January 2025.

This combination of a further reliance on reserves and delayed IMF talks should thus bring FX coverage to uncomfortably lower levels, pressing the authorities to mobilize alternative sources of funding. While the 2024 budget leaves TND10bn (\$3.2bn) in unidentified funding, it is the dynamics of the external balance that should drive the country's balance of risks through 2025. In fact, the current account deficit narrowed from 8.9% GDP in 2022 to 2.6% last year, helped by a rebound in tourism and a sharp contraction in imports. But such improvement may prove short-lived, in our view.

# Turkiye: Tighter, but probably needs more

Ercan Erguzel, Barclays, UK | Andreas Kolbe, Barclays, UK | Marek Raczko, Barclays, UK | Valentina Stoykova, Barclays, UK| Evgeniia Bystrova, Barclays, UK

Despite higher interest rates, economic growth remains strong owing to a series of factors, including strong wage growth and loose fiscal policy. With the assumptions of limited tightening in the budget balance and continuation of relatively strong real wage growth, we expect CPI to be 46.5% y/y by YE24 and 31% y/y YE25. We do not expect the CBT to start an easing cycle before January 2025.

• Credit: We consider the outlook for Turkey sovereign credit as stable in the near to medium term, with local demand likely remaining an important anchor to valuations and a bufer against volatility. However, at the index level, Turkey sovereign spreads are now c.70bp inside the index average. With long-term macroeconomic and political risks still lingering and likely continued supply in primary markets, we think an Underweight rating is appropriate. High carry in the EEMEA corporate credit context, due to prolific (mainly subs) issuance, stabilising macro conditions, and an expected improvement in NII/NIM in H2 24 are the key drivers of our Overweight rating on Turkiye Financials. Our top picks are AKBNK (OW) and YKBNK (OW), where we see value in the perps. We rate Turkiye Corporates Market Weight, given lower carry than for financials and likely additional supply. We see best value in Turkcell (OW) and Turk Telekom (OW), which we expect to benefit from the disinflation trend and deem as more resilient to consumer spending woes.

• FX and Rates: The TRY was one of the favoured currencies for investors last quarter due to high carry and a very tightly managed nominal exchange rate. However, this prolonged nominal FX stability, coupled with still-significant sequential inflation, has led to further TRY overvaluation in REER terms. Consequently, we expect the authorities to allow some TRY weakening over the summer, as maintaining a fixed exchange rate for long would risk expansion of the current account gap. We still expect the TRY to outperform its forwards in the next quarter. We expect the CBT to remain on hold throughout 2024 to combat still-high inflation and to maintain foreign investment inflows into the TRY. We also expect inflation to remain more persistent than envisaged by the CBT; hence, we see scope for some pressure on Turkish belly rates. Given TRY overvaluation and risks of a sudden stop of portfolio inflows, we see the distribution of risks around TRY forward rates as skewed to the upside, as any significant TRY pressure or premature policy rate cut would push TRY forward rates higher.

Growth remains strong despite slowdown in loan growth. Although the authorities have delivered a major hiking cycle above and beyond the market's expectations, taking the policy rate from 8.5% in May 2023 to 50% currently, there has been no material slowdown in economic activity so far, with Q1 24 growth coming in at 2.4% q/q in seasonally adjusted terms. While some of the improvement in the composition of growth has been driven by stronger support from net exports, this was in part related to a mean-reversion from record high gold imports. Lending growth has slowed materially in the past 12 months thanks to higher interest rates and selective macro-prudential measures, but strong wage growth, the arguably overvalued TRY, loose fiscal policy, and the efect of previously implemented expansionary policies (ie, early retirement for c.2.5mn people) have limited or diluted the efect of policy tightening, in our view. Related to strong wage growth, there are two key developments to monitor in the short and medium term. In the short term, there are some expectations in the local media<sup>42</sup> of an adjustment to the minimum wage in the second half of this year. In the medium term, the hikes for minimum wage earners, pensioners and civil servants for 2025 are expected to be announced in December.

We expect CPI to be at 46.5% by YE2024. High base efects should help lower inflation strongly in July and August. That said, we now expect inflation to finish this year at 46.5% y/y (up from 45.3% in our previous forecast), well above the CBT's forecast and target of 38% y/y. Our inflation forecasts are predicated on USDTRY at 37.6 by year-end 2024 and a further 10% hike in the minimum wage. Aiming to get further support from FX, the authorities have kept the TRY stable in nominal terms over the last two months. We believe this might create some imbalances in addition to supporting domestic demand. TRY has appreciated by 29% in CPI real efective exchange rate (REER) terms and by 40% in PPI REER terms since December 2021. However, as we do not see a major deterioration in the core goods balance trend, we believe the authorities feel comfortable in seeking further support from a stable TRY. We maintain our call for no change in policy rates until January 2025. The risks are skewed to the upside in the short term and downside in the medium term. If a minimum wage hike for H2 24 is announced sometime in June, the CBT might consider a 250bp hike. On the flip side, if inflation falls below 50% y/y before YE24 and sequential CPI moderates towards 1% m/m, the CBT might consider starting its easing cycle earlier, in November or December.

We are not particularly positive about the inflation outlook in the medium term (out to 2025). We regard post post-2002 as a good benchmark, ie, when Turkiye managed to bring inflation down to single digits from c.70%. Four important characteristics of this period were: 1) super tight monetary policy, with the ex-post real policy rate at almost 10% over 2002-10 super tight fiscal policy with consecutive years of primary budget surpluses over 2004-13 except during the Global Financial Crisis in 2009 and 2010; 3) a series of structural reforms helping to bring longer-term financial inflows, such as FDI; and 4) TRY remaining stable in nominal terms at 1.6-1.8 thanks mainly to the significant depreciation of 55% during the 2001 economic crisis. To achieve the same factors now, we think the authorities could meet the first two relatively easily by: a) holding the policy rate stable when inflation falls below 50% (not our base case); and b) delivering major fiscal tightening with the announcement of the Medium Term Programme in September 2024.

Limited (geo)political risks ahead. Following a busy period politically, with elections in 2023 and earlier in 2024, Turkiye has now entered a period of political calm, free of elections until 2028, which should bode well for macro-economic and financial stability. That said, there are discussions about a potential revision to the constitution, and if the government is unable to reach a constitutional majority of 400 MPs out of 600 in total, this might require a public referendum. We are of the view that President Erdogan will try to make a constitutional changes through a grand coalition with other political parties, including the main opposition CHP, as he would then be able to avoid a public referendum. We do not expect significant changes to Turkiye's foreign policy following the outcome of the US Presidential election. Turkiye is currently in the phase of rapprochement with western allies following some tension in recent years. We expect this positive trend to continue.

## Ukraine: Prevailing status quo

Brahim Razgallah, Barclays, UK | Andreas Kolbe, Barclays, UK | Stella Cridge, Barclays, UK

Ukraine's short- and medium-term outlook is highly clouded by uncertainty, likely favouring the status quo for domestic elections, peace talks and debt negotiations, with progress unlikely before the US elections.

• Credit: We retain a Market Weight rating on UKRAIN sovereign credit. The separate communications by the Ukrainian authorities and the bondholder committee, released on Monday, 17 June, point to a number of diferences that would need to be overcome to ultimately reach a restructuring agreement, including: the amount of coupon payments Ukraine would be able to make in the front years (during the IMF programme); the size of notional haircuts and the structure of contingent recovery value instruments related to this; and the question of whether any post-restructuring bonds would exclude the existing GDP warrants from cross default (possibly in light of the need to embark on restructuring negotiations for the warrants, too). Based on our calculations and assuming a 15% exit yield, Ukraine’s original sovereign proposal would likely result in recovery values in the low to mid-20s, and the amended base structure (which does not yet account for the 4th IMF review, according to the statement) in values in the mid- to higher 20s. These numbers are not far from current market prices of Ukraine bonds. Hence, any negotiated solution around this proposal would likely leave upside, especially if the economy develops positively and an “upside scenario” in the contingent bonds/recovery instrument gets triggered (albeit the details of this would still need to be negotiated). However, we believe that the degree of uncertainty about Ukraine’s future economic prospects and the timeline and chances of success of a restructuring remain high. Given this, and the opportunity costs of a further prolonged non-payment period, we still see risks as two-sided for Ukraine bonds at current prices and maintain a Market Weight rating. We rate the Ukraine Corporate sector Underweight. While companies continue to prove resilient to the ongoing war, we see mounting challenges going into H2 24, including a potentially less supportive political backdrop (which can influence aid, and FX availability, for corporates). Corporates are also exposed to the ongoing efect of multiple assaults on infrastructure in Ukraine (power, transport). Mitigating factors include long-standing relationships with bondholders, which can help address upcoming debt maturities independent of sovereign debt considerations.

Navigating the clouds of uncertainty. War fatigue, compounded with heightened uncertainty about a heavy election calendar, have favoured the status quo, considerably diluting any progress from the June Ukraine peace summit in Switzerland, to which Russia has not been invited. We believe peace prospects remain elusive through next year, thus extending martial law and consequently presidential elections after Zelensky's term has oficially ended in 2024. The president still commands a more than 60% approval rating in recent polls<sup>43</sup> , but his popularity has fallen sharply from 90% in early 2022, pointing to the role of mutually reinforcing domestic power competition and the state of war. In other words, any peace deal that may entail territorial concessions to Russia would potentially prove detrimental to Zelensky's popularity.

Since 2022, Ukraine has become closely reliant on EU and US financial support, making elections on both sides of the Atlantic centre to the country's medium-term outlook, which should leave the status quo in place for longer. The rise of far right parties in recent EU parliamentary elections may somewhat weaken the unwavering commitment to fund the Ukrainian war, but the likely re-election of EU Commissioner von der Leyen<sup>44</sup>  and the EU's proximity to the conflict should anchor future funding, in our view, notably after gaining EU accession status in December. Still, the US election is the biggest source of uncertainty in the near-term outlook due to the wide policy divergence between Biden and Trump. This is thus likely to delay peace talks, plans to use seized Russian assets to fund Ukraine, and an agreement with bondholders, in our view, as highlighted by the still wide gap after failed recent negotiations.

Economy rebounds but volatility remains high. Ukraine's posted a strong rebound in real GDP to 5.3% last year, thanks to the normalisation of economic activity, favourable base efects and ample external financial support. We expect growth to slow to 3.0% this year before modestly rebounding to 3.4% next year. Sequentially, growth has been decelerating (Figure 37), but high frequency indicators point to still-resilient domestic demand, despite recent attacks on its energy sector resulting in supply bottlenecks. Labour markets remain tight, with nearly a third of companies reporting dificulties finding new employees and real wage growth rebounding to nearly 17% y/y in early 2024. Lending to households has also accelerated, to 18.6% y/y, its highest print since April 2022, while real narrow money supply, which tracks economic activity, remains at a two-year high, accelerating to 20% y/y in April. Despite challenging external funding conditions in Q1 24, reconstruction eforts remain strong, with construction permits decelerating to 40% y/y, from a peak of 64.7% in June 2023.

Current account adds to external funding pressure. Despite the negative 2022 shock on goods exports, which nearly halved through April, Ukraine's current account initially shifted to a surplus. Yet, the normalisation of economic activity with the return of refugees and reconstruction eforts has boosted imports, while the changing composition of external financial support from grants to loans has reduced net secondary income by \$12bn from its June 2023 peak. The NBU has announced the easing of several FX restrictions since May, allowing, under certain conditions, for the repatriation of new dividends, repaying debt service, and FX transfers. However, some large corporates like Kernel have communicated that significant restrictions remain in place with regards to servicing debt issued prior to mid-2022 using liquidity held in Ukraine. The current account thus swung into a deficit in 2023, which widened by \$2bn to \$11.3bn in January-April 2024, its widest gap since 2014. Upward pressure on external imbalances should thus pressure central bank reserves further, even after the NBU shifted towards a de facto crawling-peg FX regime. As a result, we believe room for further policy easing will be limited, despite elevated real yields.

## Zambia: Looking up after successful restructuring

Michael Kafe, Barclays, UK | Andreas Kolbe, Barclays, UK

Zambia's debt restructuring exercise is now complete, and we believe that this sets the stage for a pickup in economic activity, especially once the negative efect of recent weather vagaries wears of.

• Credit: Following the successful conclusion of the eurobond restructuring and the rally in bonds leading up to this event, further near-term upside for the new Zambia bonds A and B looks naturally less generous. However, given the macroeconomic context and the relatively short duration of Bond A, with its attractive amortization features, we think Bond A provides attractive short-dated carry, even at yields below 10%. Bond B, in our view, ofers longer-term upside based on our assessment that the 'upside case' is likely to be triggered in 2026 (see SSA credit: Spring in the step, 3 April 2024 and further discussion below). Based on our calculations, the market implies a c.70% probability to the upside scenario being triggered, leaving c.15-20 points of upside in a positive outcome. We maintain an Overweight rating on Zambia sovereign credit and recommend buying Bond B.

Kwacha under pressure. Due largely to delays in the debt restructuring programme, and as a consequence the inflow of international capital including from the IMF, the Zambian kwacha has come under significant pressure, leading to some 450bp of tightening to help rein in accelerating inflation. This, together with a severe drought, has challenged the country’s growth outlook. After contracting at a double-digit pace over the five quarters ending December 2023, activity in agriculture appears set to remain weak this year. Afecting not only the agricultural sector, the dry weather conditions have also impacted the country’s hydro power availability, constraining manufacturing and mining activity in the process. Thus, despite the sharp rally in copper prices, copper export revenues declined in 2023, and have now slipped below nontraditional export proceeds. With imports remaining relatively sticky (driven in part by the need to import maize grain staples), and net service and income payments in structural deficit, Zambia’s current account position has swung back into deficit territory.

Nevertheless, we note that the restructuring process has now been completed following the approval of the government’s consent solicitation on the country’s 2022, 2024 and 2027 eurobonds totalling some USD3bn nominal and USD840mn in PDI. The new, restructured bonds have now started trading, and should help resurrect capital market activity including the creation of fundamental benchmark price points that would hopefully catalyse inward private capital in the period ahead. Thankfully, the IMF has also reached a staf level agreement on the third review of the ECF that should lead to the release of some USD575mn of inflows upon IMF board approval later this month, supported by other multilateral inflows. We note that the size of the programme has also been increased from USD1.3bn to USD1.7bn after the authorities request for additional access was granted. Also, after averaging some 63000mt/month in the first quarter of the three years ending 2022, copper production fell to 48000mt/month in the first quarter of 2023 but appears to have rebounded somewhat to 51000mt/month in the first two months of this year.

The conclusion of debt restructuring should help revive business sentiment, including in copper production. We are confident that this, together with high copper prices, a strong external environment and a likely rebound in Zambia GDP once weather conditions normalise, should result in a triggering of additional coupon flows to bondholders in Bond B, with a relatively high likelihood (see Zambia's debt restructuring: Creativity provides scope for uplift; upgrade to OW, 30 October, 2023 and EM sovereign restructurings: Default lines, 23 May 2024). The recovery in copper should also combine with tighter monetary conditions that help place a lid on imports to shift the current account back into surplus territory before the end of this year.

We forecast a 3.0% of GDP current account surplus that is somewhat lower than the IMF’s 3.7% of GDP forecast for this year, but expect this to reach 5.5% of GDP in 2025 - higher than the IMF's 5.2% of GDP estimate. Inflation has turned up, rising from single digits through the second half of 2022 to a high of 14.7% y/y in May. We expect inflation to peak around 15% y/y by June, and thus expect one final 50bp rate hike, taking terminal rates to 14%. The authorities are likely to maintain a relatively tight stance until inflation falls back into single digits, in 2025, where we look for policy easing of some 350bp.

## Latin America

## When politics call back

From elections to fiscal discussions and controversial reforms, political noise has been the norm across Latin America this year. Either because of that, slower disinflation, the Fed's morehawkish stance, or because they are simply moving closer to the end of their easing cycles, central banks have become more cautious.

Trade Summary | Argentina | Brazil | Central America and the Caribbean | Chile | Colombia | Ecuador | Mexico | Peru | Venezuela

## A resurgence of political themes

There has been a plethora of political noise in Latin America this year. This is partly due to elections in Mexico, Panama, the Dominican Republic, and to a lesser extent, El Salvador. There has also been a lot of noise in Brazil, mainly following a split BCB vote in May that caused market participants to question the future board's credibility and commitment to the center of the inflation target. More recently, there have been increased market concerns about President Lula's approach to fiscal accounts amid the overall perception that MoF Haddad's agenda for increasing tax revenue could be losing support, at the same time that the administration appears to be resisting curbing spending growth. In Colombia, while political noise has been the norm since President Petro won the election, fiscal noise has added to the mix, in an on-and-of manner. However, we see room to outperform the fiscal targets and noise to fade (Colombia: Trying not to miss the forest for the trees).

In Argentina, President Milei was able to show meaningfully positive fiscal results in H1 24, as well as significantly lower inflation. Nevertheless, we believe sustaining these achievements will be challenging. Passing appropriate tax legislation is necessary achieve a more-permanent structural fiscal adjustment and reduce the reliance on transitory measures. The approval of Milei's watered-down omnibus bill reduces political risks. However, the senate did not pass parts of the fiscal package that were necessary for the adjustment to be sustainable. At the time of this writing, the government is negotiating to try to get them passed. In this context, challenges likely will continue given the needed fiscal subsidy reductions (regulated price increases) have been postponed several times. This will be a real test for the government, particularly the population's tolerance for the adjustments needed to end an era of disorderly monetary and fiscal policies. Moreover, Argentina requires a weak real exchange rate as part of the adjustment, including the ability to pay back hard-currency obligations. But the currency has sharply appreciated YTD, adding to the list of challenges.

In Mexico, the unexpected high level of support for the ruling party Morena in the recent general elections (June 2) has generated a lot of attention. The two-thirds constitutional majorities in the Lower House and the Senate that Morena will enjoy have unlocked the possibility that President López-Obrador (AMLO) could pursue the approval of any constitutional reform he

AMLO is focused on the approval of judiciary reform that could trigger rating actions and could reduce the ability of the country to take advantage of nearshoring (Mexico: Doubling down on the judiciary reform).

## We are not done with elections yet

We are still awaiting elections in Venezuela in July, Uruguay in October, and local elections in Brazil, also in October (Figure 1). Between elections and transitions, the region is going to continue on a political news roller coaster for the second half of the year. It is worth highlighting that uncertainty is elevated for the elections in Venezuela on July 28, given that it cannot be regarded as a regular free and fair electoral process. However, the government's strategy to divide and demobilize the opposition has not delivered the results it expected, turning the opposition candidacy of Edmundo Gonzalez into a real challenge for President Maduro, which supports the possibility of a political transition.

## Slower disinflation and more cautious central banks

Inflation has continued to decline, but at a slower pace. Inflation in Mexico and Peru surprised to the downside in May. In the former, the surprise was driven by core CPI. In Peru, it was due to food prices. Nevertheless, the current political noise has created a quite volatile scenario for USDMXN that will probably will last until October when the newly-elected president will take up ofice. As a result, we expect CPI to reflect some FX pass-through in 2025, and we have adjusted our inflation forecast upwards to 3.8% for YE25, from 3.5% previously (Figure 2).

In Chile, headline inflation surprised to the upside in May but core kept falling. Therefore, we expect the BCCh to be comfortable continuing with another 50bp rate cut this month. Nevertheless, inflation will be afected by a reduction in the subsidy for electricity tarifs in the foreseeable future. In this context, along with the fact that the BCCh is moving closer to the end of its easing cycle, will likely reduce the pace of cuts to 25bp. In Brazil, there has been higher than usual uncertainty in headline and food inflation due to large-scale floods in the state of Rio Grande do Sul. Indeed, IPCA inflation in May surprised to the upside, and we revised our 2024 inflation forecast to 3.9%, from 3.7% before, although it remains unclear whether the shocks caused by the floods will fully dissipate in the coming months. In Colombia, disinflation may have slowed in headline CPI, given reduced base efects, but should have been sustained in core CPI.

Central banks have become more cautious, some because of slower disinflation, other, more Fed-sensitive banks because of its more-hawkish stance, but for the majority simply because their easing cycles are closer to ending. Potentially looser fiscal policy as well as politics to a certain extent have played roles in some countries.

In this context, the central banks of Chile (BCCh), as already mentioned, and Peru (BCRP) are probably going to continue to ease but at a slower pace, while the central bank of Colombia (Banrep) can still ease more and even increase the pace of cuts in September, in our view. We now expect Brazil's BCB to leave the Selic rate unchanged at 10.50% on 19 June, interrupting the easing cycle initiated last August when the Selic was at 13.75%. The decision to stand pat would be a response to additional deterioration in medium-term inflation expectations amid market questions about the future BCB board's commitment to the inflation target. Moreover, increased uncertainty on the path of fiscal accounts and the adverse behavior of local assets, especially the BRL, are likely to result in greater caution by the board (we expect a unanimous vote in favor of holding rates).

The central bank of Mexico (Banxico) barely started cutting rates, delivering a 25bp rate cut back in March, before it paused. However, the current political developments are contributing to a weakening of the currency, and with the US election campaign kicking into high gear by September, Banxico may have conducted the shortest easing cycle in history, and likely will not be able to resume easing until the end of the year or into next year. We actually believe rate cuts will not be possible until next year, probably accompanying the Fed (Mexico: AMLO supports judiciary reform proposals- We no longer expect rate cuts this year).

## Hard fiscal endeavors

Countries in the region face an uphill climb in making ends meet, due to enlarged fiscal spending, economic slowdowns, assistance to SOEs, and political challenges. Brazil's latest fiscal update from the government still shows a primary deficit within the target range in 2024, though underperformance risks remain high even if flood aid is not accounted for. The focus now is on compensatory measures to ofset the BRL26bn (0.2% of GDP) cost of extending the payroll tax break. An initial attempt by MoF Haddad to curb PIS/Cofins tax credits was strongly rejected by the business class and was ultimately sent back by the President of Congress, Rodrigo Pacheco, underscoring market concerns about the possible lack of support for Haddad within the administration. Moreover, concerns about the 2025 budget (to be presented on 31 August) are increasing in the absence of measures to curb growth in public spending, particularly in healthcare and education.

Mexico's underspending against the budget so far in 2024 could create conditions for a lower deficit than estimated by the government (5.9% of GDP) or, instead, a chance to replenish the stabilization funds with newly approved reforms that increased funding sources. Nevertheless, the potential inability of the central bank to cut rates due to a more-volatile currency, given increased political risks, could either make it harder for the government to consolidate in 2025 or it might even corner the government into conducting an abrupt deficit reduction in 2025 to restore credibility. There is trust in President-elect Sheinbaum and Finance Minister Ramírez de la O. The volatile issue is the market-unfriendly reform package that AMLO wants to approve in September.

Despite the noise that potential changes to the fiscal rule could generate in Colombia, authorities are signaling no change to the 2024 budget deficit target of 5.3% of GDP. If this is confirmed in the medium-term fiscal framework presentation due on June 14, it should ease the market's biggest concern. Peru may miss its fiscal target for a second year in a row, amid weak economic activity. Petroperu's financial stress poses additional challenges to the sovereign, including the risk of a rating downgrade. Tax collection in Argentina should eventually feel the pinch from its deep recession, but steps toward a more-structural fiscal adjustment could soon materialize in a floor vote in the Senate.

Last, but not least, along with inflation, the focal point on the fiscal arena in Chile is the reduction of the subsidy applied to electricity tarifs during the COVID-19 pandemic. After some delays, this reduction started in June, in order to repay the electric power generation firms the debt that has mounted since the subsidies were implemented. Outside of this issue, the fiscal outlook in Chile should return to business as usual.

## Persistently resilient growth

Two of the countries that posted the largest GDP growth rates last year, Mexico and Brazil, will probably grow the most this year, despite some slowing down. Chile has surprised to the upside, and the apparently sustained increase in copper prices is helping. On the other side of the spectrum, recent economic activity data in Colombia and Peru surprised to the downside. However, in the case of Colombia, the worst seems to be behind it, although Peru is still struggling to come out of a recession.

While we believe monetary easing has been behind the upside surprise in Chile, and likely helped in Brazil (although fiscal spending helped more), this is not necessarily the case in Colombia. However, as the easing cycle advances, we think growth in Colombia could accelerate, converging towards 3% next year. In Mexico, 2023 growth was not driven by monetary policy, but several one-ofs that extended into Q1 this year, and then the distortions from the electoral cycle started to kick in. In Argentina, the economic slowdown continues, and the more the government implements fiscal adjustments, the more economic activity sufers, as it continues on the path to a 4.0% contraction this year. Economic activity in Central America and the Caribbean started the year with mixed trends, but is leaning towards deceleration in the coming months.

We revise our 2024 growth forecasts for Mexico and Ecuador to the downside, and Peru to the upside (Figure 3). High frequency data in Mexico have signaled weaker economic activity in Q2 24. While nearshoring-related industries such as construction continue to boost growth, electoral spending seemed to have had a lower efect, and political uncertainty could add later in the year to slower investment. As a result, we now believe GDP in Mexico will grow 2.4% this year, from 2.6% previously. In Ecuador, a series of concurrent issues such as electricity rationing, as well as fiscal adjustment have had a relevant impact slowing down economic activity, prompting us to revise our 2024 growth forecast downwards to 0.1%, from 2.0% earlier in the year.

In contrast, slightly higher fiscal spending and the authorized pension fund withdrawals have increased Peru's economic dynamism this year, where we now expect the economy to grow 1.9% in 2024, from 1.5% previously. In Brazil, we revised our 2024 GDP growth forecast earlier this month to 2.2%, up from 1.9%, thanks to a strong start to the year amid a tight labor market, rising incomes, and significant fiscal support. An unusual increase in the number of business days this year and other idiosyncratic factors may help mitigate uncertainties caused by floods in the south; at the same time, reconstruction eforts can have a positive efect in growth next year, which we see at 1.9%.

In Central America and the Caribbean, we maintain positive perspectives for growth, in general. The brighter economies are Dominican Republic and Honduras, which accelerated during the first four months of the year. Meanwhile, Guatemala's prints have been stable. Although Costa Rica and El Salvador have decelerated from last year, in line with our expectations, they are still growing above their historical averages. The weaker story is in Panama, where it seems that policy uncertainty around elections had a larger impact than expected, in addition to the mining shock, and the economy remained flat on an annual basis during the first quarter.

## Argentina: Time for a check up

Gabriel Casillas, BCI, US  |  Sebastian Vargas, BCI, US | Ivan Stambulsky, Barclays, UK

• Credit: Remain Underweight. The path to regaining market access and/or replenishing reserves stocks is proving to be gradual. Import USD payments are just being normalized;<sup>45</sup> the treasury has piled on additional short-term debt to the balance sheet; the oficial exchange rate has appreciated, with the parallel exchange rate gap widening (40-45%) when exports are seasonally higher. Fiscal policy faces consensus-building dificulties which have pushed the authorities to rely on discretionary fiscal adjustment policies.<sup>46</sup>  Inflation has declined but consensus expectations are a still- high 5-6% m/m in H2. The market reacted positively to the passing of part of the omnibus bill (the regulatory but not the fiscal articles) through the senate. Going forward, the much-needed lifting of exchange rate controls face the challenges of a strong exchange rate and significant local debt rollovers. The strong bid for distressed EM HY has tempered but a strong local bid remains, albeit at a more moderate pace than at the start of the year. Accordingly, we recommend switching out of Argentina '30s into Ecuador '30s, as the later has underperformed, and, on the curve, we recommend defensive trades such as switching out of Argentina '30s into Argentina '41s to pick up c.16 points.

• FX: The Milei administration has been able to continue to deliver on fiscal adjustment promises while preserving political stability. The approval of the Bases law, although watered down, was a success on the institutional politics front and improves the chances of a new programme being agreed with the IMF. However, there are some indications that the administration is prioritizing short-term popularity over unbalances correction more than before. The postponement of energy price hikes and the partial reversal in private health are illustrative examples. Most importantly, persistent real FX appreciation could compromise FX reserve accumulation as economic activity picks up, even more so if the PAIS tax is cut by 10pp as planned, efectively acting as a discrete-jump revaluation. This trajectory feeds the risk of another discrete jump or an acceleration of the crawl going forward, as markets reassess the need for a higher REER. The elimination of capital controls should not be delayed by too long, as such a step is a landmark of normalization, and could be used as an opportunity to correct the built-in overvaluation as part of a regime change.

Beyond politics. In a tight vote, the senate passed the non-fiscal chapter of President Javier Milei's watered down omnibus bill. The authorities want to persist with the fiscal chapter of the bill at the lower house which would increase tax collection in about 0.4% of GDP/year. Separately, the lower house has passed an initiative to increase pensions in a similar amount with a veto-proof two-thirds majority that will be voted at the senate. The market was relieved after the senate vote, despite the rejection of the fiscal chapter, because an outright defeat in congress would increase governability concerns. Meanwhile, in addition to a challenging political landscape, six months into the Milei administration, we identify some important weaknesses in Milei's economic plan that we think investors should have on their radar screens.

Local debt gradually back to the fore. President Milei has indicated that there are two main hurdles to unwinding exchange rate controls: the reducing the central bank's interest-bearing liabilities and lowering the stock of puts embedded in local debt new issues. The central bank owes interest-bearing liabilities, or repos (pases), to the financial system. The policy objective is to regain full control of money supply growth, and interest paid on repos is an autonomous contributor to money growth. To achieve this objective, the authorities have coordinated a twopronged strategy: lower interest paid on repos,<sup>47</sup>  and exchange central bank repos for shortterm treasury debt (eg, Lecaps) at scheduled auctions. The amounts involved are sizable. Repos amounted to c.\$33bn in January and are now at c.\$18bn . From a consolidated government accountability standpoint, the transaction is a right-left pocket situation, leaving the liability side of the consolidated government balance sheet unchanged. Higher policy interest rates will increase the interest bill of the treasury, which on its side is trying to bring the budget deficit down. Accordingly, we do not think that moving repos to the treasury reduces the financial dependence of monetary policy. Moreover, when and if exchange rate controls come to an end, the increase in the stock of short-term bills creates new financial vulnerabilities:

• Debt growth can accelerate. If and when exchange rate controls are lifted, asset substitution will likely lead to a convergence of peso and USD funding yields  (USD yields are currently at 22.5%). YTD, the low cost of funding, courtesy of exchange rate controls and the central bank's negative real interest rate policy, has somewhat limited local debt growth. But the 2% ARS crawling peg has still led to a substantial increase in debt stock in USD terms (Figure 5). However, as short-term debt is increasingly rolled over at market rates that converge to higher USD funding yields, the impact of the cost of funding on debt will also increase, and due to its short-term structure, we expect local debt growth to compound quickly. The interest on short-term bills will not show up in the interest bill of fiscal accounts (due to accounting methodology), but growth in debt should factor into the overall efect.

• T-bills are securities with secondary markets. Repos have no secondary market and the only buyer of repos is the central bank. While banks have regulatory limits for FX exposure, the risks of portfolio changes that pressure reserves will increases as short-term treasury securities move to a broader investor base, such as mutual funds. As the sovereign has no access to credit markets, hard-currency bondholders can only expect to be paid out of (currently extremely low) central bank USD liquidity. This is not a risk under stringent exchange rate controls, but hard-currency bondholders should monitor rollover risks in shortterm bills if and when these controls are lifted.

• Local debt auctions are sizable. The treasury successfully auctioned c.\$5bn in short-term debt last week. This is large in magnitude considering that eurobond and bonar debt service from July 2024 through January 2025 is c.\$7.3bn. Again, assuming the exchange rate controls are removed, with nominal instability currently high, a low bid/cover in a local auction would afect global credit markets because it would imply a funding shortfall and because local investors could increase demand for reserves (ie, capital flight). We think this is the reason the treasury has accumulated c.\$13bn in cash – to mitigate rollover risk. However, there is c. \$35bn in short-term local debt maturing in the remainder of 2024.

• Banks' exposure to sovereign risk has increased. Central bank repos (c.1 day) are a cash proxy because the central bank would return pesos if banks experienced deposit withdrawals. The local debt market is much less liquid (particularly during times of stress). The fact that repos amounted to about 50% of deposits leads us to believe that the banking system's exposure to the treasury has increased substantially with the authorities strategy to move repos to local short-term bills (Figure 7).

FIGURE 5. Debt stock growth
![](images/0b562882e4a844f7eddded4c423caaf5520640c5e3790587dc6f72147411100c.jpg)
Source: Ministerio de Economia, Barclays Research

FIGURE 6. Local currency debt maturities in 2024
![](images/dd107626cbf72af754624d6734342b861b03802d195fe865f4bb9a3b40eb9f27.jpg)
Source: EcoGo, Barclays Research

Low reserves accumulation. In January, the general expectation was that the substantial trade balance would allow the central bank to substantially increase net reserves, particularly in 2Q, when exports peak. Moreover, the recession negatively afected imports in 2Q, with the high initial level of import inventories also positively contributing to the trade balance. But at the peak of the export season, the central bank was barely able to accumulate reserves, as reserves accumulation YTD was largely the result of new debt accumulated among importers (Figure 9). What went wrong? The combination of a 2% exchange rate crawl and high inflation eroded the initial benefits of peso depreciation. In addition, the negative real rate policy has led to an unattractive economic equation for exporters, including farmers, who can hoard soybeans funded at negative rates. Until the authorities solve the peso appreciation problem, reserves growth will remain sluggish. Moreover, as domestic income recovers, initially high inventories of tradable goods decline and imports rise. Another layer of increasing demand for dollars has been the gradual increase in the access to dollars for importers. While in December, c.17% of imports were allowed USD dollar access, this increased to c.67% in April. Moreover, as household income recovers in USD terms and demand for credit rises (it has recently improved), outbound tourism should resume. Lastly, while the approval of the RIGI law (regulatory framework for large investments) is a positive, we think it will take time before we see FDI inflows, which are likely contingent on exchange rate realignment. In terms of the capital account, some scattered external bond issuance from provincials and corporates have taken place, but new issuance will likely remain limited. The sovereign has no capacity to leverage up, and multilateral lenders have almost reached their limits. We thus think that Argentina requires a weak real exchange rate to pay back hard-currency obligations. That said, near-term debt service (until 2025) depends on liquidity and the willingness to pay. We think the latter is high, but it will likely be a struggle to replenish liquidity bufers under a strong exchange rate regime. Therefore, we expect the country to face challenges with 2025 hard-currency payments and we cannot rule out the possibility of a voluntary swap of 2025 hard-currency maturities.

FIGURE 7. Bank's exposure to treasury risk has increased
![](images/f6502cf2aa41476cc0f8b371387d7afdc9a7015203aada521e4979a69697b0de.jpg)
Source: BCRA, Barclays Research

FIGURE 8. Exporters are not selling much Cumulative oilseed and grain exports, USD bn
![](images/9f63014f5ea411912f40951112da167ea579485e115d164a99da23cf1a2eb36a.jpg)
Source: CIARA, Barclays Research

Exchange rate anchor in trouble. The government has committed to lowering inflation and has prioritized this as its most important goal. Proof of this is the fact that, despite Milei's strong fiscal austerity pledge, the authorities have delayed regulated price increases for transportation, and started  natural gas and electricity in June. The generalized recession in labor-intensive sectors, such as public infrastructure projects and retail, has certainly helped to put a cap on wages, which has helped to moderate inflation YTD, though the 2% exchange rate crawl remains an important inflation anchor. However, the strength of the anchor is proportional to the authorities' commitment to the 2% crawl. The authorities are thus locked in<sup>48</sup>  to the 2% crawl until a transition to a new regime is designed. Meanwhile, the real appreciation of the peso (Figure 10) weakens the exchange rate as an inflation anchor in as much it translates into low reserves accumulation. More structurally, it seems that there are too many 'claims' on reserves: reserves are required to pay back hard-currency bonds without access to international capital markets; to end exchange rate controls; to provide a backstop to potential money demand instability; or to swap the peso stocks if the peso is de facto decommissioned in a 'currency competition' scheme. In short, we think that the sharp real exchange rate appreciation YTD is a significant vulnerability of Milei's economic program.

FIGURE 9. Change in net reserves due mostly to increase importer debt
![](images/8d5642c2d3e78c9fed07a1dd660604ae2e6ca94087eb25b2ff296d2ad7d5c068.jpg)
Source: BCRA, Barclays Research

FIGURE 10. The real exchange rate in troubled territory
![](images/47463b05e7870f047c5b390dc83b9faaf3f5befe192ffeed9cad3ee32492e379.jpg)
Source: BCRA, Barclays Research

Soft fiscal underbelly. While Milei might believe in fiscal austerity, he has faced dificulties delivering permanent or structural fiscal outcomes as president. It is illustrative that, after six months of negotiations, the government was unable to garner enough support in the senate to back the fiscal chapter in the omnibus bill. Instead, the authorities have delivered (much needed) fiscal liquidity strategies, pulling levers in their direct control. On the revenues side, the YTD performance has relied on the initial peso devaluation, the PAIS tax<sup>49</sup>  and the sole structural measure – the update in fuel taxes (Figure 11). On the spending side, pensions were crushed due to domestic price liberalization and account for c.40% of the YTD adjustment. However, again, the lack of consensus building led the lower house to pass a pension increase initiative that adds 0.4% of GDP/year. The president said that he will veto the bill, but the lower house passed it with a two-thirds veto-proof majority. The senate has yet to vote on the lower house's proposal. We see risks that parts of the remaining YTD budget adjustments will be hard to sustain, for example the complete halting of 6,000 infrastructure projects. Stopping transfers to provinces might face legal challenges (as in the case of the Province of Buenos Aires) or could be reversed due to social pressures (provincial police/teacher strikes) or political agreements with governors, particularly as we approach the 2025 election year.  In other words, the YTD fiscal results due to the initial shock therapy have not evolved into fiscal agreements, as illustrated by the rejection of the fiscal chapter of the omnibus bill and lower house's pension increase initiative.

## Brazil: Credibility issues

Roberto Secemski, BCI, US | Sebastian Vargas, BCI, US | Andrea Kiguel, BCI, US

The credibility of the Lula administration on the monetary and fiscal policy fronts is being questioned by market participants following the split Copom decision in May and signs that recent eforts to increase fiscal revenue are facing greater push-back in society and in Congress, which could put the fiscal framework at risk down the line. The local perception that MoF Haddad does not necessarily have President Lula's ear when it comes to curbing growth in public spending clouds the outlook ahead of the presentation of the 2025 budget in end-August.

• Credit: Remain Overweight. Brazil spreads cheapened (relative to peers) on the back of fiscal uncertainties. However, we recommend taking advantage of Brazil spread weakness.

• FX: Real rate spreads to the US are close to levels seen at the end of past cycles, where the BCB was thought to have over-cut as carry eroded and fiscal conditions were deteriorating. The narrative today is not so diferent, with the country going through a confidence crisis as inflation expectations de-anchor and fiscal targets come under question. The authorities are showing good will to rebuild credibility, but this will take time to be reflected in local assets, especially as local fund managers have limited firepower after recent moves. We stay bearish the BRL, which still needs local premium. The global narrative has not helped either, with the Mexican and French election shocks leading to a large carry unwind, and we likely need a global catalyst for sustained relief in the USD.

• Rates: Markets have priced the end of the BCB cycle, and focus has shifted to the transition to the next board. Inflation expectations are de-anchoring in the process, as markets weigh how dovish the next board may lean. Concerns about fiscal slippage have added to the credibility crisis. This points to a more hawkish reaction function today, especially as local data has been on the strong side, and markets are pricing a hike premium over the next year. While we do not expect hikes from the BCB at this point, we doubt markets are in a rush to price these out, leaving risks skewed to higher rates. Past cycles suggest the Jan 25/26 FRAs has decent premium, but an estimation of risk-neutral rates suggests there are still upside risks if breakevens continue to rise. Moreover, the term premium has lagged FX and fiscal risks, suggesting the Jan 26/31s have room to steepen after largely flattening on the pricing out of cuts (see this note for details).

MoF Haddad's momentum for pushing additional revenue measures in Congress as part of an ongoing efort to meet the ambitious primary fiscal target of 0% +/- 0.25pp of GDP this year appears to have waned following some recent defeats. This has ignited market concerns that President Lula could ultimately opt for an "easy way out," declining to freeze expenditures in upcoming bimonthly reviews (the next one is 22 July) and instead possibly downgrading this year's fiscal target (similar to what was done for the 2025 and 2026 targets on 15 April). At the same time, Lula's reported unwillingness to curb growth in public spending creates dificulties for the economic team as it prepares the 2025 budget draft, to be presented on 31 August.

In the short term, the extension of a payroll tax break for 2024-27, approved by Congress last year at a cost initially estimated around BRL20bn/year in terms of foregone revenue has been a particularly dificult issue. President Lula vetoed it, citing the lack of funding for such a measure, per the Fiscal Responsibility Law. Congress later repealed that veto, thus reinstating the extension (still with no funding source), but a 2024 budget that did not account for this was approved shortly thereafter. To try to reduce the fiscal damage from the overturned veto, MoF Haddad introduced Provisional Measure 1202 (MP 1202) on 28 December to gradually fade those payroll breaks, but the push-back from Congress was very strong, forcing the economic team to backtrack. The matter was then taken to the Supreme Court, which gave the executive and legislative branches 60 days to create the proper funding for the extension of the payroll tax break (now quoted at BRL26bn) or else the benefit could not be extended (though for now it remains valid). Haddad tried his luck again, this time with MP 1227 on 4 June, which immediately limited the use of certain tax credits related to the PIS/Cofins charges. The reaction was even stronger than before, with Congress sending back the proposal a week after receiving it, this time with Lula's support. The impasse resulted in the perception that MoF Haddad is isolated and weak.<sup>50</sup>

After significant market noise since early June, President Lula ofered stronger words of support for MoF Haddad over the weekend (16 June), saying, "Haddad will never be weakened while I am the President, because he is my Minister of Finance, chosen by me and maintained by me." Importantly, Lula finally alluded to the possibility of trimming public spending, though he said that no fiscal adjustment would be made "on the back of the poor," likely meaning that he will not concede to changes in the annual adjustment of the minimum wage. The president also criticized those who want "to worsen public healthcare and education." While it remains unclear what kind of spending cuts (or, rather, cuts in spending growth) would be entertained for next year, any measures would need to be presented ahead of the budget draft (PLOA), due on 31 August.

All in all, we continue to see the delivery of the zero target in 2024 as unlikely. We forecast a primary deficit of 0.8% of GDP this year (which includes some 0.2pp of GDP in flood-related assistance for Rio Grande do Sul (RS), which is not subject to the target), and 0.6% in 2025. We expect gross public debt to rise from 74.4% of GDP in 2023 to 77.7% in 2024 and 80.4% in 2025, with upside risks depending on further assistance to RS and rising volumes of precatorio payments (e.g., exceeding BRL100bn in 2025, as recently announced by the ministry of planning).

Another source of significant market noise has been the upcoming transition in the BCB's board, as President Lula will appoint three new members, including the next BCB governor, sometime in H2 24 (to take ofice in January). According to the local press,<sup>51</sup> the intention is to announce the replacement of Roberto Campos Neto in August. However, a split (5-4) Copom vote in May caused market participants to bring forward their concerns about the future board's credibility and commitment to the center of the inflation target, as all four board members appointed by Lula thus far voted for a more dovish 50bp cut on 8 May, instead of the 25bp reduction that was implemented. This triggered additional deanchoring of inflation expectations for 2025 (now at 3.80%, from 3.64% before the May meeting), also lifting median IPCA expectations for 2026 to 3.60%, from 3.50% before (which itself was already detached from the center of the target, established at 3.0%).

We thus expect the BCB to leave the Selic rate unchanged at 10.50% on 19 June, interrupting the easing cycle that was initiated in August, when the Selic rate was at 13.75%. The decision to stand pat would be a response to additional deterioration in medium-term inflation expectations. Moreover, increased uncertainty on the path of fiscal accounts and the adverse behavior of local assets, especially the BRL (some 4% weaker than in the May meeting), are likely to result in greater caution by the Copom, as the bank's own inflation forecasts for 2024 and 2025 are likely to rise. We expect the decision to interrupt the easing cycle to be unanimous; otherwise, we would expect increased noise and volatility as a result, as inflation expectations would likely continue to deanchor. We forecast the Selic rate to stay at 10.5% until early 2025,

with the new governor potentially finding a window of opportunity to trim rates 100bp next year, assuming a more constructive external environment amid Fed cuts in the US.

In our view, a unanimous hold this week combined with the upcoming publication of a decree regulating the inflation targeting regime before end-June (using a "rolling window" instead of a calendar-year basis, as announced a year ago but not yet implemented) could help to slow or even halt the ongoing deterioration in expectations as the government reinforces its commitment to the 3% target. However, we believe market estimates for inflation may take time to retrace, critically depending on the appointment of the next BCB governor and that person's inflation-fighting credentials.

We recently tweaked our inflation forecasts amid higher than usual uncertainty due to largescale floods in the state of Rio Grande do Sul, which afected the IBGE's ability to collect prices in the metropolitan region of Porto Alegre (the institute resorted to online surveys for nearly two thirds of the basket there last month). IPCA inflation in May surprised to the upside, mainly due to foodstufs, and we revised our 2024 inflation forecast to 3.9%, from 3.7% before, although it remains unclear whether the shocks caused by the floods will fully dissipate in the coming months. Our 2025 forecast was increased 10bp, to 3.6%.

Earlier this month, we revised our GDP growth forecasts for 2024 and 2025 upward, to 2.2% and 1.9%, respectively, from 1.9% and 1.7% before, balancing a stronger start to the year with the lingering uncertainty from the floods in the south, as well as the remarkable strength of the labor market. We would likely have revised our 2024 estimate up further were it not for the uncertainties in Rio Grande  do Sul, though we expect reconstruction eforts to support economic activity in the several quarters ahead.
