# Central America and the Caribbean: A political risk oasis

Nestor Rodriguez, BBMX, Mexico | Jason Keene, BCI, US

The region experienced three general elections—in El Salvador, the Dominican Republic and Panama—during the first half of the year without major surprises or political turmoil. In El Salvador, a lack of clarity on the need for a fiscal adjustment to provide debt sustainability remains in focus, while in the Dominican Republic, a potential tax reform is in the works. Panama's new administration will need to juggle a variety of pressuring topics, including a fiscal adjustment, pension reform, water management and mining. Other focus points include the end of the IMF program in Costa Rica, the political calibration of Arevalo's agenda in Guatemala, and the delayed IMF review in Honduras.

## Credit Strategy

Since our last Emerging Markets Quarterly, we have downgraded Honduras and the Bahamas to Market Weight, both of which were Overweight previously:

• Honduras (MW) has failed to undertake even a modicum of monetary and exchange rate liberalization, delaying the IMF’s first review and leading to a continued drop in FX reserves (CAC Sovereign Strategy: Freshly squeezed trade ideas, 18 March 2024).

• Bahamas (MW) has performed well since the start of the year, and while fiscal outturns are strong, consolidation eforts appear to be losing momentum. We also prefer to tread cautiously into what is likely to be an active hurricane season (Bahamas Sovereign Strategy: Warmer waters cool conviction, 30 May 2024).

As an ofset to these downgrades, we initiated coverage of Suriname at Overweight and upgraded the Dominican Republic to Overweight (previously Market Weight):

• The Dominican Republic (OW) has reduced its market footprint in recent years, and fiscal reform should contain supply expectations. While valuations are historically tight, we see space for spread compression to reform-oriented BB-rated peers like Costa Rica, Paraguay, and Oman (Dominican Republic: Follow the yellow brick road, 15 May 2024).

• Suriname (OW) is on the verge of a final investment decision that could propel the country into being one of the largest per capita oil producers in the world by the end of the decade (Suriname Sovereign Strategy: The Caribbean phoenix, 22 May 2024).

We have maintained our ratings in the following names: Costa Rica (OW); El Salvador (UW);
Guatemala (MW); Jamaica (MW); and Panama (MW).

## Costa Rica: Life after the IMF

The IMF program is coming to an end, while macro environment should remain stable this year. The IMF completed the sixth and final review of the EFF with a disbursement of around \$272mn, and third and final review of the RSF, allowing for about \$243mn to be disbursed. This marked the end of the program that began in March 2021. As expected, the IMF statement was positive, as it highlighted the successful conclusion of the program amid strong growth and fiscal performance. However, the fund mentioned that it is critical to institutionalize the central bank’s autonomy and clarify its mandate and decision-making processes; that legislative changes that could erode revenue should be avoided and the coverage of the fiscal rule should be maintained; and that is critical for the public employment bill to be fully implemented by all afected institutions.

This year, the landscape should be supported by economic growth, which we forecast at 4.2% (still above the historical average), and the fact that government needs to comply with a 1.85% primary surplus for this year embedded in multi-year eurobond law. This law allows Costa Rica to issue another \$1.0bn in external bonds this year (likely by end-Q3, in our view) and next.

But underlying risks could mount in 2025. The fiscal surplus over January-April was just 0.6% of GDP, the lowest level since 2021. This comes amid a deceleration in income growth to just 0.6% y/y, with tax income declining 0.6% y/y during that period. Although the economy remains strong, it is decelerating. Hence, in order to comply with the target, the government will need to cut expenditures, and primary expenditures have grown 9.3% y/y through the first four months of the year. Heading into 2025, the government will not be under a particular fiscal surplus target, and the campaign cycle will begin leading up to the February 2026 election. Meanwhile, a political confrontation between Congress and the executive<sup>52</sup> will likely prevent any progress on proposals such as assets sales or President Chaves' recent push to reform to the Ofice Comptroller to weaken its fiscal audit powers. Overall, we forecast a primary fiscal surplus of 1.4% in 2025, shrinking from 1.9% in 2024. Hence, public debt will likely continue to decline and consolidate below 60% of GDP, but we see a chance that this downward trend will decelerate.

## Dominican Republic: High expectations for the second term

In line with expectations, President Abinader was reelected amid a stronger political mandate. On May 19, President Abinader was reelected with 57.4% of the vote, more than the amount required to win in a first round. Furthermore, his party, the PRM, will now have a stronger position in Congress. In the Senate, the party and its allies would hold 29 out of 32 seats. Meanwhile, projections for the lower house indicate that PRM won as many as 144 seats out of 190. Amid this stronger political mandate, the focus now are on reforms. Abinader mentioned plans on for constitutional reform. The focus would be to give more independence to the attorney general and new mechanisms to re-election status in the future where a President can only be immediately reelected once. Both proposals seems to be positive for governance and power division, but details are still need to be known.

Meanwhile, August and September likely will be crucial to know potential fiscal reform. As we wrote in Dominican Republic: Follow the yellow brick road, the focus is on fiscal reform. Our expectation is that Abinader's administration will propose something after the start of his second term on August 16 along with the 2025 fiscal budget that needs to oficially be submitted to Congress before October 1. Our expectations are for them to be approved given the political mandate and for positive economic momentum as the economy grew by 4.1% y/y in Q1 2024 and we expect to continue to accelerate as the monthly economic index did in April by 7.9% y/y. Our 2024 growth forecast is at 5.0%.

This, along with the final approval of the fiscal responsibility law, should bring clarity on the fiscal targets ahead. The law sets a cap to expenditure real growth of 3.0% y/y per year.  We have estimated that a net adjustment of around 2.0pp of GDP would ensure public debt is in a downward trend. For this year, we estimate a deficit of 3.1% of GDP with debt relatively stable at 45%. Finally, we expect the government to issue in external markets in coming months as uncertainty from elections eases. Our projection is for around \$2.0-2.5bn but this could be in diferent tranches, first focusing on DOP-linked bonds, as the government has been vocal on continuing to reduce its share of foreign currency debt.

El Salvador: The medicine is known, but there is a reluctance to take it No fiscal discipline amid weak economic start to the year. This year, we estimate the fiscal deficit at 2.6% of GDP. This is higher than the 1.8% we forecast in our previous EMQ, but it

reflects that the government's expenditure in the months after elections continued to grow by 36.8%  y/yon average between March and April. Although revenue has performed relatively well, growing 15.2% YTD, the fiscal deficit is now wider than a year ago. Furthermore, there is risk of economic activity decelerating faster than expected. After growing 3.5% in 2023, driven by public investment, the monthly economic index has only grown 2.6% up to March.  We are now projecting the economy to grow 2.8% instead of 3.0%, but it should be important to monitor that stronger deceleration amid weaker remittances growth (2.2% YTD) does not afect tax collection at some point. After April's issuance (see El Salvador: Kicking the can, but up a steeper hill), and even amid a wider deficit, we think that the government is likely to close the financing gap with domestic sources such as pension funds and domestic banks, but the tight fiscal space remains a focus.

The government continues to mention a potential IMF program but no clear signs yet. Bukele inaugurated his second term in ofice on June 1. During his speech, he mentioned that to fix the economy, the country might need to take a "bitter medicine." In our view, this could be related to a potential fiscal adjustment. It is also relevant amid prospects for a potential IMF program —around 3% of GDP has been mentioned in the past. However, we remain cautious on the materialization of such plan, and overall on an IMF program being reached soon, as the execution data does not show any initial attempts at fiscal discipline. Regarding an IMF program, authorities from El Salvador continue to indicate that a deal could be closed soon; nonetheless, the IMF has maintained a more-prudent tone to the press, saying that it is still addressing diferent topics such as the risks of bitcoin and need for fiscal transparency. In our view, another sign that the deal is not near is because the government continues to rely on financing alternatives such as multilateral CAF or issuing in the external market at high interest rates.

## Guatemala: Political bumps hindering Arevalo's plans

Weak equilibrium in Congress could make it dificult to approve budget modifications and lead to a low fiscal deficit. The authorities on May 28 finally submitted to Congress their proposal to modify the budget for this year, after more than three months of delays. The plan is to increase expenditures by around 1.7% of GDP, or GTQ14.4bn, while most of this would be financed by a more realistic revenue projection (GTQ13.4bn) and the rest via the cash balance. The current budget in place is the one from 2023, which greatly underestimated tax revenues. Hence, it still aims for an overall deficit of just 1.7% of GDP. Nonetheless, the government delays could make it dificult to approve. Although the bill is currently under review by the legislative commission, the president of that body warned that this could take time, and Congress is currently in recess until August 1. If there are further delays, there is a risk that it could be overshadowed by the 2025 fiscal budget, which needs to be submitted no later than September 1.

For now, such delays and fiscal under-execution, as illustrated by an 8.4% y/y  decline in public expenditures up to May, lead us to cut our fiscal deficit forecast to 1.5% of GDP, down from 2.0%, previously. We still expect to see some widening of the deficit in the coming years,with public debt only slowly increasing from its current 28% of GDP. Thus, the strong macroeconomic position should remain intact, and we forecast growth at 3.8% this year and expect a current account surplus of 1.1%. Despite a lower fiscal deficit, we still expect the authorities to issue around \$500-600mn in external markets by end-Q3 to maintain some presence and help guide the private sector.

Political focus on the renewal of justice magistrates, attorney general actions, and Arevalo's governability. For the next few months and before October 12, Congress will need to elect 13 magistrates of the Supreme Court and others to lower courts. It is too early to know what profile they will have and how close they might be to Arevalo's administration or to the

Attorney General's ofice. This will be relevant, as the courts, particularly the Supreme Court are crucial in the process of deciding when the authorities' legal immunity ends. For now, the AG's actions have not been that loud against Arevalo. It recently started an investigation into the Ministry of Finance on allegations of bribery to buy deputies' votes to support the budget modification.<sup>53</sup>  Some could use this to afect Arevalo's image, as concrete actions would be limited by the fact that Minister Menkos enjoys legal immunity.

## Honduras: Review delays clouding the outlook

The focus remains on the absence of clarity on the IMF program without a first review being settled yet. Honduras has mostly delivered on fiscal policy despite the tax reform remaining stalled in Congress , with reports that it is being watered down, making its yield even more uncertain. The first review of the IMF program is likely being delayed, because of the lack of clarity on how to reverse the downward trend in reserves, which are now at \$5.0bn per IMF definition and below the \$6.3bn reached in December 2022, while also adjusting FX market operations as the Central Bank is reluctant to stop defending the currency. Given the delays, if authorities agree to implement such changes, the most optimistic scenario is that the first review is done alongside the second, which is scheduled for September. For now, amid USD scarcity Honduras authorities have mentioned the possibility of a new external issuance of around USD800mn, but there has not been any oficial guidance yet.

The rest of the economic landscape is doing relatively well, with growth accelerating and the fiscal deficit still low. The economy has surprised to the upside as the monthly activity index grew 5.0% y/y on average during the first fourth months of the year. This was driven by a recovery in manufacturing, after a weak 2023, reacceleration of the construction sector and some services that more than ofset weak agricultural sector results. Although there are risks that activity could be afected by FX markets disruptions, we think that the external sector through exports and remittances will provide some support and now expect the economy to grow 4.0% this year, up from 3.2% previously and also up from 3.6% in 2023.

Strong growth has helped fiscal accounts where up to March, the fiscal surplus is at 0.5% of GDP, higher than the 0.1% registered a year ago. This came amid further expenditure containment by 0.21pp of GDP and higher revenues by 0.14pp of GDP. We forecast a deficit of -1.3% of GDP for the full year, similar to last year. Risks of a wider deficit might only materialize into 2025, driven by politics with elections occurring in October of next year.

## Panama: Juggling priorities

The incoming administration has stated that is its top economic priorities include a fiscal plan without tax increases and solving the financing of the pension deficit. Raul Mulino was elected president on May 5, and his administration will take ofice on July 1 (see Panama: Elections over, several tests lie ahead). He already has announced his cabinet, with technocrat Felipe Chapman leading the Economics and Finance Ministry. In recent interviews, Chapman made clear that he is aware of the need to work on a fiscal adjustment. Nonetheless, he mentioned that raising taxes will not be part of the adjustment for now, focusing on expenditure cuts and making tax collection more eficient. The fiscal adjustment has also been mentioned by Mulino as one of his top priorities along with a solution to the pension situation — where there is no announced plan yet — as well as water management and migration policies at the Darien Gap.

Mining it is unlikely to be part of the agenda this year. Mulino also said that concrete decisions on the country's current mining ban might not be addressed until after the pension issue is solved. This is in line with our view that Mulino's administration has no urgency to

spend political capital on this in the short term. It is important to recall that a February poll indicated that 90% of the population was against mining activity and although that could change with diferent rhetoric from the new administration, as mentioned above, they also have other priorities. Mulino already has acknowledged the precarious social situation, mentioning his government will be careful to not afect "social peace" with their policy proposals.

Furthermore, political equilibrium at Congress is not clear and could be tested. With a busy legislative agenda ahead, there is also a lot of focus on potential alliances in Congress, as Mulino's party Realizando Metas only holds 14 seats out of 71. He likely could add two from political ally Alianza and one from Molirena, and then 13 deputies from PRD, which was also an ally in some candidacies in the past election, bringing a coalition to a total of 30. However, that would still fall short of the 36 needed for a simple majority. The main question is if 16 deputies from the Panameñista and CD parties will support Mulino or align with the main opposition formed by three deputies from MOCA and 20 independent deputies under the VAMOS coalition. The main challenge for Mulino to approach the opposition could be to distance himself from former president Martinelli, but not so much that could cause him to lose support from sympathizers at Realizando Metas and PRD. The composition of the leadership of Congress should become clearer on July 1.

Once the new administration is in ofice in July, all eyes will be on its medium-term fiscal strategy and 2025 budget. The incoming administration will likely need to revise the fiscal responsibility law and set new targets, as the deficit targets of 2% and 1.5% of GDP for this year and next year, respectively, are unlikely to be met. The focus will be on how credible the new targets will be amid lack of plans to raise taxes. The 2025 budget, which needs to be submitted in the first 40 days of Congress' sessions, likely by August, could also give clarity on fiscal priorities.

We maintain our fiscal deficit forecast for 2024 at 4.4% of GDP but risks are to the upside. Fiscal data up to Q1 show that fiscal accounts are relatively stable, with a fiscal deficit at 1.9% of GDP, similar to the 1.8% registered a year ago. Nonetheless, we expects some deterioration to materialize ahead. Capital expenditures could decelerate as the new administration takes ofice with a learning curve, but this would be partially ofset by the rising interest bill, which already increased 25% y/y in Q1. Furthermore, strong revenue performance in Q1, with growth of 10% y/y could be afected by economic deceleration. GDP only grew 1.7% y/y in the first quarter of the year. The print was driven by construction and services such as real estate and tourism that more than ofset the decline registered by mining activities (49.5% y/y). We expect subdued growth until Q4, when it will benefit from some base efects, but our full-year 2024 growth forecast remains at 2.2%.

Finally, we maintain expectations of a new issuance of around \$1.5bn in external markets in coming months. The timing is uncertain, but risks are tilted towards sooner rather than later, as soon as July, amid uncertainty if the new administration will announce a plan to settle arrears, which, although there is no oficial number, some reports have said it could could be up to \$2.0bn.<sup>54</sup>  Furthermore, in some signs of tight liquidity, the country recently issued domestic debt to cancel pending payments to the Saving Fund (FAP) by \$1.3bn, and it recently chose not roll over a domestic bond (\$1.0bn PABONT).

## Chile: Last call

Gabriel Casillas,  BCI, US | Sebastian Vargas, BCI, US | Andrea Kiguel, BCI, US

Political noise and less predictable monetary policy have clouded Boric’s fine macroeconomic management. Nonetheless, the economy has been recovering, particularly commerce, and inflation has trended downward since its peak in 2022 towards the 3% target. Moreover, we believe there is a window of opportunity to achieve the approval of a pension reform in the next few months. These will not be exempt from challenges, particularly the reduction of the electricity tarif subsidies that will have a positive fiscal outcome but will add some inflationary pressures. While we believe this will not preclude the central bank from continuing to ease, it might foster the central bank to reduce the pace of cuts, more so with the Fed’s more hawkish stance.

• Credit: Remain Overweight. We think that Chile valuations/fundamentals remain relatively attractive to regional IG peers.

• FX: The CLP underperformed most of last year as the BCCh normalized rates, with carry erosion weighing on the exchange rate. But now rates are closer to neutral levels, and the BCCh can aford to go slower in easing, with a more predictable reaction function. This makes carry erosion less of an issue, and the CLP should find support, trading more closely to fundamentals. Strong copper prices, which have certainly provided a boost for the current account, along with sideways oil prices, rebounding growth, and investments in copper mining are also constructive for the peso. Moreover, AFP flows have stabilized, with Fund A holdings quite high (predominantly ofshore equity funds), suggesting there is less AUM running to chase USD. We see room for the CLP to outperform other low-yielding FX in this environment.

• Rates: Inflation has largely eased, but is in the last mile, and could prove dificult to get back to target. A more stable CLP should still help the disinflation process, along with lower fuel prices. However, electricity prices are the big upside risk, and we stay long 2y breakevens, as 1y expectations have started to creep up and should be monitored closely. The BCCh still has room to ease in this context, but likely at a slower pace. Spreads to the US are still at tight levels, and while this becomes less of a factor if the FX is stable, there is no rush to ease given that economic growth has bottomed, the labor market is slowly recovering and inflation risks are to the upside. Markets are pricing a slow normalization, in this vein, with a terminal around 4.60% (close to the BCCh's upper bound of neutral), in a non-continous cycle. We think the front-end is fairly priced, and expect terminal pricing to fluctuate between 4.50 and 5.00%, depending on the US data.

## Boric’s window of opportunity for a pension reform

While macroeconomic management has been improving, President Boric’s term has been surrounded by political noise, with no results in practical terms. The two eforts to rewrite the constitution were rejected and no reform has been approved. The tax reform the government recently submitted to congress aiming to increase tax collection by 3.6% of GDP was rejected. Moreover, the proposed healthcare and labor market reforms have not been submitted yet. These add to the security problems that, far from being solved, have sadly worsened.

Unfortunately, time is running short in terms of the political calendar with municipal elections in October, and then presidential elections next year in November. Nevertheless, it seems that there is a window of opportunity to achieve the approval of a pension reform, which has been on and of the table over the past 12 years. The Lower House already approved Boric’s plan, which includes an increase in the compulsory contributions, a move to a mixed pension system, and enhanced industry competition.

There has been a lot of discussion around this reform, with the most relevant topic the increase in the contributions, which has been suggested at 6%, and how much would go to individual accounts and how much would go to collective savings and solidarity. The government seeks a 3%+3% 50/50 formula , while the opposition has ofered a 5%+1%, with 5% going to individual accounts and the remaining 1% to social security.

All in all, it is our view that there is space for agreement for this reform to be approved. It is dificult to estimate the short-term macroeconomic efects, but it would likely have three medium- and long-term efects: (1) A short-term negative impact on aggregate demand, with the higher contribution, however, on the positive side, (2) a better investment outlook with legal certainty for investments in the system, after so many years of reform threats; and (3) an increase of financial resources to fund long-term investments as overall assets under management of pension funds return to an upward trend, particularly after the past withdrawals.

## Fuel prices and CLP challenges for CPI are over, now it is time for electricity tarifs

CPI has trended downward since its peak back in August 2022 above 14% to around 4.0% currently, as the pandemic and war in Ukraine shocks have dissipated. The change in base year and methodology caused some confusion, but there is no doubt inflation is at significantly lower levels (Figure 1). Fuel prices and the past depreciation of the CLP boosted CPI in the most recent prints. Positively, the higher copper price-induced appreciation of the CLP, along with slightly lower fuel prices, will start to be observed in inflation (Figure 2).

This should allow the BCCh to continue easing as they have been since July 2022, but probably at a slower pace, given that a significant part of  the cycle has already happened, as well as the Fed’s more restrictive stance, and a new CPI challenge amid the delayed structural reduction of the electricity tarifs subsidies which started in June being after frozen during the pandemic. Electricity tarifs have a weight of 2.2% in CPI, and the reduction of the subsidy will increase them, through single-digit monthly increments, by a total of 30-40%.

In this context, we revise our 12-month headline inflation forecast to 4% for YE24, from 3.5% earlier, and to 3.5% by YE25, from 3.0% previously. We expect the BCCh to cut the monetary policy rate by 50bp for the last time this week, even though consensus recently has moved to a 25bp cut, and then lower the pace to 25bp in the coming meetings to take the rate towards 4.50% by YE24 and to 4.25% in 2025.

## Fiscal policy back to 'normal', with focus on the electricity subsidy reduction

Along with inflation, the focal point of fiscal policy is the reduction of the subsidy applied on electricity tarifs during Covid, which after some delays, started in June. While for market participants.  the efect on inflation has been the issue, the Ministry of Finance will need to repay the electricity generators the debt that mounted since its implementation, and move the system back to a sustainable framework. All in all, we continue to expect a deficit of 1.9% of GDP by YE24, and 2.0% in 2025.

## Economic activity improving, but legal uncertainties continue to "add sand to the wheels"

Economic activity has recovered since last year, mainly due toa favorable base efects, as monetary restriction and fiscal consolidation kicked in. The central bank has now eased 525bp since July of last year, and higher copper prices have also created a more favorable growth outlook. However, the usual copper price impulse is not what it used to be, as it has been impaired by legal uncertainties that have been non-stop since “el estallido” social riots back in 2019. This was followed by the two constitutional referendums, and a series of marketunfriendly reform proposals. Fortunately, private consumption, or commerce, from the aggregate supply side that we observe in the central bank’s GDP monthly proxy Imacec, rebounded in April. In this context, we continue to project GDP to grow 2.5% this year and 1.7% in 2025.

## Colombia: Fade the noise

Alejandro Arreaza, BCI, US | Sebastian Vargas, BCI, US | Ivan Stambulsky, Barclays, UK

A second upward revision of the deficit targets in less than six months does not help to anchor fiscal expectations and could cause noise and volatility to persist in the near future, although however, this should gradually decline. The announcement of an expenditure freeze helps to contain risks, in our view. The driver of the widening of the deficit is an increase in the interest bill that seems to have been largely over-estimated, and this creates an opportunity for positive surprises as actual data comes in, as we have seen in previous years. In the interim, debt levels that remain largely below what was expected when Colombia lost its IG status in 2021 provide a bufer against the uncertainty.

• Credit: Remain Market Weight. Despite the recent spread sell-of, we think current spreads reflect the balance of risks facing Colombia.

• FX:  The COP has not been spared from the global carry unwind, but we think of this as a near-term overshoot for now. We expect the currency to recover in the near term, as Banrep will likely err on the side of a bit more caution, given the external environment. Fundamentals warrant easing, as disinflation is on the right track and economic activity remains slow. We expect authorities to deliver more than market pricing later in the year, and the COP should underperform as carry erodes, which has been a consistent theme across EM.

• Rates: Colombia's disinflation story has had momentum in yearly terms, with base efects helping early in the year. Food prices have added some upside pressures, while energy inflation has finally turned and services are, importantly, coming of. Inflation expectations have remained above target for this year and next, however, keeping authorities still cautious. Growth is also slow, though expected to recover a bit later in the year. Banrep should continue to ease in this context. We see room for the IBR curve to continue to steepen, especially the 1s5s. The market is currently pricing 50bp and 25bp cuts, with a terminal rate of roughly 8% in one year. We think authorities have space to ease faster than this, potentially cutting in 75bp moves if the market gains confidence that the Fed eases this year. That said, hedging the back-end of the curve could provide protection over receiving outright, as the fiscal outlook remains challenging. US elections could add extra pressures on the FX.

## Trying not to miss the forest for the trees

The Medium Term Fiscal Framework (MTFF) presented on June 14 brought a second upward revision of the fiscal targets in less than six months, which does not help to anchor fiscal expectations. Contradictory signals from the authorities and large margins of discretion make fiscal uncertainties likely to persist and undermine credibility. Amid a highly polarized political environment that has permeated into fiscal policy discussions, this contributes to the general noise. In this context, the MTFF does not provide much relief. However, the expenditure freeze announced by the authorities (see Colombia: Reassuring signals) does help to contain some of the existing pressures on the fiscal accounts,is a signal of fiscal responsibility from the authorities, and, in our view, could create an opportunity to outperform the current targets, leaving room for positive surprises as actual data comes in.

Considering the large and recurrent changes to the oficial forecasts in both directions over recent years, we think the figures presented in the MTFF need to be taken with a grain of salt, as they could be misleading. For example, in 2021 the MTFF projected large deficits and an increase in debt levels to nearly 70% GDP, while actual data over subsequent years ended up showing smaller deficits and a decline in debt levels to 55% of GDP, nearly 15pp lower than estimated. This has weakened the credibility of the report as a guide to the medium-term

outlook, and in our view at most it could provide some signals about the direction of policy in the near future (2024-25).

The latest revisions have been moving in a negative direction; however, they do not appear suficient to reverse the improvement in terms of debt reduction achieved as a result of the outperformance of the targets in the previous three years. Nonetheless, the volatility of the oficial forecast and unambitious deficit targets do not help to build credibility, and instead contribute to volatility and risks on a range of macroeconomic variables. Should this persist over the longer term, the concern is that a de-anchoring of expectations could turn a bigger increase in debt levels into a self-fulfilling prophesy; however, short-term risks appear contained.

The main element incorporated in the MTFF is the expenditure freeze, which should help to contain fiscal risks. In response to a revenue shortfall, the government has stopped the execution of initiatives amounting to 1.2% of GDP. Relative to last year, they are also cutting primary expenditures by a similar amount. Additionally, they moved from what seemed to be overly optimistic revenue assumption all the way to what could be seen as overly conservative estimates.

The government is no longer expecting revenues from litigation, which amounted 0.6% of GDP. Instead of an increase in tax collection of 20% y/y estimated for this year in the budget approved last year, a 2% y/y decline is now expected. This seems to contradict the expectations of a gradual recovery of the economy from the cyclical slowdown and inflation estimates, which results in an expansion of nominal GDP of c.7%. Although tax collections have been afected in the past couple of months, the drivers of the underperformance seem to be mainly temporary issues such as changes in the deadline for tax payments, pre-payments of taxes and returns, which could be at least partially reversed over the coming months.

The previously mentioned revisions result in the 2024 and 2025 deficit targets increasing to 5.6% and 5.1% of GDP, from 5.3% and 3.7%, respectively. However, the widening of the deficit comes from higher interest levels, which arerising to 4.7% of GDP from 3.9% last year, which we think is an over estimate. The government assumes an increase in the nominal interest bill of nearly 30% relative to last year, while data for Q1 24 is showing a decline of 8% as a result of the COP appreciation, UVR indexation and lower interest rates. If this trend is sustained, the interest bill could be c.1pp of GDP lower than the oficial estimate.

Although deficits could end up being smaller than budgeted in the interim, they are creating additional supply risk in the bond market. The additional financing this year is focused in the local market, where the government plans to issue COP10trn more than previously planned. There is not an increase in external issuance planned for 2024, and according to the financing plan, there should be just c.USD1.5bn left for the remainder of the year. However, external financing in 2025 would be USD9bn, c.USD6bn of which would be bonds. This amount could change depending on the evolution of the actual data, but a potential pre-financing this year could imply a risk of larger supply than previously estimated.

The most concerning issue contained in the MTFF in our view is the large discretion that authorities have, which generates a high degree of uncertainty. The fiscal rule is extremely flexible, which make the targets highly volatile and sensitive to the assumptions and actua performance of the economy. The authorities' main commitment seems to be to the 55% net debt anchor level set in the fiscal rule, but they seem willing to use the space given in terms of the deficit to provide stimuli to the economy. Therefore, although we see the expenditure freeze as a positive , given that this is not a formal cut, it risks being reversed if revenues come in higher than estimated, which would keep the deficit at worryingly high levels. In that scenario, imbalances in the rest of the economy could reemerge, including a widening of the current

account deficit and a reopening of the output gap, which could generate FX pressures, limit Banrep's space for rate cuts and increase the debt dynamics beyond what authorities currently expect.

Amid the existing uncertainty, there could be at least three mitigating factors. First, the time constraint. While the government retains the option to revise the freeze, it could find it is more dificult to execute the projects that were initially planned later in the year. Second, given that the fiscal rule target is based on the primary balance, if the interest bill is the driver of the outperformance, the government in principle cannot use that space to finance primary spending, otherwise it would be breaking the rule. Third, as part of the MTFF, the government is requesting an end to the transitional period of the fiscal rule one year earlier than initially intended. This has opened space to have a larger deficit next year as the primary structural deficit would not be fixed in 2025 but instead be determined by the formula set in the rule, which was supposed to enter into efect in 2026. Given that net debt levels are lower than when the fixed targets were introduced, the rule allows a larger deficit. However, if debt moves higher due to other factors (eg, COP depreciation), the rule will become tighter, demanding a smaller deficit.

The revision of the fiscal targets could mean the noise and volatility persist for now; however, there is significant room for positive surprises as actual data come in. Although the uncertainty generated by the contradictory signals from the authorities could imply higher risks over the sovereign credit rating, Colombia's risk premium already largely exceeds rating peers; therefore, the impact of potential rating actions should be low.

From a rating perspective, the main short-term risk could come from Moody's, which could put its Baa2 rating on negative outlook; however, given that Fitch and S&P already have it in high yield, the potential impact should be limited. A negative outlook from Fitch and a downgrade from S&P, which is already on negative outlook, cannot be ruled out but they might still wait. In the case of S&P, the rationale for the negative outlook involved concerns about investment and growth, not necessarily the fiscal position. The outperformance of the fiscal targets over the past two years still places Colombia's debt level significantly below what was projected when the sovereign fell to high yield, which should provide a bufer against the uncertainty in the interim.

Despite fiscal noise, the economic activity outlook is improving. We think there is room to outperform the consensus 2024 GDP growth forecast of 1.3% for 2024. Carryover after the Q1 24 GDP print already places annual growth above the consensus forecast. Although y/y growth in the quarter (0.9%) was still weak, it was stronger than expected at the end of last year, when we and consensus were estimating the economy could contract in the quarter. Moreover, sequential growth was still very strong (1.1% q/q). Considering the fiscal stimuli and the monetary easing, the cyclical recovery should be sustained, and growth could even exceed our 2024 forecast of 1.8% and converge close to its 3.0% long-term trend in 2025.

Amid a still noisy environment and with fewer concerns around economic activity, Banrep does not seem to have to rush to accelerate easing. The bank’s GDP forecast for Q1 called for growth of 0.3%; therefore, it was surprised to the upside by the actual figure. Authorities recognized that the balance of risks to their current 1.4% GDP growth forecast for this year is tilted to the upside after having already revised it up from 0.8% earlier this year. This seems to reduce the pressures for larger rate cuts for now. On the contrary, it could be a signal of alert, as faster growth could lead to a reopening of the output gap that would make it more challenging return inflation to the target.

We believe Banrep is likely to maintain its cautious approach of cuts of 50bp per meeting and delay an acceleration of the pace of easing to 75bp at least until September. As inflation

expectations become more aligned with a return to the target range (3.0% +/-1pp), and if the Fed’s starts to cut in September, Banrep could feel more comfortable to move from cuts of 50bp to cuts of 75bp, bringing the policy rate to 8.5% by the end of the year. On the other hand, this more-gradual approach could help to anchor longer-term inflation expectations, potentially opening space to bring the policy rate down to 6.0% in 2025.

As President Petro enters into the third year of his term, attention is likely to shift toward succession. His confrontational approach and direct involvement in the campaign could generate noise, but he is likely to be increasingly seen as a lame duck. So far, out of his three main landmark reforms, only the pension reform has been passed, and it was watered down, and procedural issues mean could be overturned by the constitutional court.<sup>55</sup>  Potential candidates are already starting to be discussed, and most seem more moderate than Petro. Therefore, if the fundamental position holds over the coming months, a potential return to Colombia's conservative roots could be on the horizon, which could become the decisive catalyst that has been missing for an improvement in risk perception.

## Ecuador: Keeping the momentum

Alejandro Arreaza, BCI, US | Sebastian Vargas, BCI, US

The achievement of an IMF program has significantly reduced short-term risks, facilitating the management of funding needs. Now the government faces the challenge of sustaining momentum. Authorities are willing to advance with the reduction of the fuel subsidy, which could be supportive. As the country enters the electoral cycle, political noise could pick up, but possible liability management transactions could help to balance those risks, while the base scenario is a re-election of President Noboa in next year's elections, which could be a strong positive catalyst.

• Credit: Remain Overweight. We think the downside risks have declined, given hardcurrency liquidity, multilateral funding, and President Noboa’s high popularity. In addition, we think the debt-for-nature-swap could support market valuations and technicals, particularly given that Ecuador’s positioning is a weakness to the credit.

## Rising to the challenge

The achievement of an agreement with the IMF for an Extended Fund Facility (EFF) alleviates the liquidity situation and gives additional room for the administration to maneuver, while adjustment measures address the structural imbalances of the economy. The authorities are trying to maintain this positive momentum and appear willing to move forward with the reduction of the fuel subsidy, which could be supportive in the short term. In the longer term, the outlook is still highly contingent on a fragile and volatile political situation. Nonetheless, we see elections scheduled for February 2025, as a potential positive catalyst. The uncertainty in the interim could be at least partly mitigated by possible liability management transactions.

The IMF board approved an EFF program for Ecuador on May 31, which provides liquidity relief for the government. This is set to be followed by a disbursement of USD1bn. In terms of financing, the country is supposed to receive more than USD12bn in multilateral funding over the next four years (of which USD4bn from the IMF). In 2024, total disbursements amount to USD4.8bn, exceeding our expectations (USD4bn). The multilateral lending has already resulted in a recovery in international reserves to USD6.0bn. This financing should help the government prevent further accumulation of arrears and, together with the domestic financing, provides a path to muddle through with obligations, at least in 2024-25. The authorities retain the option to apply for a Resilience and Sustainability Trust (RST), through which the government could obtain c.USD1bn in additional funding.

The execution risks are considerable, but the government is starting the program on a strong footing. The program includes an ambitious fiscal consolidation plan amounting to 5.5% of GDP in the non-oil primary balance, including subsidies, over the next four years (2024-28), moving the public sector into an overall surplus of 0.8% of GDP by 2028. However, unlike previous programs that were concentrated on the expenditure side, in this case two-thirds of the adjustment comes from the generation of new revenue, which could make the adjustment more dificult to revert, supporting its credibility. Moreover, actions taken prior to the signing of the agreement on the tax front already amount to c.1.8% of GDP, which, combined with the fuel subsidy reduction, could deliver nearly half of the planned adjustment. This puts the country on track to achieve the targets for the first review, due in August, and should pave the way for the disbursement of an additional USD500mn before year-end.

Despite the challenges, the authorities are sticking to the program and plan to advance with the adjustment of gasoline prices in the coming weeks.<sup>56</sup> There were concerns that the government would delay this measure until after next year's election, given the political sensitivities, so an early announcement should be a positive news that reafirms the commitment to the adjustment process.

The total fuel subsidy bill is a heavy burden for the government that amounts to c.USD3bn, and it creates arbitrage opportunities that foster smuggling and demand for imported refined production, which is also creating pressures in the trade balance. The gasoline subsidy adjustment represents just a fraction of this, but it could still generate net revenue of c.USD300mn in 2024 and USD600mn annually thereafter (c.0.5% of GDP), which takes into account government plans to set a transfer mechanism to mitigate the efect in sensitive and vulnerable sectors.

The potential economic gains would have to be balanced with the political implications of the measures. Protests and/or an erosion in the government's political capital are risks that cannot be totally dismissed, but we consider them relatively contained. Much depends on the strategy used by the administration and its communication. The authorities have been evaluating the alternative of a one-of increase, which could increase the risk of protests, versus gradual monthly increases, which could be easier to digest but likely would need to be completed before the end of this year to dilute the political efect ahead of the presidential elections scheduled for February 2025. Additionally, the indigenous movement that led protests in the past has been weakened over the past two years.

Despite the myriad challenges the country faces (electricity blackouts, closing of the ITT oil field, weak economic activity), President Noboa's popular support remains relatively high, hovering around 60%, which for now appears to be keeping political risks contained. The boost he received after the escalation of the security crisis has been wound down. This seems likely to be a normalization to the pre-crisis level, rather than a full reversal in sentiment as his popularity has stabilized in recent weeks. Yet, there is a risk that his support declines further due to the headwinds the administration is facing at a time when it is more pressured to deliver results. Noboa's capacity to do so on the economic front seems limited, given constrained resources. As such, preserving the administration's political capital likely hinges on any progress it makes on the security front, which remains the top priority in public opinion polls,<sup>57</sup> and its capacity to capture the anti-correista vote, which represents about a third of the electorate.

The election remains a potential positive catalyst in the medium term, as polls currently point to a Noboa re-election. This would likely be viewed as supportive of the reform agenda and should lower the risk regarding the government's willingness to pay, which could move the country closer to market access. Although the political situation is fragile and volatile, some of the risks are counterbalanced by a fragmented  opposition, which supports Noboa's re-election aspirations.

Polls currently put Noboa at the cusp of a first-round victory. Candidacies are still being defined and a lot will depend on the profile of the candidates, so the current figures could be subject to meaningful changes once these are defined. Nonetheless, the correismo political movement, which remains Noboa's main challenger, is facing a big dilemma between loyalty and electability that is creating internal divisions. Carlos Rabascall, who was the correista vice presidential candidate in 2021, is preparing to run independently. He could receive enough support to push correismo below the one-third of the vote it has obtained in previous firstround elections. According to the electoral rules, if a candidate receives at least 40% of the vote and is 10pp ahead of the second place candidate, he or she would win in the first round. This

gives Noboa an opportunity to avoid a run-of, preventing a more uncertain scenario, as run-of elections tend to be more polarized and the margins between candidates tend to be small.

In the center-right, Jan Topic, who had a security-focused platform that could have attracted part of Noboa's potential electorate, has dropped out the race. He was slated to be the Social Christian Party (PSC) candidate, and the party now has no clear alternative candidate to replace him. It cannot be ruled out that they end up endorsing Noboa, which would also help the president avoid the dispersion of his potential electorate.

In absence of big mistakes, Noboa's level of support could be more or less sustained, in which case his ADN party could significantly increase its number of seats in the National Assembly. Although polling points to the legislature remaining fragmented, the more seats ADN wins, the closer it will be to forming a new majority coalition, which could facilitate the approval of reforms after the elections. In the previous elections, ADN won 14 of 137 seats, with Noboa obtaining just 23% of the vote. If he is able to get something closer to what polls are currently suggesting, his party could more than triple its number of seats, to around 50.

The authorities are looking to leverage the Amazon rainforest to use a debt-for-nature swap similar to the one conducted last year with the Galapagos,<sup>58</sup> which could help to reduce the debt burden and improve the debt profile. Multilateral organizations are willing to cooperate with the provision of guarantees that are necessary to structure the transaction. Therefore, we think these transactions could help to contain downside risks due to political uncertainty in the short term, while preserving the optionality the election presents.

A potential reduction of the debt burden could help to contain downside risks, even in the negative scenario of the country failing in its macroeconomic adjustment and having to go through a new debt restructuring process. Ecuador's debt levels are moderate and coupons are low; therefore, the most the government could negotiate with creditors is an extension of maturities, which in principle suggests relatively high recovery values. The main risk remains political uncertainty and its implications for the government's willingness to pay.

## Mexico: Ride the lightning

## Gabriel Casillas,  BCI, US | Sebastian Vargas, BCI, US| Erick Martinez, BCI, US

Mexican assets are facing a highly volatile environment triggered by President López-Obrador's push for several market-unfriendly reforms, particularly the one to overhaul the judiciary system, after the unexpected landslide election. It is our take that volatility will last all summer and the new administration will implement a more market-friendly agenda, right after President-Elect Sheinbaum's inauguration on October 1. We believe "the devil will not be in the details" with respect to the judiciary reform, but the implementation make it more palatable for market participants. In the meantime, we do not expect Banxico to be able to resume its easing cycle this year.

• Credit: Remain Underweight. The Mexico elections injected volatility, and the lingering potential reforms pushed by President AMLO will keep investors on their toes, but we think that credit rating downgrades would be contingent on fiscal actions rather than institutional aspects associated with the reforms. The US elections could bring immigration and tarifs back to the fore, potentially adding spread volatility. In terms of upside risks, under current valuations, we think that president-elect Claudia Sheinbaum does not present a risk to Mexico's fiscal trajectory, and timely actions to put fiscal accounts on a positive track represent an upside risk to our view. That said, we think markets will undergo more spread volatility before Sheinbaum’s team is fully in charge and a fiscal plan is put in motion.

• FX: The MXN has been under pressure since the elections, as the former darling carry trade of EM gets unwound and markets incorporate the premium associated with AMLO's reforms. We think the MXN move has been exacerbated by positioning, which implies an overshoot along the way. The peso may have to see a bit more weakness before the FX Commission steps in, in our view, but we note that the speed of the move should matter; and this FX move has been significant and fast. We think the MXN could remain under pressure in Q3, as the US elections and the discussion of AMLO’s reforms could generate additional noise in Mexican local assets, even if we think that the worst-case scenario for the reforms could be priced already. We expect the peso to gradually recover in H1 25, but settling at a weaker level than before, as the market needs to incorporate more of a risk premium under the new regime.

• Rates: We see room for a stabilization in rates following the election overshoot. While it is true that FX pressures will force Banxico to stay on hold in June, the possibility of an emergency hike to stabilize the FX seems unlikely for now. Moreover, rate diferentials versus the US have widened significantly, and the market is pricing a terminal rate close to 9.7%. This is way above Banxico's estimates of neutral (1.8% to 3.4% real, to which we would add 3.5% expected inflation) despite limited evidence of FX pass-through to inflation. The fiscal efect of AMLO’s reforms in their current state is a question mark, but Finance Minister Ramírez de la O has doubled down in his commitment to reduce the fiscal deficit from \~6% of GDP in 2024 to about half that in 2025, and rating agencies have confirmed that institutional deterioration will be a slow burn. We think the belly of the curve could recover, as it should benefit from a downward repricing of Banxico’s rate path, while higher term premia associated with fiscal uncertainty and weaker institutions should be more evident in the back end of the curve. As such, we now recommend receiving 5y TIIE vs paying 5y SOFR (entry: 580bp, target: 540bp, stop-loss: 605bp, 1m carry-roll: -0.6bp). We expect the ASW to remain wide for the time being, as foreign investors are unlikely to rush into the local market until there is more clarity regarding the reforms.

## Yearning for transition

The ruling party, Morena, achieved a landslide victory in the general elections in June 2, including two-thirds constitutional majorities in both chambers (Mexico: Unexpected landslide victory for Morena). This renewed the president’s push behind several reforms he submitted to congress back in February (Mexico: AMLO's 20 initiatives - political rhetoric, not a market trigger). This is because AMLO has the full month of September to win approval of the reforms he wishes,from newly elected legislators taking up ofice on September 1, until President-elect Sheinbaum's inauguration on October 1.

AMLO's reform package includes judiciary reform, in which AMLO wants to replace the current protocol of Senate approval with one where justices will be publicly elected, potentially undermining the division of powers in Mexico by tying the decision-making process to politics, which could favor the government in power. We believe this reform could potentially trigger negative rating actions as it could weaken the country's institutional framework, meaning private-sector firms could no longer turn to an independent judiciary to contest potential actions by future government administrations when firms believe they are acting within the legal framework (Mexico: Doubling down on the judiciary reform).

While high real rates as well as Sheinbaum's announcement that the current Finance Minister, Rogelio Ramírez de la O, and his team will stay in her administration have played a role in preventing the USD/MXN reaching significantly higher levels, AMLO’s persistent support for this reform is keeping USD/MXN highly volatile. This prompted us to change our monetary policy call to no rate cuts for the rest of the year (Mexico: AMLO supports judiciary reform proposals- We no longer expect rate cuts this year). Banxico might be able to resume its easing cycle in 2025 (Figure 10). However, the current volatile environment could persuade the FX Commission, formed by the central bank and the Ministry of Finance to intervene in the FX market (FX Views: MXN: Thinking about FX intervention), in case of disorderly currency swings, as the exchange rate is the most important channel of transmission of monetary policy in Mexico.

Market participants trust that President-elect Sheinbaum will be fiscally responsible, and respectful of central-bank autonomy. Moreover, market participants envision Sheinbaum's administration as more open to private-sector investment, particularly in energy, which we think is an important step if she really wants to pursue an energy transition. Sheinbaum's decision-making process is also perceived to be based upon cost-benefit analyses, rather than on occurrences. Another important aspect is Sheinbaum's quite diferent approach to security vis-à-vis AMLO. As Mayor of Mexico City she did not implement the "hugs, not bullets" soft approach to organized crime. She was a hardliner, fighting cartels hand in hand with her Secretary of Security, Omar García-Harfuch.

## There should be some FX passthrough after four months of volatility

Inflation has declined to 4.7% in May, from 8.7% in September 2022, while core has also downtrended significantly to 4.2% in May, from 8.5% in November 2022. Some pandemic-era and Russia invasion-related shocks have already dissipated, and Banxico's timely and decisive rate hikes since 2021 allowed inflation to slow down considerably. In our view, the observed disinflation and the benign inflation expectations outlook could have allowed Banxico to cut rates several times this year. However, board members have decided to act quite conservatively and keep quite high real rates, currently around 7.0% (ex-ante).

This is turning out to be quite convenient particularly with the highly volatile currency, given the political noise the country is experiencing at the moment, and a more-hawkish stance from the Fed. In this context, we keep our YE24 12-month inflation forecasts at 4.1% for headline, and 3.7% for core. However, we expect higher USD/MXN volatility in the next four months (until Sheinbaum's inauguration) to have a passthrough of around 30bp to headline inflation in 2025, which we now expect at 3.8%, from 3.5% previously (Figure 11), and of 20bp to core inflation, that we now also anticipate at 3.8%, from 3.6% before (Figure 12).

## Market concerns lie on institutional degradation, not on fiscal fears

Fiscal cost is not a major source of concern about AMLO's reform proposals. Concerns lie on the institutional aspect of the proposed reform. Certain reforms do embody a certain fiscal cost, potentially reaching 0.8pp of GDP, while others, such as the reduction in the number of legislators, the shutdown of certain autonomous institutions, and the significant spending cut proposed for the National Electoral Institute (INE) could ofset the costs, yielding a zero fiscal cost for the set of reforms. As a result, our concerns lie on the institutional aspect of the proposed reform.

Market participants trust that President-elect Sheinbaum will be fiscally responsible, and respectful of central-bank autonomy. Moreover, keeping Ramírez de la O as Finance Minister makes the fiscal adjustment proposed for 2025 more credible (Mexico: FY25 preliminary budget guidelines: higher yields kick in; remain Underweight). In our view, reducing the deficit from the estimate for year-end 2024 at 5.9% of GDP to 3.0% in FY25 is feasible. There are several one-ofs in 2024 such as AMLO's infrastructure projects (1.0% pt), as well as the Pemex' budget line (0.8% pt), and certain non-recurrent expenses — such as the cost of the presidential elections and the economic census (0.5% pt)— that the consolidation eforts end up being around 0.3% pt of GDP, which is somewhat easy to implement in a first year of government that government spending slows down (Figure 13).

Keeping a deficit at 3.0% in the current growth conditions could leave the debt-to-GDP ratio around 50% (Figure 14). Recall that the budget negotiations are to take place by mid-November, instead of September as is usual, because of elections. It is our take that discussions will be around keeping (or not) the Pemex' budget line. Even though, theoretically, Pemex does not need additional money to pay for its bond maturities in 2024 and 2025 given the capital the government injected, downstream operations have made Pemex burn cash at a faster pace than before (LatAm Corporate Credit: LatAm Oil & Gas: PEMEX/CFE: Tough love for Mexico). As a result, the government might want to keep a budget line for the state-owned oil company, while it announces an holistic plan to tackle Pemex' structural issues. This is why we expect a deficit of 4.0% for YE25 and a debt-to-GDP ratio of 51%.

## Economic activity started to cool down earlier than expected

GDP posted quarterly growth of 0.3% in Q1 24, after no change in Q4 23. Growth in the first quarter was mainly boosted by the reversal of two important one-ofs that afected economic activity in the last quarter of 2023. On the one hand, there was automotive production, which declined more than 40% in real terms in November and December. Although several companies in this sector change their models and stop production in these months, on this occasion almost all of them did it in unison — something very unusual. However, this behavior could be explained by last year's strikes in this sector in the United States, as well as the transformation toward the production of electric vehicles. In the end, a positive aspect is that this is temporary and although it has taken longer to recover than we had expected, the automotive sector has been growing in the first three months of the year (+40% between January and March). On the other hand, El Niño afected agricultural production in Q4 23 and January 2024.

The very few data releases that we have for Q2 24 point out towards a not-so-strong quarter. The acceleration of government spending to finish President López-Obrador's "key" infrastructure projects (eg, "Dos Bocas" refinery, the Mayan train), as well as providing an advance payment of social transfers to beneficiaries in April, as the law states that a government cannot transfer handouts two months previous to the election, along with the normalization of automotive and agricultural production, should have allowed for a strong Q2 24. Nevertheless, industrial production contracted 0.5% m/m in April. Even with the seasonal adjustment that accounts for the efects of the Easter holiday, all components declined except construction, which posted a monthly increase of 1.8%.

IMEF manufacturing PMI in April provided a lead to what we could see in hard data later on as it declined to contractionary territory below 50 for the first time since May 2023. Moreover, the indicator in May came in below 50 as well. Another abnormal signal that we detected in the economy was a non-seasonal fall in formal employment in the months of March and May, despite the quite low unemployment rate. Not quite significant, but these months usually observe positive growth. As a result, we revise our GDP forecast for 2024 to 2.4%, from 2.6% previously, while we maintain our 2025 GDP projected growth at 1.6% (Figure 16).

Looking forward, we continue to believe there are three drivers of growth for the remainder of the year: (1) Greater dynamism in the two sectors that have sufered temporary shocks: agriculture and automotive; (2) Strong nonresidential construction investment as development of industrial parks continue, which has been one of the ways in which nearshoring has boosted the Mexican economy; and (3) Growth of the United States' economy, with a boost more from the manufacturing side – in line with the rebound in manufacturing globally – and less from the channels of remittances and tourism, where the efects of restrictive monetary policy in the US are beginning to be seen, particularly on remittances.

## Peru: The economy is no longer immune

## Alejandro Arreaza, BCI, US | Sebastian Vargas, BCI, US | Ivan Stambulsky, Barclays, UK

Fiscal deterioration in Peru is accelerating as tax collection remains  weak and pressures are beginning on the  expenditure side. Although the deficit could still be relatively moderate, the consistent underperformance of the fiscal targets is undermining credibility and increasing pressure on the credit rating. While political risks seem set to pick up, the BCRP continues with its easing cycle but with a more cautious approach.

• Credit: Remain Market Weight. In the near term, technicals outweigh relatively tight valuations and fundamentals, in our view.

• FX: The PEN has not been hit as hard as peers in the region as a lower-yielding and lower-vol currency. This should continue to support the PEN in relative terms, as the global environment remains unfavorable for carry. In addition, BCRP's active policy in the FX market is particularly binding in moments of global turbulence, and monetary policy will likely remain influenced by a strong preference for FX stability. Finally, the PEN has not reacted much to higher copper prices, adding a bufer in case terms of trade deteriorate at the margin. The political outlook remains fragile, with Boluarte at historically low popularity levels.<sup>59</sup> However, we do not see this as a likely trigger for FX moves yet.

• Rates: The BCRP held rates unchanged at its last meeting, even when inflation is at target and real rates remain high. This shows how much of a priority FX stability is for the board, and makes us believe there is not much support left for the Soberanos curve stemming from lower policy rates. In this environment, we expect US rates to remain the main driver of Soberanos, with the next leg lower conditional on more clarity around the timing and depth of Fed cuts.

## Governability problems turn into fiscal pressures

The myth of the Peruvian economy being immune to persistent political instability is vanishing. The efects started to permeate to the fiscal accounts last year through weaker economic activity that afected tax collection, which continues this year. However, the path of deterioration seems to be accelerating as the complex governability situation is pushing the authorities to relax constraints on the expenditure side and adopt fiscally costly measures.

Considering current trends, we revise our already above consensus 2024 fiscal deficit forecast of 3.0% of GDP to 3.7%; however, risks are tilted to the upside. Instead of a recovery of tax collection YTD, data have continued to show a decline, while expenditures are registering a strong increase. Public investment in particular is up 38.3% y/y. The 12mma deficit is moving towards levels not seen since 2021 during the pandemic.

Measures being adopted could add further fiscal pressures. Congress has passed legislation with tax incentives such as cutting the VAT for specific sectors to provide stimuli. Meanwhile, other bills, such as the minimum pension, could add pressures on the expenditure side, especially in the context of massive recent withdrawals from the pension system that could force the government to provide large subsidies in the future. The national oil company Petroperu is also under financial stress, and the government is likely to rescue it in our view. A recapitalization of the company could widen the fiscal deficit in about 1% of GDP; alternatively, if the sovereign provides guarantees for the company to look for financing, it would generate a contingent liability for which the sovereign would have to be responsible in the future.

Although our revised deficit forecast is still relatively moderate, the consistent underperformance of the fiscal targets is undermining credibility and increasing pressure on the credit rating.  After having missed the fiscal rule target last year, these figures are putting the fiscal accounts on a trajectory that, rather than converging to the 2.0% of GDP deficit target set for 2024, is moving it farther away. Returning to the path set in the fiscal rule would require the adoption of adjustment measures that the current administration does not seem to have the political capital to implement. Therefore, a reform of the fiscal rule to relax the constraints might end up being hard to avoid, or the government will have to keep asking for waivers from Congress.

The sovereign credit rating seems be set to keep moving downward. S&P already downgraded it to BBB- earlier this year. Fitch (currently BBB) and Moody’s (currently Baa1) are on negative outlook and in the current conditions seem likely to move in the same direction. Their outlook after a potential downgrade will be key. Similar to S&P, they could stabilize it, which could contain the risk of falling to high yield in the near future. However, in the absence of corrective measures, Peru’s investment grade status could be under scrutiny in the coming years. Relative to rating peers, Peru has a clear disadvantage from the institutional point of view; therefore, it does not require a large deterioration in the economic fundamentals to add further pressures to the rating in our view.

The fiscal stimuli coupled with the announced withdrawal from the pension funds are likely to provide support to domestic demand. Therefore, we are increasing our GDP growth forecast for 2024 to 1.9% from 1.5%, previously. However, we remain below the market consensus of 2.3% as weak business confidence is likely to cap the recovery of private investment. Base efects due to the shocks that the economy faced last year should be making it easier to rebound this year, but the economy grew a modest 1.4% in Q1.

As we move into 2025 we expect political risk to increase. President Boluarte is in a very precarious equilibrium. Her approval rating has sunk to a new low of just 5%. Three impeachment motions have been already promoted this year against her, while she continues to be under investigation by the prosecutor. It seems very challenging for her to stay in ofice until the end of the presidential term in 2026. As she enters into the last year of the term, it will be increasingly likely that she loses the support of the parties that have been supporting her in Congress. Their main incentive to support her has been avoiding an early election, but in the last year of the term, according to the constitution, if they remove her from ofice, they could just appoint another legislator to replace her.

For now the main anchor remains the central bank. The BCRP continues the easing cycle with a cautious approach. Despite inflation having declined faster than expected, already reaching the 2% target, the bank paused this month, keeping the policy rate at 5.75%. This does not mean the end of the cycle, but it does show the limited space the bank has considering that its rate is reaching development market levels. Nonetheless, assuming a likely beginning of easing by the Fed in September, we expect the bank to make three more cuts before year-end to reach 5.0% and continue towards 4.0% next year. Low rates risk is contributing to capital outflows against a backdrop of political instability and weakening economic fundamentals; however, ample reserves provide room for the BCRP to smooth those potential pressures.
